<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 1: "WISH YOU WERE HERE"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>BOSTON.  August 11, 2009</p>
<p>Four hours. That's what I've got left.</p>
<p>Four hours before WBCN becomes just another frequency in the graveyard of American radio. Four hours before the suits flip the switch and turn us into automated Christian contemporary—because apparently Jesus rocks harder than Freddie Mercury ever did. Four hours before forty-one years of breaking bands, of late-night conversations with musicians who poured their souls into three-minute songs, of watching Boston transform from a city of steel and sweat into whatever the hell it's become now—four hours before all of that gets erased like a bad demo tape.</p>
<p>I'm Maxanne Sartori. Sixty-three years old, sitting in Studio B with a bourbon that costs more than most people's car payments, watching the ON AIR sign blink red like a dying heartbeat. Can you believe it? The station called and brought me back. This one last time, and I'm in the same studio where I watched Steven Tyler hear his own voice on the radio for the first time back in '73—the look on his face when he realized that raw, untamed sound was actually going out to thousands of people, that it was real, that it mattered. The same turntables that spun the first singles from bands who went on to fill stadiums. The same microphone that's carried my voice through the 70's, the years of chaos, rebellion, and music that made parents wary of what was coming.</p>
<p>"Maxanne?" The program director's voice crackles through the intercom like a bad phone connection. The Kid's twenty-eight, wears sneakers that cost more than my first apartment—a studio in the Back Bay I rented with my first WBCN paycheck—and thinks rock history started with Nirvana. "You've got four hours left. Make them count."</p>
<p>Make them count.</p>
<p>Make them count. As if four hours could contain what WBCN meant to a city that lived and breathed rock and roll. As if I could summarize forty-one years of rock radio in some tidy narrative arc with a beginning, middle, and end. As if the story I'm about to tell could be wrapped up with a happy ending and a lesson learned. It doesn't work that way, I'm afraid.</p>
<p>But here's the thing about endings—they make liars of us all. You promise to go out with dignity, say something profound, make those final moments matter. And then you find yourself sitting in a radio booth with a paper cup of Heaven Hill and a note Steven Tyler had sent around the time he heard the station was closing. *For the first lady who ever gave a damn*, the note said. Even after all these years, even after becoming one of the biggest rock stars in the world, he still remembers.</p>
<p>I adjust the headphones—the same Sony MDR-7506s I've worn since 1971, held together now with electrical tape and pure stubbornness. The console lights pulse expectantly. Outside the booth windows, Boston glitters in the August darkness—all glass towers and gentrification where dive bars used to breed bands, where the Rat used to host shows that would make your grandmother blush.</p>
<p>"This is Maxanne Sartori, and for the next four hours, you're listening to the final broadcast of WBCN, 104.1 on your FM dial. The Rock of Boston. The station that launched Aerosmith when they were just kids from New Hampshire with more attitude than money. The station that gave J. Geils his first shot, that broke The Cars when they were still just a garage band, that made Boston believe it could rock as hard as anywhere else."</p>
<p>I take a sip of bourbon—another violation of modern radio protocol, but who's going to fire me? The warmth spreads through my chest. The good kind that makes you tell stories you've been carrying too long.</p>
<p>"But tonight isn't about the bands that made it. Tonight is about the ones that didn't, and there were many. And tonight is about one I could never get out of my head after all these years—it's about two kids from Sudbury who should have changed everything. Tonight is about the night I watched two potential stars die in a high school auditorium, when two seventeen-year-old boys who should have been allies became rivals over a question that seems quaint now: What is authentic rock and roll?"</p>
<p>The silence goes out over the city—dead air on every radio still tuned to us. In the engineering booth, the kid they've assigned to babysit my final show looks up from his phone. Maybe something in my voice warns him this won't be the nostalgia trip he expected.</p>
<p>Spring, 1979. Disco was gasping its last breath. The Bee Gees had spent three years convincing America that falsetto voices and dance floors were the future of music. *Saturday Night Fever* had turned every bar into a discotheque. But authentic rock and roll was fighting back. Fighting back hard. The Clash were about to teach America that punk had something to say too. MTV was three years away from making image matter more than sound.</p>
<p>I spin my chair to face the wall of albums, each one a memory, a story, a moment when music mattered more than metrics. The vinyl spines catch the booth's amber light, each one a ghost. My fingers find the right cart without looking—muscle memory, my hands knowing these grooves better than my own face in the mirror.</p>
<p>"Nick Johnson lived in a house where the mortgage was late and dreams were luxuries you couldn't afford. His father had died in a construction accident two years earlier, crushed beneath a load of steel beams when a crane cable snapped—the kind of industrial tragedy that makes page three of the Metro section and gets forgotten by everyone except the families who eat silence for dinner afterward. His mother worked doubles at Friendly's, came home smelling like fryer oil and other people's happiness, and never once complained about her son spending grocery money on guitar strings."</p>
<p>I can see the kid in the WBCN engineering booth scrolling through his phone now, probably updating his status to "babysitting some old lady's final radio show." He has no idea what a garage means to rock and roll. He grew up with GarageBand, thinking music could be manufactured in a bedroom with the right plugins. He never felt four walls, concrete floor, and kids who had nothing to lose.</p>
<p>"Jackie Marsh lived less than a mile away as the crow flies, but between them stretched Hop Brook—named a century ago for its hops, the old mill long gone to modernization. The brook divided Sudbury like a scar. In winter, kids from both sides skated the same nearby pond trying to emulate Bobby Orr's graceful glides across the ice, but when the ice melted, they returned to their proper territories. Jackie's father was a psychiatrist and a professor at Boston University. He published papers about childhood trauma."</p>
<p>I pause, letting the silence breathe.</p>
<p>"These boys lived in the same town, went to the same school, walked the same streets. But America designs its suburbs to prevent exactly these collisions—invisible lines drawn by property values and school districts."</p>
<p>Through the booth window, I can see the billboard across Boylston Street advertising luxury condos where The Rat used to host bands too raw for radio. Twenty-four hundred square feet starting at 2.2 million—you could buy the entire block of Kenmore Square for that in '77. The Ramones played that club back when Joey was so tall he had to duck under the pipes backstage, and the sound system was one blown fuse away from silence.</p>
<p>"I met them both in the spring of 1979, about two months before the Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School Battle of the Bands. I was there as a judge, WBCN sponsoring the event, back when we could afford to care about local music. Back when Clear Channel hadn't swallowed half the FM dial and algorithms hadn't replaced intuition. We told ourselves we were there to scout talent. That was half the truth."</p>
<p>I reach for the cassette on my desk, feeling the weight of it in my hands. The plastic case is warm from my grip, the label slightly faded, but still cataloged in its perfect place thirty years later. "Demo - Nick/Jackie - un-Authorized - May 1979" written in block letters. My letters. The tape itself has that old-cassette smell—plastic and magnetic oxide and the faint trace of cigarette smoke. I can still remember that day, the early morning of May 16, 1979, just hours after the Battle of the Bands, when they both came to this very studio and proved that harmony was still possible, even after everything that had happened.</p>
<p>"This tape I am holding is the only recording that exists of Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh playing together. The rest of the story? That's what the next four hours are for. So pour yourself something strong, turn off your iPhone, and let me tell you how the Battle of the Bands became a war."</p>
<p>The ON AIR sign pulses. Four hours left to tell the truth.</p>
<p>I crack my knuckles and lean into the microphone one more time.</p>
<p>"But first, we need to talk about a garage on Dutton Road, and why the ghost of a dead construction worker might have been the only adult who understood what was really at stake ... "</p>
<p>I close my eyes and let the memory take me back. March 1979. Mud season—the snow gone gray at the edges of the yards, the brook running high and brown with melt, the maples still bare against a flat tin sky. That's when I first drove out to Sudbury, following rumors about a kid who played guitar like he was trying to resurrect the dead.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 2: "DRAW THE LINE"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>SUDBURY.  March, 1979</p>
<p>Maxanne Sartori drove up to the Johnson house in her powder-blue Karmann Ghia, the car bottoming out twice on potholes the town never fixed on the working-class side of Hop Brook. Sparks kicked off the undercarriage. She didn't care. She'd heard rumors about a kid on Dutton Road — a kid with a voice that could stop you mid-dial-turn, playing in a garage where the sound punched you in the chest before you opened the door.</p>
<p>The Johnson house squatted like it was tired: mustard paint peeling in long strips, gutters sagging, front steps listing to port. The lawn had surrendered to New England months ago, brown grass poking through dirty snow like stubble on a dead man's face. The bones of the house were good — wide clapboard, solid frame, the kind of place built by someone who knew what plumb meant.</p>
<p>From the detached garage came something alive. Distorted power chords hit the cold March air and traveled through the concrete foundation, through the frozen ground, up through the soles of Maxanne's boots and into her chest.</p>
<p>Grace Johnson answered the door in her Friendly's uniform, polyester clinging to her frame, name tag reading GRACE in that cheerful corporate font that lies about everything. She smelled like fryer oil and coffee and the particular exhaustion of a woman working her second double this week.</p>
<p>She studied Maxanne — bell-bottoms, oversized sunglasses, standing on the cracked front step like a visitor from a planet where people bought clothes that didn't come from the Jordan Marsh clearance rack.</p>
<p>"You're that lady from WBCN. The one who broke Aerosmith."</p>
<p>"Guilty as charged."</p>
<p>"Nick's in the garage." Grace paused. Something crossed her face — the memory of hope. "Don't fill his head with dreams we can't afford."</p>
<p>"Mind if I listen?"</p>
<p>Grace held her gaze a beat longer than comfortable. "He's been at it since school let out. Won't stop until dinner."</p>
<p>Maxanne crossed the yard toward the garage. The sound grew with each step — bass thrum in the frozen dirt, a snare crack that echoed off the neighbor's siding. The garage door hung open six inches at the bottom, enough to see slivers: a bare bulb swinging on its cord, the silhouette of a kid bent over a guitar, four concrete walls holding more noise than they were built for.</p>
<p>She ducked under the door.</p>
<p>Inside, the air was different — warmer, thicker, heavy with sawdust and motor oil and the metallic bite of old concrete. Dan Johnson's tools hung on a pegboard along the back wall, arranged by size. Claw hammers, box wrenches, a socket set with an empty slot where the 9/16ths used to live — the one he'd had in his pocket the November morning the cable snapped. Tucked behind a pipe wrench was a flattened guitar pick, tortoiseshell, worn thin. Below it, barely visible behind a rusted coffee can, the spine of a spiral notebook with staff lines printed on the pages.</p>
<p>Nick Johnson stood in the center of the garage, mid-solo, fingers moving on a pawn-shop guitar held together with electrical tape at the input jack and faith everywhere else. Twenty-five dollars at the Goodwill in Framingham. New strings from Store 24 when he could scrape together four bucks from mowing lawns. The frets were worn smooth in the first position from a year of playing the same progressions until his calluses cracked and bled and hardened into something useful.</p>
<p>Three other kids backed him — Lincoln boys, Maxanne would learn later, from the even-smaller town that shared a high school with Sudbury but not much else. They stopped when they saw her. That teenage terror of being caught caring about something. The music died mid-note like a bird dropping out of flight.</p>
<p>"Don't stop on my account," Maxanne said, settling onto a paint can. "Play it like you mean it."</p>
<p>They traded looks — four boys deciding whether to trust a stranger from actual radio. Nick decided for them. He launched back into "Dream On" with a fury that made the wrenches rattle on their hooks.</p>
<p>"Every time that I look in the mirror," he sang, "all these lines on my face getting clearer..."</p>
<p>His voice cracked on the high notes. The garage walls seemed to lean in. On the pegboard, a claw hammer vibrated against its nail, buzzing at a frequency only the concrete could hear.</p>
<p>Nick's eyes drifted to the tools. The music faltered — half a beat, barely noticeable, the way a runner flinches passing the spot where someone fell. Then his fingers found the riff again and drove it home.</p>
<p>When they finished, nobody moved. The last note hung in the air. Dust motes froze mid-float.</p>
<p>"What's your band called?" Maxanne asked.</p>
<p>Nick shrugged. "Don't have a name yet. We're just... playing."</p>
<p>"You got a demo?"</p>
<p>The other boys looked at Nick. He shook his head. "Can't afford studio time."</p>
<p>Maxanne pulled out her card. WBCN, 104.1 FM.</p>
<p>"Come by the station. We'll find some time."</p>
<p>Nick hesitated. His eyes moved between the card and the tools on the wall.</p>
<p>"Why?" he asked. "Why would you help us?"</p>
<p>Maxanne stood, knees protesting. "Because Steven Tyler played his first gig in a garage not much bigger than this one. The only difference between him and you is somebody opened a door."</p>
<p>She paused at the garage door, looking back at four boys holding instruments the way drowning people hold wreckage. She wanted to say more. She didn't.</p>
<p>Walking back to the Karmann Ghia, she heard the music start again behind her. "Sweet Emotion" — that bass line crawling out of the garage and across the frozen yard like something that refused to stay buried. Four boys with nothing to lose. The sound carried across Dutton Road, past the potholed asphalt and the bare trees, toward Hop Brook, where it died against the far bank.</p>
<p>Less than a mile away, on the other side of that brook, a boy sat at a Steinway preparing for a recital.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 3: "KILLER QUEEN"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Jackie Marsh woke at 6:47 AM — three minutes before his alarm, the way he always did. The walls were painted a color his mother called Dove Gray. The bookshelves held alphabetized volumes he'd actually read. His piano sat in the corner like a judge waiting to render verdict.</p>
<p>Everything had its place. The tuxedo hung pressed on the closet door for tonight's recital. Sheet music stacked in performance order on the piano bench — Chopin, Bach, Debussy, Rachmaninoff, and the Mozart that would close the evening. His guitar — the electric one his father pretended not to know about — was stored in its case under his bed, gathering dust.</p>
<p>He sat up and looked at the framed photo on his nightstand: himself at seven, hands positioned on piano keys, his mother standing behind him with the kind of smile that suggested she'd finally gotten something right. The photo had won a prize in some parenting magazine — Classical Excellence: Nurturing Your Child's Musical Gift. Jackie remembered the photographer making him hold the position for twenty minutes while they adjusted the lighting.</p>
<p>Through his bedroom window he could see toward the other side of Hop Brook — not that he was looking that direction. Not that he was thinking about someone who used to be his friend before high school taught them they belonged in different worlds.</p>
<p>Tonight was the recital. Tonight he would sit in front of his parents' friends and his father's colleagues and play Chopin and Bach and Debussy like he'd been trained to do since he was five. Everyone would applaud politely and tell Dr. Marsh that his son was remarkably talented, and Dr. Marsh would accept the compliments like tributes to his excellent parenting.</p>
<p>And tonight, somewhere beneath all this carefully constructed perfection was a kid who still believed Freddie Mercury understood something Mozart never did.</p>
<p>The same week Maxanne Sartori discovered Nick Johnson's garage, Dr. Richard Marsh had called the station personally — a rare gesture, since university professors usually delegated such tasks to their secretaries. He wanted to ensure Maxanne understood just how significant his son's recital would be.</p>
<p>"Jackie trained at the New England Conservatory prep division," he'd said, his voice carrying the careful pronunciation of someone who'd worked hard to lose whatever accent he'd grown up with. "He's being considered for early admission to Juilliard."</p>
<p>Maxanne had wanted to decline. Classical recitals were not her beat. But something in Dr. Marsh's voice — a father's pride laced with something that sounded almost like fear — compelled her to agree. Same desperation she'd heard in Grace Johnson's *don't fill his head with dreams we can't afford*. Different words. Same underlying terror that music might ruin a child's future.</p>
<p>By seven o'clock that evening, the Marsh house stood transformed. Folding chairs in rows. A rented Steinway grand that cost more than Grace Johnson made in a year. Champagne chilling in silver buckets. The audience was prep school parents and university faculty — the kind of people who applauded with their fingertips and discussed summer houses on the Cape between selections.</p>
<p>The Marsh house sat on the good side of Hop Brook like it had been placed there by a decorator: Colonial perfection, black shutters against white clapboard, a lawn that would be green come spring. You could walk from Nick Johnson's garage to this front door in twelve minutes, but you'd have to cross Hop Brook — and that narrow stream might as well have been the Atlantic.</p>
<p>Jackie stood in the upstairs bathroom, adjusting his bow tie for the third time. His hands were steady. His mind was not. In two hours he could be back in this bathroom, everyone gone, his parents satisfied that he'd performed exactly as expected. Or he could do what he'd been thinking about for weeks, and everything would change.</p>
<p>Jackie entered the living room at precisely 7:30 PM in a tuxedo tailored to fit him, seventeen years old but carrying himself like someone who'd been preparing for this moment since birth. He was beautiful in that way that good nutrition and orthodontics and SAT tutors can manufacture. When he sat at the piano, something changed.</p>
<p>He began with Chopin's Nocturne in E-flat major. His fingers found the keys with practiced precision that spoke of thousands of hours, of lessons that started before memory, of parents who considered anything less than excellence a character flaw. The notes flowed like water, technically perfect, each phrase shaped exactly as his teachers had instructed.</p>
<p>Maxanne was watching Jackie's face. Behind the technical perfection he was dying in there.</p>
<p>The audience nodded, already composing the compliments they'd deliver with their champagne. Dr. Marsh sat in the front row, expression carefully neutral, posture radiating satisfaction.</p>
<p>Jackie moved through the program with mechanical excellence. Bach's Goldberg Variations — excerpts only, because even at a private recital you had to consider your audience's attention span. Debussy's "Clair de Lune." Rachmaninoff's Prelude in C-sharp minor.</p>
<p>Each piece was perfect. Each piece was empty.</p>
<p>Between the Rachmaninoff and the final Mozart, Jackie paused. The pause stretched longer than it should have. Dr. Marsh cleared his throat — a warning about proper recital pacing. But Jackie wasn't listening to his father. He was listening to something else.</p>
<p>His fingers found different keys. The opening notes of "Bohemian Rhapsody" emerged from that Steinway like escapees from prison.</p>
<p>The room froze. You could hear pearls clutching themselves.</p>
<p>"Is this the real life?" Jackie sang, his classically trained voice hitting notes that would have made Freddie Mercury proud. "Is this just fantasy?"</p>
<p>His hands moved across the keys with all the technical skill he'd brought to Chopin, but now that precision served something real. His left hand found a bass line that rumbled through the Steinway's frame and into the hardwood floor. His right hand built harmonies that shouldn't have been possible on a solo piano — Mercury's operatic architecture rendered in ivory and wire and eleven years of lessons finally finding their purpose.</p>
<p>He sang. His voice, trained for Schubert lieder and careful projection, broke open. The champagne glasses on the sideboard vibrated. Dr. Marsh's water glass hummed against its coaster. He played it past its own ending—longer than the record, longer than the room could stand—and somewhere in there Jackie Marsh stopped performing for anyone but himself.</p>
<p>When he finished, no one clapped. No one moved. Two hundred years of social order sat on folding chairs and tried to remember how to breathe.</p>
<p>Dr. Marsh was the first to speak. "That was... unexpected."</p>
<p>"That was rock and roll," Maxanne said, standing up because someone needed to. "That was your son showing you who he really is."</p>
<p>Jackie looked at her then — really looked — and Maxanne saw the same thing she'd seen in Nick Johnson's eyes three days earlier.</p>
<p>"You're Maxanne Sartori," he said. "You play real rock and roll."</p>
<p>Maxanne gave him her card — the same one she'd given Nick. "Come by the station. Bring whatever music lives in you."</p>
<p>Jackie took the card. His hands were still shaking. He looked at it, then at Maxanne, then at his father's face — which had rearranged itself into something Jackie had never seen before. Not anger. Not pride. Something older and more complicated. Dr. Marsh's right hand moved in his lap, pinky and ring finger curling inward once against his thumb — an unconscious reach, the shape the hand had made a thousand times on keys decades ago, years before the Baldwin lid had closed for good.</p>
<p>"Thank you," Jackie said. "For understanding."</p>
<p>The other guests murmured. Mrs. Marsh moved through the room offering champagne, deflecting questions, doing what she'd been trained to do when the social order cracked.</p>
<p>Maxanne walked to her car thinking she'd found two boys worth saving.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 4: "MORE THAN A FEELING"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>SUDBURY.  March, 1979</p>
<p>The bike had belonged to somebody else first, and Patty Lally had never once been ashamed of that.</p>
<p>It came up out of the reclaim corner at the town landfill the winter she was fourteen—the corner past the glass piles and the flattened cardboard, where people left what was still good because pitching it down the trench felt like a sin even to people who'd stopped going to church. A Schwinn, one speed, the blue gone chalky, the chrome freckled orange. A family up on the Northwest side had set it there when they moved their daughter up to a ten-speed with hand brakes and toe clips, the kind of bike that cost what the Lallys spent on heating oil between Thanksgiving and March.</p>
<p>Her father brought it home balanced across his own handlebars. He spent the back end of that winter on it in the shed with a trouble light hung from a nail—new tubes, new tires, the frame sanded and brushed and painted the blue of a robin's egg, the bars soaked in a coffee can of vinegar until the chrome came back bright enough to hold her face. He wired a basket to the front. When he rolled it out to her he didn't say much; her father never said much. "Somebody quit on it too early," was all, and he went in to wash the paint off his hands.</p>
<p>That was about the whole of what Patty believed, handed to her on two wheels: that a thing other people had quit on was not the same as a thing without worth. You only had to be the kind of person who'd stop at the reclaim corner instead of driving past it.</p>
<p>She rode it everywhere. That was the other thing about her—she went. Her mother said she'd been a wanderer since she could walk, and said it the way you'd describe weather, not a flaw. Both her parents had been the same once, kids who'd known these woods before the conservation signs went up, and what they handed down instead of money was a map nobody else carried: every cut-through and deer path and mini-bike rut between the four corners of town, the trails that showed on no plat in the town hall. She knew which stone walls had gaps you could walk a bike through and which you had to lift it over. She knew the town the way you know a face you've looked at all your life.</p>
<p>The Lallys lived on the southeast side, close enough to the Cavicchio farm that when the wind swung south the smell of it got into the wash on the line and the pages of her schoolbooks, and the bus kids learned young to say it to her like an accusation. Pig. As if she kept them herself. She didn't. Her father fixed things—furnaces, mowers, the occasional outboard—for people on every side of Hop Brook, which meant Patty had stood in the kitchens of all four quarters and watched how a Northwest mother's smile worked when a repairman's daughter reached for the wrong fork. She'd stopped minding the smell a long time ago. It was the price of the air on her side of the brook, and she'd decided early she would not apologize for the air.</p>
<p>Monday was the first warm morning of the year, so she took the long way to the high school, because the long way was the better way.</p>
<p>She cut down behind the farm and picked up the trail along Hop Brook. The brook was up with the snowmelt, running fast and brown over the granite ledges, and she stopped on the plank somebody's father had laid across it years ago and listened, the way she always did. In spring you could hear the whole register of it—a bright chatter in the riffles, a lower note in the bends where the current dug under the roots, and beneath all of it the steady hiss where it narrowed between the boulders the glacier had dropped like loose change. By July it would barely whisper; you'd have to crouch at the bank to hear it at all. In March it had everything to say.</p>
<p>The trail ran beside the old walls most of the way—fieldstone hauled by hand two hundred years ago, laid without mortar by farmers who'd come back from the Old North Bridge to find the fields still needed clearing. Half-buried now under oak leaves and moss, frost-heaved a little wider every winter. She'd laid her palm flat on one once in August and felt the cold still down in it, a cold that went all the way back to the ice. The walls had divided the town before the roads did.</p>
<p>Where the trail crossed the landfill road she slowed, the way she always slowed there, out of a kind of loyalty. It was where the bike was born. Saturdays the whole town lined up at the gate—station wagons from the Northwest waxed and quiet, pickups from the Northeast rusted at the wheel wells, the Cavicchio flatbed trailing its pennant of smell—and every one of them backed up to the same trench and pitched in. Glass in one pile, paper in another, the rest into the dark. At the Dump nobody's garbage was better than anyone else's. As far as Patty could tell, it was the only place in Sudbury where that was true.</p>
<p>From the rise past the landfill she could see the rest of it laid out the way she carried it in her head. Northeast, where the houses smelled like coffee by six and motor oil by seven, where a used guitar ran forty dollars at the pawn shop and a working family could afford to lose that bet—there was a boy over there who played in his dead father's garage; the whole town knew that now. Southwest, the professors' streets, the colonials with names instead of numbers, where another boy had been given lessons the way other kids were given vegetables. Two boys she'd watched circle each other since the eighth grade. Northwest, the old money, the dogs trained out of barking. And her own corner at her back, smelling of what it smelled of.</p>
<p>The high school sat where the southeast quarter ran out, brick and glass dropped between Route 117 and the brook's last loop, and it had four doors, one on each face—which Patty had always thought was the truest thing about it. It was the one building the whole town had to come to. Four doors, and the town poured itself through all of them at the same hour every weekday, and then did the strangest thing she knew of: it sorted itself all over again, by something other than the brook.</p>
<p>She coasted in past the loading ramp where the ramp kids sat in a row with their feet hanging, watching everyone arrive like judges who'd already ruled. Past the auto-shop bay, up already, where a Camaro hung on the lift and a boy she'd known since second grade lay under it grease to the elbows, happier than she ever saw him in a classroom. Through the breezeway off the glass corridor where the rats kept to their leather and their own company, cigarettes that weren't allowed and always there, somebody laughing too loud for ten of eight in the morning. And everywhere in between, the regulars—the great middle, books crossed over their chests, heads down, working hard at being nobody in particular, which was its own full-time job.</p>
<p>Patty chained the blue bike to the rack by the gym door—her door, the one nobody had assigned her; she'd just decided on it freshman year—and stood a second in the cold sun, watching the town she knew by heart pretend it was four different towns. In here, for thirty-five hours a week, they got to choose. Out on the brook the geography would be waiting for all of them at three o'clock.</p>
<p>Marie was at the door with two coffees from the machine, the way she always was, and handed one over without being asked.</p>
<p>"You took the long way," Marie said.</p>
<p>"I always take the long way."</p>
<p>"I know." Marie held the door. "That's why I bring two."</p>
<p>They went in.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 5: "SCHOOL'S OUT"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>September 1974.</p>
<p>The Ephraim Curtis Middle School cafeteria smelled like steam-table Salisbury steak and floor wax. Metal lunch trays clattered against the long folding tables, and someone had propped a transistor radio on the windowsill by the milk cooler. The radio was playing "Rock the Boat" by the Hues Corporation, and half the eighth graders were bobbing their heads like pigeons.</p>
<p>Jackie Marsh sat alone at the corner table closest to the emergency exit. He held a carton of milk that had been sitting out long enough to feel like bathwater against his palm. His brown hair was cut short above the ears—his mother's instructions to the barber, not his—and his polo shirt was tucked in, pressed, creased along the sleeves like someone had ironed it that morning. Someone had. His sneakers were white. Not dirty-white, not scuffed-white. White-white. The kind of white that announced to every kid in the cafeteria that this boy had not yet been allowed to discover the outdoors.</p>
<p>But the belt. Brown leather, and in the center, a gold script buckle that read QUEEN. He'd ordered it from the back of Circus magazine with three weeks of allowance money, and he wore it like a coat of arms.</p>
<p>Across the cafeteria, Nick Johnson came through the double doors carrying his tray at an angle that suggested he'd never carried anything carefully in his life. He was skinny and fast-looking, the kind of kid who ran everywhere even when walking would've been fine. His black hair hung past his collar—long enough that Mr. Fontaine had already threatened him with a note home. His t-shirt had a small hole near the hem, and his fingernails were black with grime from helping his dad frame a garage the previous Saturday. Dan Johnson believed a boy who could swing a hammer by twelve could build a house by twenty, and Nick believed everything his dad told him.</p>
<p>Nick's belt buckle read AEROSMITH in chrome gothic letters. He'd won it off Tommy Kerrigan in a bet involving a dirt bike, a drainage ditch, and a spectacular lack of judgment.</p>
<p>The radio switched to "Kung Fu Fighting" by Carl Douglas, and three kids at the center table started doing karate chops at each other, singing along in voices that cracked on every other word.</p>
<p>Nick was heading toward the far wall when the cafeteria windows threw sunlight across his buckle, and the reflection caught Jackie square in the eyes. Jackie flinched, raised his forearm. When he lowered it, Nick was standing right there, tray in hand, staring at Jackie's belt.</p>
<p>"Queen?" Nick said. "For real?"</p>
<p>Jackie straightened up. "What about it?"</p>
<p>Nick set his tray down uninvited, scraped back a chair, and sat. He pointed at Jackie's buckle with a fork that still had Salisbury steak on it. "I mean, they're okay. But Aerosmith would eat them alive."</p>
<p>"Eat them—" Jackie's voice jumped. "Steven Tyler can't even sing a whole song without screaming."</p>
<p>"That's the point! That's what makes it good!"</p>
<p>"That's what makes it loud. There's a difference."</p>
<p>Nick grinned. He had a gap between his front teeth that made him look like he was always about to say something funny. "Okay, okay. You ever heard 'Dream On'?"</p>
<p>"Yes."</p>
<p>"And?"</p>
<p>Jackie paused. He picked at the corner of his milk carton. "The piano part at the beginning is decent."</p>
<p>"Decent?" Nick almost knocked over his own tray. "Decent. That's like saying the Grand Canyon is a pretty nice hole."</p>
<p>Jackie bit the inside of his cheek, trying not to smile. "Fine. It's good. The piano part is good. But have you ever actually listened to 'Seven Seas of Rhye'? The harmonies? Brian May plays guitar like—"</p>
<p>"Like what?"</p>
<p>"Like it matters."</p>
<p>Nick chewed on that for a second. He stuck a forkful of something gray-green into his mouth—the cafeteria's version of peas—and talked through it. "Okay, but Joe Perry plays guitar like he's angry at it. Like the guitar did something to him personally and he's getting revenge."</p>
<p>Jackie laughed. It came out louder than he meant it to, and two girls at the next table looked over. He ducked his head, but he was still smiling. He couldn't help it. He'd been at Ephraim Curtis for nine days and this was the first conversation he'd had that wasn't about where the bathroom was.</p>
<p>Behind them, "Kung Fu Fighting" was still going. Everybody was kung fu fighting. A kid named Dennis Mahoney was standing on his chair doing crane kicks while his friends pounded the table.</p>
<p>Nick jerked his thumb toward the radio. "Can we at least agree that this is garbage?"</p>
<p>"Oh, this is absolute garbage."</p>
<p>"'Everybody was kung fu fighting,'" Nick sang in a high falsetto, adding karate chops for emphasis. "'Those cats were fast as lightning.' What does that even mean? Cats aren't fast. My cat sleeps nineteen hours a day."</p>
<p>"It's not about actual cats—"</p>
<p>"I know it's not about actual cats, I'm saying it's stupid. The whole thing is stupid. You know what's gonna happen? Five years from now, nobody's gonna remember any of this disco stuff. Nobody. It'll just be gone."</p>
<p>"My dad says it's a fad," Jackie offered.</p>
<p>"Your dad's smart. What's he do?"</p>
<p>"Teaches at BU. Psychiatry."</p>
<p>Nick nodded like this confirmed something. "My dad builds stuff. Houses, mostly. He says disco is music for people who don't actually like music."</p>
<p>"That's..." Jackie tilted his head. "That's actually exactly right."</p>
<p>Nick beamed. He stuck out his hand across the table, between the milk cartons and the Salisbury steak wreckage. His fingernails were still black. "I'm Nick. Nick Johnson."</p>
<p>Jackie took the hand. His own fingers were clean, the nails trimmed straight across. "Jackie Marsh. We just moved here from Ithaca."</p>
<p>"Where's that?"</p>
<p>"Upstate New York. Near Cornell."</p>
<p>Nick looked blank.</p>
<p>"It's a college."</p>
<p>"I know what Cornell is," Nick said, though his face suggested otherwise. "I mean, is there anything to do there?"</p>
<p>"Not really."</p>
<p>"So it sucks."</p>
<p>"It was fine, actually—"</p>
<p>"No, I mean moving sucks. New school, nobody knows you. Is it weird? Being the new kid?"</p>
<p>Jackie looked around the cafeteria—the kids doing karate chops, the Salisbury steak congealing on a hundred trays, the fluorescent lights buzzing their flat two-note hum, Dennis Mahoney now being wrestled off his chair by his friends. "It was weird until about five minutes ago."</p>
<p>Nick leaned back in his chair, balancing on two legs the way teachers were always telling him not to. "So here's what I'm thinking. You like Queen. I like Aerosmith. We're both right, obviously, because we both have incredible taste. But we need to figure out who's more right."</p>
<p>"That's not how being right works."</p>
<p>"It is now. Here's what we do. You bring me your best Queen record. I'll bring my best Aerosmith record. We listen. Then we argue about it."</p>
<p>"Where?"</p>
<p>"My house. I've got a turntable in the basement. My dad hooked up these speakers he pulled out of the Wayland Cinema when they tore it down. They'll rattle your teeth."</p>
<p>"My mom has a good stereo, but she doesn't let me touch the volume."</p>
<p>"Then we're definitely going to my house."</p>
<p>Jackie looked at Nick's black fingernails, his too-long hair, his shirt with the hole in it. Everything about this kid was the opposite of the boys from Ithaca, the ones whose mothers organized play dates and whose fathers played golf on Saturdays. Nick looked like the kind of kid Jackie's mother would describe as "a handful."</p>
<p>"Tomorrow?" Jackie said.</p>
<p>"Tomorrow." Nick picked up his tray and stood, bumping the table with his hip so both their milk cartons wobbled. "Bring 'Seven Seas of Rhye.' I'm gonna make you listen to 'Same Old Song and Dance' so loud your fillings vibrate."</p>
<p>"I don't have fillings."</p>
<p>"You will when I'm done with you."</p>
<p>The bell rang. Chairs scraped across linoleum. Dennis Mahoney was being told to sit properly by Mrs. Whitaker, who looked like she'd already used up her entire supply of patience and it wasn't even one o'clock. Nick and Jackie walked out through the double doors together, their buckles catching the last of the September sun through the windows.</p>
<p>Nick was already explaining how Joe Perry tuned his guitar, using both hands to demonstrate something, nearly hitting a kid with a clarinet case. Jackie was nodding, already planning which album to bring.</p>
<p>Outside, the air was warm and smelled like cut grass from the athletic fields. Somewhere a lawn mower droned. A yellow bus idled at the far curb, its diesel exhaust mixing with the September air. They had math next, in different classrooms, but Nick said he'd meet Jackie by the bike rack after school. He said this like it was already decided, like they'd been doing it for years instead of twenty minutes.</p>
<p>Jackie said okay. He was wondering how he'd convince his mother that this kid with the dirty fingernails and the hole in his shirt was someone worth knowing.</p>
<p>He could. He would.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 6: "WITH A LITTLE HELP FROM MY FRIENDS"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>It started with cassette tapes.</p>
<p>Jackie would bring Queen albums recorded from his father's stereo system — bootleg copies of A Night at the Opera and News of the World dubbed onto TDK cassettes with handwritten labels in Jackie's careful block print. Nick would bring Aerosmith tapes borrowed from older kids on Dutton Road, the lyrics to "Dream On" and "Sweet Emotion" copied in ballpoint pen on notebook paper during study hall when he was supposed to be learning algebra.</p>
<p>They'd meet in the school library during lunch, one set of headphones split between them, each kid pressing his ear to the foam pad. Mrs. Stephens would shush them every few minutes, but she never kicked them out.</p>
<p>"Listen to this harmony," Jackie would say, rewinding "Bohemian Rhapsody" for the hundredth time. "Freddie Mercury is doing things with his voice that should not be physically possible."</p>
<p>"Maybe," Nick would counter, "but listen to Tyler's scream right here in 'Dream On.' That's not singing. That's a guy bleeding his entire soul into a microphone."</p>
<p>"That's precisely the difference. Mercury controls it. Tyler just — throws himself off a cliff."</p>
<p>"Yeah," Nick said. "And that's why it's better."</p>
<p>Jackie rewound the tape again. Nick let him.</p>
<p>By spring of 1975 they'd found the conservation land that ran behind both their neighborhoods — hundreds of acres of pine and scrub oak stretching from the wrong side of Hop Brook clear through to the good side, the kind of territory that didn't belong to either world. Nick's bike was a hand-me-down Schwinn salvaged from The Dump and spray-painted black, the chain popping off every quarter mile. Jackie's ten-speed Raleigh was shiny and completely wrong for dirt trails — the thin tires slipped on roots and the derailleur caught on every rock. He wiped out twice the first day and came back the next morning anyway.</p>
<p>The trails were narrow, packed dirt threaded between white pines that dropped needles thick as carpet. Their tires hissed through the soft brown mat and kicked up the smell of warm resin and turned earth. On the steep downhills the bikes rattled so hard their handlebars buzzed and the spokes sang a high, tinny note like a fork struck against a glass. In the deeper woods, where the canopy closed overhead, the light came through green and underwater-quiet. Woodpeckers worked the dead oaks in short machine-gun bursts. Chipmunks bolted across the trail a wheel-length ahead. The air smelled different here — not like either of their neighborhoods, not like cut lawns or car exhaust or the cooking grease that drifted from the Friendly's kitchen. It smelled like cold dirt and bark and the particular sweetness of pine sap bleeding from a fresh cut, and it belonged to nobody.</p>
<p>They found swimming holes that weren't on any map. The first one was a quarry cut half a mile past the power lines, where the bedrock dropped straight into black water. The cold hit like voltage — it locked your chest, squeezed your ribs, turned your fingers white. You'd come up gasping with your jaw clamped shut and the sky looked bluer than it had any right to look and every sound was louder, sharper, the way the world gets when your body remembers it's alive. They'd pull themselves onto the granite ledge afterward and sit with their feet dangling, water running off their shorts, watching the surface go still again while the sun baked their shoulders dry. Dragonflies stitched the air above the water in electric-blue streaks.</p>
<p>Some days they didn't even swim. They'd just ride — miles of trail, the bikes ticking beneath them, the woods opening and closing like rooms in a house with no walls. They built a fort out of fallen branches near the old stone wall that marked the property line between the conservation land and the Wayside Inn meadows. It wasn't much — a lean-to with a pine-bough roof that leaked when it rained — but it was theirs.</p>
<p>The first time Jackie came to dinner at the Johnson house, Grace watched him from the kitchen doorway. She'd heard about the Marsh family from the other mothers — the psychiatrist at Boston University, the house on Maple Street, the kind of people who didn't usually sit at tables with mismatched chairs and bills spread on the counter.</p>
<p>But Jackie took the peanut butter sandwich she offered and said "Thank you, Mrs. Johnson," and ate it like it was the best thing he'd had all week. When he finished, he stood up and carried his plate to the sink without being asked. Grace's hand stopped on the dish towel. She knew that move. Not from Sudbury, not from any family on the good side of Hop Brook. That was how she'd been raised — her mother's kitchen, clearing plates before you were told, because helping was what you did when someone fed you. She hadn't expected to see it in a boy whose father drove a new Buick and taught at a university.</p>
<p>Something in Jackie's careful manners — the way he held the plate with both hands, the way he waited until she took it — tugged at a memory Grace hadn't visited in years. Her own mother's dining room table, the good china reserved for Sunday dinners, the way her mother had stood behind her chair and guided her hands: napkin in the lap, elbows off the table, always say please and thank you. Her mother had grown up in a house with a name too, out in Wellesley, before she'd married a man who worked with his hands and moved to a street without sidewalks. Grace had learned her manners in a kitchen that smelled like lavender and lemon polish, and she'd carried them to a kitchen that smelled like dish soap and meatloaf and mortgage worry. She hadn't expected to recognize those same manners in a boy from Maple Street — but she did, and the recognition sat in her chest like a stone.</p>
<p>"He's a good kid," she told Nick later. "Different. But good."</p>
<p>The next Saturday, Dan Johnson came home early from the job site. He pushed through the garage door still in his work boots, concrete dust on his jeans, a socket wrench in his back pocket he'd forgotten to put away. He found Nick and Jackie sitting on overturned milk crates, guitars across their laps.</p>
<p>"This the friend?" Dan said.</p>
<p>"Jackie Marsh," Jackie said, standing up and extending his hand.</p>
<p>Dan looked at the outstretched hand, then shook it. His grip swallowed Jackie's fingers. "You play?"</p>
<p>"Yes, sir. Guitar and piano."</p>
<p>"Piano." Dan nodded, like this confirmed something. "Play something."</p>
<p>Jackie glanced at Nick. Nick shrugged — go ahead.</p>
<p>Jackie played the opening arpeggio of "Bohemian Rhapsody" fingerpicked on the acoustic, the notes climbing in that strange, sad way, each one placed like he was setting stones in a wall.</p>
<p>Dan listened with his arms crossed, head tilted. When Jackie finished the intro, Dan said, "That's real pretty," and walked into the house. Through the screen door they heard him call out: "Grace, the kid can play."</p>
<p>The test came in October. Doug Malone had been watching Jackie for weeks — a big kid who made up for bad grades with his fists and a talent for finding people alone. He caught Jackie between third and fourth period, near the lockers where the fluorescent lights buzzed and the hallway smelled like floor wax and sweat.</p>
<p>"Hey, rich boy." Doug stepped in close. "You lost? Your people hang out on the other side."</p>
<p>Jackie looked up. His hands were damp but his voice was steady. "I'm not lost."</p>
<p>"Sure looks like it. Professor daddy, house on Maple Street. What are you doing over here?"</p>
<p>Nick appeared beside Jackie. He'd come around the corner at a half-jog and slowed to a walk, hands loose at his sides.</p>
<p>"Problem?" Nick said.</p>
<p>Doug looked at him. Nick was smaller, wiry where Doug was thick, but he stood like a kid who'd been hit before and was already past the part where it scared him.</p>
<p>"Nothing to do with you, Johnson. This is between me and the rich kid."</p>
<p>"Funny," Nick said. "Because that rich kid's with me. So now it's got everything to do with me."</p>
<p>The hallway had gone quiet. Other kids stopped walking and pressed themselves against lockers, watching.</p>
<p>Doug looked from Nick to Jackie and back. He was trying to do the math — working-class kid defending a kid from Maple Street — and the numbers didn't add up.</p>
<p>"Whatever." Doug stepped back. "Not worth it."</p>
<p>He walked away. The hallway stayed quiet for another three seconds, and then everybody moved again, like a clock that had paused and restarted.</p>
<p>Jackie and Nick stood there.</p>
<p>"You didn't have to do that," Jackie said.</p>
<p>"Yeah, I did," Nick said. Then, grinning: "Besides, if Malone punched you, you'd probably critique his form. 'That was a C-minus right hook, Douglas, really substandard wrist alignment.'"</p>
<p>Jackie laughed — a real laugh. "I would not have said that."</p>
<p>"You absolutely would have."</p>
<p>They walked to class together. Neither of them mentioned it again.</p>
<p>That weekend they were back in the garage, Dan's tools hanging in neat rows on the pegboard behind them — wrenches arranged by size, screwdrivers slotted into a wooden rack he'd built himself, the socket set open on the workbench like a surgical kit. The October light came through the single window in a yellow slant and lit up the dust that floated between them.</p>
<p>Nick strummed the opening to "Sweet Emotion" — that low, dirty, swinging riff, all bass string and attitude. The notes bounced off the concrete floor and the cinder-block walls and came back fatter, meaner. He played it again, letting the groove settle, his right hand loose on the downstroke.</p>
<p>"That's half-decent," Jackie said.</p>
<p>"Half-decent. Okay, Liberace. Show me what you got."</p>
<p>Jackie repositioned his fingers and played the piano intro to "Bohemian Rhapsody" transposed for guitar — the descending chords, Bb to Gm, that cathedral-ceiling sound, each chord ringing into the next like bells falling down a staircase. On the acoustic, without distortion, the harmonics bloomed in the cold garage air. It sounded ancient and enormous, the kind of thing monks might play if monks had Gibsons.</p>
<p>"Holy shit," Nick said. "That's operatic."</p>
<p>"Language, Nicholas."</p>
<p>"That's freaking operatic. Better?"</p>
<p>Jackie was smiling. "Marginally."</p>
<p>Nick looked at his guitar. Then at Jackie. Then back at his guitar. "What if we played them at the same time?"</p>
<p>"They're in completely different keys."</p>
<p>"So we change the key."</p>
<p>Jackie thought about it. He shifted the Bohemian Rhapsody progression, found a voicing that sat in the same neighborhood as Nick's riff. "Try it now. Start the riff."</p>
<p>Nick kicked off "Sweet Emotion" again — that swaggering, sleazy groove. Jackie came in over the top with the Bohemian Rhapsody chords, and for a second it was a car crash, two songs fighting each other in a twelve-by-fifteen-foot box. But then Jackie adjusted his timing, laid his chords across the gaps in Nick's riff instead of on top of them, and the sound locked in. The riff gave the chords a pulse. The chords gave the riff a sky. Nick's guitar was the engine; Jackie's was the wingspan. The low notes rumbled through the milk crates and the high notes rang against the hand tools and the whole garage vibrated like the inside of a drum.</p>
<p>They played it through twice, figuring out where to breathe, where to push, where to let the other one lead. By the third pass, Nick started singing — "Sweet emotion" — in his rough, throaty voice, and Jackie answered with "Is this the real life" in a clear tenor that cut through the distortion and the echo and the dust. The two vocal lines braided together and pulled apart and braided again, and the sound was bigger than two thirteen-year-olds on milk crates in a one-car garage in Sudbury, Massachusetts, had any right to make.</p>
<p>When they stopped, the silence was total. A wrench on the pegboard was still humming.</p>
<p>Nick was breathing hard. Jackie's fingers were shaking.</p>
<p>"We should start a band," Nick said.</p>
<p>"What would we call it?"</p>
<p>"Aero Queen."</p>
<p>Jackie laughed. "That's terrible."</p>
<p>"Queen Smith?"</p>
<p>"Worse."</p>
<p>They sat there grinning at each other in the yellow light, two boys from different sides of Hop Brook except this garage, this sound, this one thing they'd built out of a pawn-shop Fender and a birthday Raleigh and two hundred hours of arguing over headphones in a school library.</p>
<p>Outside, the October wind pushed dead leaves across the driveway. Inside, the wrench stopped humming. From the kitchen, through the screen door, they could hear Grace running water and Dan's work boots crossing the floor, and the small ordinary sounds of a house that was still whole.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 7: "BOHEMIAN RHAPSODY"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>The thermostat in the Marsh dining room was set to sixty-eight degrees. Always sixty-eight degrees. Jackie had checked once with a thermometer from his chemistry set, expecting a degree or two of drift, some small proof that nature still had a say in things. Sixty-eight point zero. His father's house did not permit variation.</p>
<p>The breakfast table was set the way it was set every morning — forks one inch from the plate edge, knives turned inward, cloth napkins folded into identical rectangles. Jackie knew the distances because he'd measured them on a dare from Elizabeth. She'd bet him a dollar their mother used a ruler. She was right. Not a ruler exactly — habit so deep it had become precision, years of Eileen Marsh setting this table until her hands could find the spacing the way a pianist finds an octave.</p>
<p>Dr. Richard Marsh sat at the head of the table, reading the Boston Globe. He held the paper with both hands, the way he held everything — deliberately, with the posture of a man who had taught himself to take up exactly the right amount of space. He was forty-five, lean from discipline rather than exercise. Long fingers. A span wider than it needed to be for turning pages.</p>
<p>Jackie sat across from him, thirteen and already trained in the architecture of this room. Under the tablecloth, his fingers were moving — silent scales across an invisible keyboard, the notes playing only inside his head.</p>
<p>"Juilliard's pre-college program wrote back," Dr. Marsh said without looking up. "They want to schedule your audition."</p>
<p>Jackie's fingers stopped. "When?"</p>
<p>"Next month. We'll discuss repertoire this weekend. Chopin needs another month. The Bach is ready."</p>
<p>"I've been working on other things too."</p>
<p>Dr. Marsh turned a page. The sound was precise as a metronome. "Such as?"</p>
<p>Eileen Marsh came in carrying toast cut into triangles — always triangles, because once, years ago, someone had decided that was the shape toast should be in this house, and no one had revisited the decision since. She poured orange juice into crystal glasses and sat down with her napkin across her lap and pearls at her throat at seven-thirty in the morning.</p>
<p>"Queen," Jackie said. "The way Mercury layers the vocals — "</p>
<p>"Jackie." Just his name. But the way his father said it carried the weight of a full sentence: We've discussed this.</p>
<p>"Rock music is a phase," Dr. Marsh said, folding the newspaper and setting it beside his plate, aligned with the table edge. "You have a genuine gift. I won't watch you waste it."</p>
<p>There was a tightness in his jaw. Jackie had seen it before — not anger, but something underneath anger that looked almost like pain. His father's left hand moved to the table, and the long fingers pressed the cloth once, thumb and pinky spanning what would have been an octave, then relaxed.</p>
<p>"Richard," Eileen said. Quiet. A single word that meant: not at breakfast.</p>
<p>Jackie looked at his mother. She was studying her orange juice with the focus of someone who had learned, over many years, exactly when to speak and when to let silence work. Eileen knew things. She knew about the carton in the attic crawl space behind the Christmas decorations — the sheet music boxed up in 1960 and never touched since, the Juilliard competition programs at the bottom with his father's name on them, the one his father never mentioned, the one Jackie had found when he was ten and never asked about because even at ten he understood that some doors in this house stayed closed.</p>
<p>"Your father wants what's best," Eileen said, her voice carrying the particular gentleness of a line she'd delivered so many times it had worn smooth.</p>
<p>From upstairs, a violin cut through the silence. Not careful classical scales — this was different. A melody that started with Vivaldi's precision and bent sideways into something warmer, looser. Elizabeth was up there improvising again, finding the cracks between the notes on the page and living in them.</p>
<p>Dr. Marsh glanced at the ceiling but said nothing. Elizabeth's gift was wilder, less controllable, and because it came so effortlessly he seemed to regard it the way you regard weather. You couldn't schedule weather. So he let it be.</p>
<p>"May I be excused?" Jackie asked.</p>
<p>"You have forty minutes before school."</p>
<p>"I want to practice."</p>
<p>His father nodded. Because practice, in this house, was always the right answer.</p>
<p>The Steinway grand piano filled a third of Jackie's bedroom. His father had bought it when Jackie was seven, the year his conservatory teacher said he had "remarkable hands." It was tuned every three months by a man who drove up from Boston. The piano was worth more than some of the houses on the other side of Hop Brook.</p>
<p>Jackie closed his door. Locked it — the small rebellion of a turned bolt.</p>
<p>He sat at the bench and let his hands rest on the keys without pressing them. He started with Bach. The Prelude in C Major — his father's favorite, the piece Dr. Marsh sometimes paused outside the door to listen to, his shadow visible through the gap at the floor. Jackie played it perfectly because after six years of training, the notes lived in his tendons. The Bach was beautiful the way this house was beautiful. Measured. Controlled. Every note in its place.</p>
<p>Then he stopped.</p>
<p>He listened. His father's car starting in the driveway. The garage door closing. The house settling into the particular silence it held when Dr. Marsh left — a silence that felt like an exhale, like the walls themselves relaxing by half a degree.</p>
<p>Jackie's hands found different keys.</p>
<p>The opening of "Bohemian Rhapsody" — played soft, so soft the notes barely carried past the bedroom walls. The melody ghosted out of the piano like something trapped inside it, waiting for the house to empty. He kept the sustain pedal barely pressed, dampening the resonance, muting the overtones that would carry through the floor.</p>
<p>He played the ballad section with his eyes closed. His left hand found the bass line — those dark, descending chords that Mercury had borrowed from opera and made into something new. His right hand carried the melody, and he let it ache, let it reach for notes that weren't in any of his conservatory books. He wanted to open the lid all the way, wanted to feel the hammers hit the strings with the full weight of what he was feeling, wanted the sound to fill every room until the walls cracked.</p>
<p>But he played quiet. He'd learned to play quiet out of necessity, not choice.</p>
<p>When he reached the operatic section, he sang. Barely above a whisper, Galileo Galileo rising up inside his chest and dying in his throat because volume was the one thing this house could not tolerate. The headboard section — thunderbolts and lightning — he played with his fingers close to the keys, pulling the sound inward, and even restrained it was the most alive thing in the room — more alive than the alphabetized bookshelf, more alive than the framed conservatory certificates on the wall.</p>
<p>He ended soft, the way Mercury ended it — nothing really matters — and held the last chord until the strings stopped vibrating on their own.</p>
<p>A knock on his door. Jackie flinched, pulled his hands off the keys.</p>
<p>"That was really pretty." Elizabeth's voice, muffled through the door.</p>
<p>He unlocked it. She was leaning against the frame, violin case in one hand, wearing a Fleetwood Mac t-shirt their mother pretended not to see.</p>
<p>"You heard that?"</p>
<p>"The whole thing. You know he's gone, right? You could've played it louder."</p>
<p>"I know."</p>
<p>Elizabeth looked at him like she was reading the back of a book he thought was closed. "You practice Queen like you're hiding a body."</p>
<p>"Liz — "</p>
<p>"I'm just saying. You don't have to whisper when nobody's listening." She shifted her violin case. "I figured out how to get that sliding guitar sound on 'Don't Stop Me Now' with vibrato and an open D. Want to try it together after school?"</p>
<p>"Dad gets home at six."</p>
<p>"So we stop at five-thirty."</p>
<p>She said it like it was the simplest thing in the world. Like scheduling your joy around your father's commute was something every kid did.</p>
<p>"Yeah," Jackie said. "After school. Five-thirty."</p>
<p>Elizabeth grinned — too wide, too bright, too much like someone who hadn't yet learned to measure her joy against the expectations of the room. "Cool," she said, and disappeared down the hall.</p>
<p>Jackie closed the door. Put his hands back on the keys and found the opening chord of the Chopin Ballade his father wanted for the audition. His posture straightened. His fingers found their trained positions.</p>
<p>But under the first phrase of the Chopin — just for a measure, just long enough to feel it — his left hand played the bass line from "Bohemian Rhapsody."</p>
<p>Then it was gone. Just the Chopin. Just the practice. Just the sound his father wanted to hear.</p>
<p>That night, the family sat at dinner — same measured forks, same sixty-eight degrees, same crystal throwing chandelier light into scattered prisms on the tablecloth. Eileen had made roast chicken with precisely quartered potatoes. The salt and pepper stood at the exact center of the table, equidistant from all four settings.</p>
<p>"The audition is in six weeks," Dr. Marsh said, cutting his chicken in small, even pieces. "Professor Aldridge at the conservatory will do a mock audition Saturday."</p>
<p>"Okay."</p>
<p>Dr. Marsh set down his knife and fork. He looked at Jackie — really looked — and the mask slipped. What was underneath was not authority or anger but something rawer. Something that looked, in the chandelier light, like grief.</p>
<p>"I had chances," he said. His voice was lower than usual. Eileen stopped chewing. Elizabeth looked up. "When I was your age. Different chances." He paused. "I made the practical choice. That's what you do when you come from where I came from." Another pause. "I want you to have the path I couldn't see. Not just somewhere safe."</p>
<p>The grandfather clock ticked in the hallway. Steady, mechanical, never fast, never slow.</p>
<p>"I hear you, Dad," Jackie said. And he meant it. He loved his father — the hands, the discipline, even the thermostat. He just couldn't live there.</p>
<p>Elizabeth reached under the table and squeezed Jackie's hand. Quick, hard, gone — before anyone could see.</p>
<p>"Eileen, this chicken is excellent," Dr. Marsh said, and picked up his fork, and the conversation was over the way conversations in this house were always over — not with resolution but with redirection.</p>
<p>Eileen smiled her careful smile. "Thank you, Richard."</p>
<p>And dinner continued. Sixty-eight degrees. Forks one inch from the plate. Everything in its place.</p>
<p>Later, Jackie sat in the dark. He did not turn on the piano. He listened to the house — his mother downstairs with the radio tuned to the classical station because that was the station this house played. His father's study, the murmur of pages. Elizabeth's violin through the wall, something slow and sad that might have been Barber or might have been her own invention — with Elizabeth you could never tell.</p>
<p>He thought about Nick Johnson. About that garage on Dutton Road where the concrete floor was cracked and the tools hung on pegboards and nobody measured the distance between anything. Where you could play as loud as you wanted and the only rule was that it was real.</p>
<p>Classical earned his father's approval. Rock earned his breath. He couldn't have both.</p>
<p>Five-thirty, he thought. We stop at five-thirty. And the part of him that was already learning to hide — the part that played Queen at a whisper and loved Freddie Mercury in secret and measured his joy against his father's commute — that part closed its eyes and began to count the hours.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 8: "MAMA KIN"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>The Johnson house on Dutton Road had crown molding in the dining room. Not the cheap pressed-foam kind you bought at the lumber yard — real plaster, hand-carved, with acanthus leaves that curled at the corners where the ceiling met the walls. Grace ran her fingers along the baseboard every time she vacuumed, the way you'd touch a scar without meaning to. Most of the molding was painted over now, three or four coats of landlord white, but if you looked where the living room met the front hall you could still see the original detail underneath — vines and small flowers cut into plaster by someone who'd been paid to make a house beautiful, back when this house was the kind of place where people got paid to do that.</p>
<p>Nick didn't know any of this yet. He was thirteen, and the house was just the house.</p>
<p>March, 1976.</p>
<p>Nick came through the back door at four-fifteen with a brown paper bag from Store 24 and mud on both knees. The bag held a set of guitar strings — Martin Marquis, medium gauge, $3.49 — and a Slim Jim he'd already eaten in the parking lot.</p>
<p>Grace was at the kitchen table, still in her Friendly's uniform, the brown polyester one with the orange piping that made her look like a UPS driver. Her name tag was crooked. She had the bills spread in front of her — electric, mortgage, the Sears card — arranged in a row like a hand of cards she hadn't decided how to play.</p>
<p>"Boots," she said without looking up.</p>
<p>Nick kicked them off at the door. "Hey, Mom."</p>
<p>"How was school?"</p>
<p>"Fine."</p>
<p>"Fine like you went to all your classes, or fine like you went to some of your classes?"</p>
<p>"All of them." He held up the bag. "Got new strings."</p>
<p>Grace glanced at the bag, then at his knees. "You walked to Store 24 in the mud."</p>
<p>"The shortcut's faster."</p>
<p>"The shortcut goes through the Hendersons' yard."</p>
<p>"Mrs. Henderson doesn't care."</p>
<p>"Mrs. Henderson called me last week, Nick." Grace set down her pen. Her eyes were the color of strong tea and they didn't miss much. "Your father's working late. There's leftover chicken in the fridge. Do your homework before you touch that guitar."</p>
<p>"Yes ma'am."</p>
<p>He didn't do his homework first. Grace knew he wouldn't. She listened to him cross the kitchen in his socks, heard the side door open and close, heard his feet on the two concrete steps down to the garage. She picked up her pen and went back to the column of numbers that never came out right.</p>
<p>The garage smelled like sawdust and motor oil and the cold mineral breath of a concrete floor that never fully dried. Dan Johnson's workbench ran the length of the back wall — a slab of three-quarter-inch plywood on two-by-four legs, scarred with saw cuts and drill holes and half-moon burns from coffee mugs set down hot. His tools hung on pegboard above it, arranged by type: wrenches in descending order, screwdrivers slotted into a rack he'd built from a piece of oak scrap, the socket set open in its case like a jaw full of silver teeth. Everything had a place. Everything was where it belonged.</p>
<p>Nick's corner was the opposite. A milk crate for a chair. His guitar — a twenty-five-dollar pawn shop Stratocaster copy held together with electrical tape at the strap button — leaned against the wall next to a shoebox full of picks and a dog-eared copy of a Mel Bay chord book he'd gotten from the Sudbury library and never returned.</p>
<p>He sat on the crate and changed the strings. The old ones came off stiff, the wound wire unraveling where his pick hand had worn through the nickel plating. He threaded the new ones through the bridge, wound them at the tuning pegs, stretched each string by pulling it away from the neck and retuning until it held. The fresh strings were bright under the single overhead bulb — clean steel, tight and sharp against the rosewood fretboard.</p>
<p>He played for an hour. The same riff, over and over. The opening to "Walk This Way" — that nasty, syncopated thing Joe Perry played, all muted scratches and pull-offs, the notes tumbling over each other like they were in a hurry to get somewhere bad. Nick could hear it perfectly in his head. His fingers were slower than the song. He'd get through the first four bars clean and then his ring finger would land a fret too high and the whole thing collapsed into a buzz.</p>
<p>He played it again. And again.</p>
<p>Dan's truck pulled into the driveway at six-forty. Nick heard it before he saw it — the particular rattle of a 1968 Ford F-100 with a loose heat shield and 140,000 miles of opinion about how it should be treated. The engine cut. The truck door opened and closed. Work boots on gravel.</p>
<p>Dan came through the garage door sideways, turning his shoulders to clear the frame. He was still in his work clothes — Carhartt jacket with drywall dust on the shoulders, jeans that had gone soft as chamois from washing, steel-toed Red Wings caked at the soles. His hands were enormous. The pads of his left fingertips were worn flat and glassy — dimes stacked on dimes — in a pattern that didn't come from framing alone, though Nick had never seen his father hold a guitar.</p>
<p>"Hey, bud." Dan set his lunch pail on the workbench. "That 'Walk This Way'?"</p>
<p>"Trying to be."</p>
<p>Dan pulled off his jacket and hung it on the nail by the door. He stood behind Nick and watched his hands. "You're rushing the second bar. Slow down."</p>
<p>"It sounds wrong slow."</p>
<p>"Sounds worse fast if the notes aren't clean." Dan reached past him and pressed Nick's ring finger down flat against the fret wire. His hand was warm and rough and smelled like joint compound. "Here. Not behind the fret — right on top of it. Feel that?"</p>
<p>Nick pressed. The note rang out clean, no buzz.</p>
<p>"Now the pull-off. Don't lift straight up. Pull down toward the floor. You're snapping the string, not just releasing it."</p>
<p>Nick tried. Better. The note had a voice now instead of just a click.</p>
<p>"Again," Dan said.</p>
<p>Nick played the first two bars. Dan's hand stayed near the neck, not touching, just hovering — ready to correct. When Nick hit the transition to the third bar, Dan tapped the body of the guitar twice with his knuckle, setting the tempo the way a drummer would.</p>
<p>"There," Dan said. "Hear that? You had it."</p>
<p>Nick played it again. This time the riff came out whole — rough, a little ragged at the edges, but the bones were right. The notes fell where they were supposed to fall.</p>
<p>Dan squeezed his shoulder once, hard enough to mean something. "Good. Now play it fifty more times."</p>
<p>He picked up his lunch pail and went inside. Through the screen door Nick heard him say "Smells good" to Grace, heard the faucet running, heard the quiet domesticity of a house settling into evening. He played the riff again. And again. The fresh strings bit into his fingertips, and by the twentieth repetition the pain had become a kind of company — proof he was working, proof the music was costing him something, which meant it was worth something.</p>
<p>Dinner was chicken and rice and canned green beans. Grace served it on plates that didn't match — two blue, one white with a chip on the rim that she always took for herself. The kitchen was small, the table pushed against the wall so you had to slide in sideways to reach the far chair. Dan sat with his back to the window. Grace sat closest to the stove. Nick sat between them, still flexing his left hand under the table, the fingertips dented and sore.</p>
<p>"He's getting better," Dan said to Grace, forking rice.</p>
<p>"I can hear." Grace looked at Nick. "You do your homework?"</p>
<p>"Most of it."</p>
<p>"Nick."</p>
<p>"I'll finish after dinner."</p>
<p>Grace held his eyes for a beat, then let it go. She turned to Dan. "The banister's loose again. The one at the top of the stairs."</p>
<p>"I'll look at it Saturday."</p>
<p>"It's been loose since November."</p>
<p>"Then another week won't kill it." Dan said this with half a smile, and Grace shook her head but didn't push. There was a language between them built from years of these small negotiations — what got fixed, what waited, what they pretended not to notice because noticing cost money they didn't have.</p>
<p>Grace stood to clear the plates. As she passed the doorway to the dining room, she stopped. Her hand went to the frame, her thumb finding the groove where the original molding met the casing — a seam you'd only notice if you knew to look for it. She'd traced it as a girl in a house with a name, on her way to a dining room with a mahogany table and eight matching chairs. She traced it now in Dan Johnson's house, where the plaster was his family's and the memory was hers.</p>
<p>"What?" Dan said.</p>
<p>"Nothing." Grace pulled her hand away. "The paint's peeling."</p>
<p>She carried the plates to the sink and ran the water hot.</p>
<p>Nick finished his homework at nine — or close enough to call it finished. He brushed his teeth, said goodnight to his parents, and lay in bed with the lights off, listening.</p>
<p>Grace's footsteps in the hallway. Her bedroom door closing. The click of the lamp on her nightstand, and then quiet.</p>
<p>He waited.</p>
<p>Twenty minutes. Maybe thirty. Long enough for the house to decide everyone was asleep.</p>
<p>Then the sound came through the wall. Not from the house — from below it, from the garage, muffled by drywall and floor joists. Guitar. Acoustic, played soft, the notes dampened like someone was cupping the sound with their palm. Nick couldn't make out the song — just the rhythm, steady and slow, a fingerpicking pattern that rose and fell like breathing. Sometimes the player paused, and Nick could almost hear the shift of fingers on the fretboard, the small squeak of dry skin on wound strings. Then the music would start again, patient and unhurried.</p>
<p>Nick had heard it for years — since he was nine or ten, since the first night he'd stayed up late enough and the house had gone quiet enough. He'd never mentioned it. Some things you heard through walls were like prayers — not meant for you, not meant for anyone, and saying them out loud would break whatever made them true.</p>
<p>The music went on for a while. Nick lay still and listened, his sore fingertips pressed against the cool sheet, and the sound came up through the floor and the bedframe and into his chest like a second heartbeat.</p>
<p>Nick closed his eyes. The riff from "Walk This Way" moved through his fingers against the mattress — pull down, not up, right on the fret, feel that — and somewhere beneath him the guitar kept going, quiet and sure.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 9: "JUST WHAT I NEEDED"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Three hours and forty-seven minutes left.</p>
<p>I'm sitting in Studio B, the clock on the wall ticking past two a.m., and behind the glass my engineer — a college kid in a Bruins hoodie who thinks music starts with a laptop — has queued up The Cars' "Just What I Needed." Ric Ocasek's guitar figure bleeds through the monitors at low volume, clean and hungry, the kind of riff that sounds like wanting before you know what you're wanting. I realize I've been feeding you the end of the story without ever serving up the start. The Battle of the Bands in 1979 — that's the big finale, sure — but tonight we need to rewind the tape. Not the first garage jam. Not even the friendship. The instant two best friends looked at a middle school talent show and thought, hell, why not give it a shot?</p>
<p>I take a sip of coffee — gone cold hours ago, but it's fuel either way — and I think about May 1975. Not the night. The decision. The Tuesday in April when Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh stopped being two kids who played music in a garage and became a problem for every parent who thought the world came with a seating chart.</p>
<p>Outside the booth windows, Boston glitters in the August darkness, but I'm seeing Sudbury in spring — cut grass, warm asphalt, thirteen-year-olds who still believe the next four minutes might change something.</p>
<p>I need you to understand something about middle school talent shows in suburban Massachusetts in 1975. They weren't about discovering new voices. They were about maintaining the social order — proving that the kids who were supposed to win would win. I spent seven years at WBCN looking for kids who could win a Rumble at The Rat. The talent show circuit was the opposite. The Rat rewarded danger. Talent shows rewarded safety.</p>
<p>I reach for the cassette on my desk, the one labeled "Demo — Nick/Jackie — Not Authorized — May 1979." But tonight I'm not playing that tape. Tonight I'm telling you about the day they decided to make it.</p>
<p>Nick told me the story years later, in Studio C, while we waited for an engineer who was late and the coffee was bad and neither of us was in a mood to be polite about it. Jackie had been sitting on Dan Johnson's workbench, feet dangling, watching Nick fight with a Zeppelin riff on his acoustic. Middle school talent show — May 15th — Mrs. Reed had mentioned it in music class.</p>
<p>"We should enter," Jackie said.</p>
<p>Nick looked up. "Why would we do that?"</p>
<p>"Because we're good," Jackie said. Not bravado. Recognition. "Because we're really good, and nobody knows it except us."</p>
<p>That was the hinge. Not when they became friends. Not the first mashup in Dan's garage. When Jackie said *nobody knows it except us* and Nick realized he was right. Proof that nobody sees doesn't change anything. You have to carry it out and let people argue about it.</p>
