<p>They spent three weeks on it — garage magic translated for a gymnasium full of fluorescent lights and parents who thought Freddie Mercury was a Roman god. Queen meets Aerosmith. Risky as hell. Middle school talent shows aren't built for that kind of risk.</p>
<p>I glance at the console — the old Neve 8068 with its rows of faders and the coffee ring I've been meaning to wipe off since 1986 — and let the moment settle.</p>
<p>An ambulance wails down Tremont. Behind the glass, my engineer looks up from the mixing board, peering at me the way you watch a pot that might boil over. I crack my knuckles and keep going.</p>
<p>I'm not going to tell you how May 15th ended. You don't get the final score from the booth. You get the fluorescent lights first, the lemon floor wax, the photocopied programs still tacky from the Xerox machine, the rabbit that escaped into the bleachers during the magic act. You get two kids walking out with two acoustic guitars and no safety net.</p>
<p>Nick and Jackie drew sixth. Karen Bowers was closing the show — you know the type. Blonde, privately coached, voice built to spec. They weren't supposed to be the headline.</p>
<p>But I'm ahead of myself again, and that's the habit I'm trying to break tonight — narrating the recital from the third row instead of letting you sit in the gymnasium.</p>
<p>May 15, 1975. Ephraim Curtis Middle School. Seven-thirty p.m.</p>
<p>The folding chairs are waiting.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 10: "DREAM ON"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>May 15, 1975.</p>
<p>Ephraim Curtis Middle School gymnasium. Seven-thirty PM.</p>
<p>The folding chairs had been arranged in rows by Mr. Petrocelli, the custodian, who'd waxed the basketball court that afternoon with the same lemon-industrial compound he used every Thursday, so the whole room smelled like floor polish and the particular sourness that happens when two hundred people sit close together under fluorescent lights on a warm spring evening. The lights hummed at a frequency you couldn't hear so much as feel behind your eyes — a sixty-cycle drone that flattened every color to the same institutional yellow-green. Programs had been photocopied on the school's Xerox machine, the ink still tacky, and parents fanned themselves with them because the gymnasium had no air conditioning and somebody had propped open the fire doors, letting in the May air that smelled like cut grass and warm asphalt and did nothing to help.</p>
<p>Parents sat in their good clothes. Kids fidgeted. Teachers patrolled the aisles like nervous ushers, shushing the sixth-graders who were already throwing wadded-up programs at each other.</p>
<p>Backstage was a storage room that smelled like rubber kickballs and chalk dust. Karen Bowers was in the corner doing vocal exercises, her voice climbing chromatic scales with the mechanical precision of a music box while her mother adjusted the collar of her navy dress for the third time. Karen would sing "Tomorrow" from Annie, the closing act, the slot everyone understood was reserved for the winner.</p>
<p>Nick and Jackie sat on a folding table near the emergency exit, their acoustics resting against their thighs. Nick's was the pawn-shop Fender with the crack in the pickguard. Jackie's was his birthday Gibson, the frets worn smooth in the first position from six months of daily work. They weren't warming up. They were just sitting, the way you sit when you've rehearsed something so many times your hands know it better than your brain does and all that's left is waiting.</p>
<p>"You ready?" Nick asked.</p>
<p>Jackie pressed his thumb across the low E string and released it. A quiet thud. "Yeah."</p>
<p>That was all they said.</p>
<p>In the audience, Dan Johnson sat with his work boots still on, concrete dust in the creases of his jeans. He'd come straight from the job site because Grace told him he was coming. Grace sat beside him in her blue church dress, her hands folded in her lap.</p>
<p>Six rows back, Dr. Richard Marsh sat with Eileen. He wore a gray suit, no tie. He'd come because Jackie asked him to, which was unusual enough that Eileen had noted it without comment. Beside him, Elizabeth was reading the program with the critical eye of someone who'd already found three spelling errors.</p>
<p>The first five acts were what they were. "Für Elise" on a piano tuned a quarter-tone flat. A boy whose card tricks kept slipping. Three girls whose harmony drifted like a radio signal from a distant station. A kid reaching for Jimmy Page and landing somewhere around a campfire in the Berkshires. The audience clapped after each act with the polite enthusiasm of people fulfilling an obligation.</p>
<p>Then Mr. Hennessey, the vice principal, read from an index card. "Next up — Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh."</p>
<p>They walked out carrying their guitars by the necks. No music stands. No sheet music. Two thirteen-year-old boys in jeans and t-shirts — Nick in a faded Aerosmith shirt with a hole near the collar, Jackie in a plain white tee his mother had ironed that afternoon. They sat in two chairs at center stage and adjusted their guitars in their laps. Nick tilted his chair back on two legs the way he always did, then let it drop forward.</p>
<p>They looked at each other. Nick raised his eyebrows a fraction of an inch. Jackie nodded.</p>
<p>Nick's right hand came down on the strings.</p>
<p>The opening riff of "Walk This Way" hit the gymnasium like a sentence in a language the room didn't know it spoke. Not the album version — you couldn't get that from a single acoustic. What Nick played was Joe Perry's riff stripped to its rhythmic bones, all muted downstrokes and hammer-ons, the strings slapping against the frets with a percussive crack that bounced off the cinderblock walls and the waxed floor and came back harder. He played it with his palm half-damping the bridge, so the notes came out short and punchy and mean, more rhythm than melody, the guitar turned into a drum that happened to have pitch.</p>
<p>Four bars in, Jackie entered.</p>
<p>Not with "Walk This Way." With the descending piano chords from "Bohemian Rhapsody," transposed for guitar, fingerpicked — each note placed separately, deliberately, the way you'd set glasses on a table. The chords dropped through the spaces between Nick's riff like water through a grate. Where Nick's guitar was all attack and percussion, Jackie's was sustain and air. The notes hung and opened the sound upward while Nick's riff drove it forward, and for a beat — maybe two — the audience didn't know what they were hearing.</p>
<p>Then the two patterns locked.</p>
<p>It happened on the fifth bar. Nick's riff landed on the downbeat and Jackie's chord caught the upbeat, and the syncopation clicked into place like a latch finding its strike plate. The Aerosmith groove gave the Queen harmony a pulse. The Queen harmony gave the Aerosmith groove a ceiling. Together the two acoustics produced a sound bigger and stranger than either song alone.</p>
<p>The audience stopped moving. Not the polite stillness of attention. The other kind — the kind where the woman three rows back stops fanning herself with her program and doesn't notice.</p>
<p>Nick sang first. The verses of "Walk This Way" in his rough, untrained voice — a voice that cracked on the high notes and didn't care, that sounded like a kid who'd learned to sing by singing along to records in a garage with the door closed. He leaned into the microphone and the words came out hot and a little too loud, the way Aerosmith was supposed to be too loud.</p>
<p>Then Jackie answered. Not the next verse. The "Is this the real life" passage from "Bohemian Rhapsody," his voice clear and placed, each note supported from the diaphragm the way his teacher had drilled into him, a choirboy's instrument carrying Freddie Mercury's question. His tone cut through the room the way a bell cuts through traffic — clean, high, impossible to ignore.</p>
<p>Nick's voice was gravel. Jackie's was glass. In the call and response they'd built over five months in Dan Johnson's garage, Nick threw a line out raw and Jackie caught it and sent it back polished, and then Nick roughed it up again.</p>
<p>The middle section was where they'd spent the most rehearsal time and it showed. Jackie's guitar shifted into the arpeggiated bridge of "Bohemian Rhapsody" — the operatic section, all ascending figures and dramatic pauses — while Nick wove the "Walk This Way" riff underneath in a lower register, palm-muted to a whisper, just enough to keep the pulse alive. Their voices braided on a harmony they'd invented themselves, not Freddie's and not Tyler's but a third thing, a major third that split the difference between precision and abandon.</p>
<p>Then the crash. Both guitars open-strummed, full volume, both voices rising on the final chorus — Nick's rasp and Jackie's clarity fused into a sound that rattled the folding chairs and buzzed in the fluorescent light casings and hit the back wall and kept going, through the propped-open fire doors, into the May evening.</p>
<p>They ended together. A single chord, struck hard, left to ring.</p>
<p>The silence lasted three seconds. Nick glanced at Jackie. Jackie glanced at Nick.</p>
<p>Grace Johnson pressed both hands to her mouth. Her eyes were wet.</p>
<p>Then the gymnasium broke open. Not applause — noise. Parents standing. Kids yelling. Mr. Hennessey clapping with his hands above his head, which no one had ever seen him do. Mrs. Stephens, the librarian who'd shushed them a thousand times, on her feet with her fist in the air like she was at a concert and not a middle school event in a room that smelled like floor wax.</p>
<p>Six rows back, Dr. Richard Marsh did not stand. He sat very still. His left hand had opened once in his lap, thumb and pinky spanning an octave the hand had not been asked to hold in a very long time, and then it had closed again. Elizabeth was standing on her chair, clapping, her program on the floor.</p>
<p>Karen Bowers sang "Tomorrow." Her voice was flawless. Every note arrived on time, technically irreproachable. The audience clapped when she finished — polite, appreciative, the way you clap for something you admire but that hasn't changed the shape of the room you're sitting in.</p>
<p>The judges deliberated for eleven minutes. Everyone already knew.</p>
<p>"First place — Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh."</p>
<p>They walked back onto the stage grinning, holding their guitars, and the applause hit them again and they stood in it. Dan Johnson was on his feet, work boots on the waxed floor, clapping in the slow hard way of a man who built things with those hands every day and knew solid construction when he heard it. Grace stood beside him, her mascara ruined, laughing and crying at the same time.</p>
<p>Someone had a Polaroid.</p>
<p>The photograph: two thirteen-year-old boys, arms around each other's shoulders, guitars at their sides. Nick's grin enormous, crooked, his Aerosmith shirt riding up. Jackie's smile quieter but no less total, the kind that uses the whole face. Behind them, the banner — EPHRAIM CURTIS MIDDLE SCHOOL ANNUAL TALENT SHOW 1975 — in hand-painted letters on butcher paper, folding chairs askew, programs on the floor.</p>
<p>Dan drove the Johnsons home. Windows down, the May air carrying the smell of lilac from someone's yard on Dutton Road. Nick sat in the back seat holding his guitar case and the first-place ribbon and he couldn't stop talking — the riff, the moment the harmony locked, the look on Karen Bowers' face. Grace reached into the back seat and squeezed his knee, once, and let go. Dan watched the road and said nothing, but in the rearview mirror his eyes were bright.</p>
<p>At the Marsh house, Jackie came through the door still holding the ribbon. Elizabeth said, "You were the best thing that's ever happened in that gym, including basketball," and went upstairs.</p>
<p>Dr. Marsh was in his study. Jackie stood in the doorway.</p>
<p>"Your harmonics were good," his father said quietly. "The voicing in the bridge. That was sophisticated work."</p>
<p>Jackie didn't move. His father had never commented on his rock arrangements. Not once.</p>
<p>"Goodnight, Jackie."</p>
<p>"Goodnight, Dad."</p>
<p>Jackie sat on his bed with the ribbon in his hand and the window open to the May night. From down the street, through the trees, the slow rhythmic tock-tock-tock of someone's sprinkler. He thought about the fifth bar when the riff and the chord locked. He thought about Nick's face when the silence broke. He thought about his father's left hand opening once in his lap and closing again.</p>
<p>On Dutton Road, Nick lay on his bed with his hands behind his head, still hearing the sound. In the kitchen, Dan and Grace talked in low voices, and he could hear them laughing — Grace's high laugh and Dan's low one, two tones braiding the way two voices will when they've known each other long enough to harmonize without trying.</p>
<p>The Polaroid sat on Nick's desk, still tacky, the colors still deepening. Two boys. Two guitars. The gymnasium behind them already emptying, the folding chairs being stacked, the banner coming down.</p>
<p>But in the photograph, they're still up there. Still grinning. Still thirteen.</p>
<p>May 15, 1975. A Thursday.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 11: "RADIO, RADIO"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Three hours and nineteen minutes.</p>
<p>The talent show story settles over the booth like the last note of a song nobody wants to follow.</p>
<p>I take a pull of the Pappy. Let the burn settle.</p>
<p>"You want to know how I got here?" I say into the microphone. "Not the booth. Not WBCN. I mean here — the woman in the chair. You've got to go back further than Sudbury. Further than 1979. You've got to go all the way back to a bar in Allston that smelled like spilled beer and ambition, and a twenty-three-year-old who was stupid enough to believe that talent and conviction were the same thing."</p>
<p>I spin the chair toward the album wall. Third row, left side. My hand finds it without looking — Aerosmith's debut, the blue wings on cream, January '73. The vinyl's in pristine condition because I never let anyone else touch it.</p>
<p>"1973. Twenty-three years old. About to find the real thing for the first time."</p>
<p>The memory takes me like a riptide.</p>
<p>Tuesday in January. The kind of cold that makes your teeth hurt and your breath hang in the air like tiny ghosts. I drove my Dodge Dart down Commonwealth Ave — heater worked when it felt like it, which was almost never — heading to a bar on Brighton Ave called the Brickyard because a sound engineer named Paulie DeLuca had cornered me at the station that afternoon and grabbed my arm.</p>
<p>"Maxanne, there's a band playing the Brickyard tonight. You need to hear them."</p>
<p>"What kind of band?"</p>
<p>"The singer moves like he's trying to shake demons off his skin. The guitar player looks like he'd kill you and not lose sleep over it. They sound like what you'd get if the Stones and the Yardbirds had a kid and raised it behind a dumpster."</p>
<p>Paulie had seen ten thousand bands and been impressed by maybe six. So I went.</p>
<p>The Brickyard sat between a laundromat and a sub shop, a narrow room with a ceiling so low the tall guys hunched, a stage the size of a dining room table, and a sound system that had been state-of-the-art around the Eisenhower administration. The floor stuck to your shoes. The bathroom door didn't lock. The neon Budweiser sign in the window buzzed like a dying insect. The room held maybe twenty people. Half of them teenagers with fake IDs and nowhere warmer to go. A woman at the bar read a paperback, unbothered by anything. The bartender — a thick-necked guy named Sal — wiped glasses with a rag dirtier than the glasses.</p>
<p>Then five guys walked onto that postage-stamp stage, and the air in the room changed.</p>
<p>The singer first. Twenty-four, rail-thin, lips that belonged on a different species, hair that looked like it had been in a fight with a light socket and lost. He wrapped his hands around the microphone stand like it was the only thing between him and the floor. Didn't say hello. Didn't introduce the band.</p>
<p>He looked at the guitar player — dark-haired, cheekbones sharp enough to cut glass, eyes that said he'd already seen things he wasn't going to share with you — and nodded.</p>
<p>The guitar player hit the opening riff.</p>
<p>I've spent my whole career trying not to sound like a tent-revival preacher, so I'll just say this: the sound that came off that stage was alive the way a downed power line is alive. Dangerous, electric, throwing sparks into the night and not caring who gets burned.</p>
<p>Steven Tyler sang like a man who'd swallowed fire and was coughing up something beautiful before it consumed him. Joe Perry played guitar like each note was a personal argument he intended to win. The bass player laid down grooves that producers would spend thousands trying to replicate. The drummer kept time like a heartbeat in a chest that was running out of reasons.</p>
<p>They played forty-five minutes. Four people left during the set — the woman with the book, a couple who'd been fighting since I walked in, some kid who probably had a curfew. The rest of us stood with our mouths open like we'd forgotten how jaws worked.</p>
<p>I had my portable Panasonic cassette recorder in my bag. I carried one everywhere — the day I talked my way into WBCN I swore I'd never let a sound I believed in go unrecorded. The tape quality was garbage. Tinny, distorted, the Brickyard's acoustics making everything sound like it was being played inside a coffee can. But underneath the noise you could hear it. The signal underneath the static that said this is real.</p>
<p>After the set I walked up to the singer. He leaned against the wall by the emergency exit, smoking a Camel, T-shirt soaked through.</p>
<p>"Maxanne Sartori. WBCN."</p>
<p>He looked at me like I'd told him I was from Neptune. "The radio station?"</p>
<p>"The one where I'm going to play your album tomorrow night."</p>
<p>He stared. Then he smiled — this enormous, disbelieving grin that transformed his face from dangerous to heartbreakingly young. "We have an album. Nobody plays it."</p>
<p>"I'm going to play it."</p>
<p>"You're going to play it."</p>
<p>"On WBCN. Tomorrow. Eleven o'clock. If I can find a copy."</p>
<p>He reached into a duffel bag and handed me a record like it was a relic. "Dream On," he said. "If you're only going to play one, play that."</p>
<p>I took the album and walked out into the January cold, and the air tasted different. Cleaner. Like the frequency had been adjusted and I was hearing the world properly for the first time in my life.</p>
<p>The next night I walked into WBCN at ten-fifteen. Charlie Morris, the station manager, was still in his office — unusual for Charlie, who believed the night shift ran itself or it didn't deserve to exist. He had his feet on the desk and a joint between his fingers and looked up at me with the tolerant suspicion of a man who'd built his station on the principle that rules were suggestions.</p>
<p>"You've got that look," he said.</p>
<p>"I found something."</p>
<p>"You always find something."</p>
<p>I put the Aerosmith album on his desk. "Track three."</p>
<p>He studied the cover. Studied me. Dropped the needle.</p>
<p>For four and a half minutes, neither of us breathed. When the piano came in, Charlie closed his eyes. When Steven Tyler's voice hit that open-throated scream, Charlie opened his eyes and looked at me like I'd just told him where they buried the treasure.</p>
<p>"Where'd you find this?"</p>
<p>"A bar in Allston. Tuesday night. Twenty people in the room."</p>
<p>Charlie picked up the album. Turned it over. Pointed at me with the hand holding the joint, ash dropping onto the desk. "This is what we DO, Maxanne. Find the real shit. Play it tonight. Eleven. Full volume."</p>
<p>Eleven o'clock, I dropped the needle on "Dream On" without announcing it, without context, without checking with the program director who'd left at six. Steven Tyler's voice pouring out of radios across greater Boston — into dorm rooms at BU and Northeastern, into apartments in Cambridge and Somerville, into cars on the Pike, into a city that didn't know yet what it was hearing.</p>
<p>The request line lit up before the song ended. Every light on the board, blinking like a switchboard plugged into something too powerful for its wiring. I played it again at midnight. Twice more before my shift ended at two. By Wednesday afternoon, kids walked into record stores asking for the album by name. By Friday, it had sold more copies in Boston than in the rest of the country combined.</p>
<p>That was discovery. A woman with a cassette recorder in a half-empty bar, hearing something true and having the nerve to share it.</p>
<p>Three months later, a kid walked into Studio C for his first radio interview. Twenty-three, skinny as a parking meter, leather jacket that had seen better decades, a bandana that looked like he'd stolen it from a gas station mechanic. He sat down across from the microphone and his hands shook — not nerves, exactly, but the vibration of a body that contains more energy than it knows what to do with.</p>
<p>"You're Bruce Springsteen," I said.</p>
<p>"That's what they tell me."</p>
<p>"From New Jersey."</p>
<p>"Asbury Park. Born to be anywhere else." He grinned. Wild. Unpracticed.</p>
<p>I leaned into the mic. "WBCN, 104.1. I'm sitting here with Bruce Springsteen, who just released his debut album, Greetings from Asbury Park, N.J. Bruce, welcome to Boston."</p>
<p>And this kid — unknown, unpolished, absolutely electric — leaned into the microphone and said, with the kind of pure unguarded joy that fame will burn out of you before you ever learn to protect it:</p>
<p>"This is my first time on the radio. My mom lives out in California. HI MOM!"</p>
<p>The control room cracked up. I cracked up. And somewhere out in California, a woman probably pressed her hand to her mouth and understood that her son was going to be all right.</p>
<p>That was 1973. The year I found Aerosmith in a bar in Allston and Springsteen walked in off the street with nothing but a guitar and his mother's love. The year WBCN was exactly what a radio station is supposed to be.</p>
<p>I open my eyes. 2009. Studio B. The ON AIR sign still pulsing its cardiac red.</p>
<p>The album wall stares back at me. Aerosmith. The Cars. Boston. J. Geils. All discovered within spitting distance of this booth.</p>
<p>"That was what we were," I say into the mic. My voice sounds old to me now. When did that happen? "We didn't market. We discovered. We didn't focus-group a goddamn thing. We listened. We put our ears to the ground and when the ground shook, we told people about it."</p>
<p>I pick up the demo tape. Turn it in my hands. Demo — Nick/Jackie — Not Authorized — May 1979.</p>
<p>"That was 1973. By 1979 I'd learned other words. Sponsors. Demographics. Make it pay. But that's a different meeting, and a different fall, and we'll get there when we get there."</p>
<p>Outside, a bus pulls away from the curb on Boylston, its taillights red as the ON AIR sign.</p>
<p>I lean back. The chair creaks like old leather does — tired, holding on.</p>
<p>"But we're getting ahead of ourselves. After that talent show, summer came. Camp. Two boys who still thought the world had room for both of them."</p>
<p>I cue "Dream On." The album cut. The piano intro that still sounds like someone praying in a church they're not sure they believe in.</p>
<p>I press play. Steven Tyler's voice fills Studio B, fills the airwaves, fills whatever's left of a city that once believed a sound from a dive bar could change the world.</p>
<p>I close my eyes and let the music say what I can't.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 12: "SUMMER BREEZE"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Camp Elbanobscot sat on forty acres of Sudbury conservation land between Route 20 and the reservoir — an outdoor camp that for some odd reason specialized in music. Not archery, not sailing, not the kind of place where rich kids learned tennis and poor kids learned they couldn't afford to learn tennis. Music. Guitars and keyboards and voices carrying through white pines that had been standing since before the Revolution.</p>
<p>The wilderness part was real. Pine needles under your boots on the path to the lake. Pine cones scattered along the conservation floor, dry and resinous, crushed when you didn't watch where you stepped. Humidity that stuck your shirt to your back by ten in the morning. Hiking boots by the cabin door. Fallen trees left where they'd dropped — nobody on this land cut firewood; the blowdowns rotted slow and fed the moss. The lake had a dock, a rowboat with chipped green paint, kids fishing for sunfish with worms kept in a coffee can. Blue sky in July that went white at the edges by afternoon.</p>
<p>The music part was cared for. The converted barn had a piano that stayed almost in tune and acoustics that made everything sound better than it was. Instruments lived inside — cases latched, keyboards under tarps until someone needed them. You wiped your hands before you touched the keys. Counselors meant it. This was the strange thing about Elbanobscot: fifteen minutes from anybody's house in town, and the only currency that mattered was whether you could play.</p>
<p>Sunday, late July.</p>
<p>Grace's wood-paneled wagon came up the gravel access road with the timing a half-beat off in third and didn't stop until the tires were on the camp-side grass. Nick sat in the back with his acoustic across his knees and Jackie's case beside him — no seatbelt, window down, pine resin on the air mixing with the hot vinyl smell of the seat. Grace put the wagon in park and turned around.</p>
<p>"Two weeks," she said. "You earned it."</p>
<p>She had her wallet because she was Grace Johnson and she did not leave the house without it. She had a windbreaker over her sweatshirt though it was eighty-two degrees. She didn't get out.</p>
<p>"Go on," she said.</p>
<p>Nick climbed out with both guitars. Grace watched him cross to the registration table by the flagpole, then pulled the wagon back onto Route 20 toward Dutton Road and the mortgage and whatever Dan was building that Sunday.</p>
<p>Jackie arrived twelve minutes later in the back seat of the Marshes' Volvo wagon — college sticker on the rear window, upholstery still smelling like the chamois Dr. Marsh used on Saturdays. Stephen Marsh cut the engine and let Jackie out with his guitar and a duffel that had been packed by someone who believed in folding. Stephen didn't wave. Jackie lifted a hand to the closed window anyway, adjusted his strap, and walked up the same gravel Nick had walked.</p>
<p>They claimed bunks on the same wall of Cabin 7 that afternoon — screen walls, mosquitoes with open invitations, guitars propped against pine planks inside the cabin where the dew couldn't reach them. By dinnertime they'd mapped the place. The lake was a ten-minute walk through birch trees, path worn to bare dirt, pine cones littering the edges. The practice meadow sat on a slope above the water; if you played there at sunset the sound carried down across the lake and came back a half-second later, softened.</p>
<p>That first night, lying in their bunks while eight other boys settled into camp fatigue, Nick said, "This is different."</p>
<p>Jackie didn't ask what he meant. He knew. At Elbanobscot, nobody's last name meant anything. Nobody knew that Nick's house had a mortgage three months behind or that Jackie's father had opinions about rock and roll that could freeze a room. Here the only currency was whether you could play, and both of them were rich.</p>
<p>"Yeah," Jackie said. "It is."</p>
<p>The next morning they met Patty Lally.</p>
<p>She walked into the dining hall during breakfast and the room shifted. Not dramatically — nobody dropped a fork or stopped talking. But the air moved. She was thirteen, tall and lanky, with straight wheat-colored hair that hung past her shoulders. She was barefoot. Her feet were brown with dirt and she didn't seem to notice or care, and she carried a canvas bag slung across one shoulder with a songbook sticking out of it, pages flagged with torn strips of newspaper.</p>
<p>She smelled like something. Not perfume — the opposite of perfume. Something faintly animal and earthy that the other kids couldn't quite place, a smell that lived in her jacket and her hair and the leather strap of her bag. Southeast Sudbury, the wind off the Cavicchio fields, a smell that got into curtains and school notebooks and didn't wash out all the way. Patty had grown up breathing it. She never apologized for it because she'd never been taught that the place you came from was something to apologize for.</p>
<p>She slid her tray onto the table across from Nick and Jackie and sat down like she'd been sitting there all summer.</p>
<p>"You two are in the intensive, right?" Not a question, really. A confirmation.</p>
<p>Nick nodded. "Guitar. I'm Nick. This is Jackie."</p>
<p>"Piano and guitar," Jackie added.</p>
<p>"I know who you are." She bit into an apple, chewed, kept talking. Her hands on the apple were small and strong, nails cut straight across, a faint permanent stain at the base of her thumb and a white scar across one knuckle; Nick noticed and didn't ask. "Talent show. 'Walk This Way' folded into 'Bohemian Rhapsody.' You made Karen Bowers cry in the bathroom afterward. That was the best thing I've ever seen at Curtis."</p>
<p>"You go to Curtis?" Nick said, and felt immediately stupid, because of course she did. She'd been in the hallways, in the cafeteria, at assemblies, a presence he'd walked past a hundred times without registering.</p>
<p>"Same school since kindergarten," Patty said. She looked at Nick with a level gaze that had no accusation in it, just amusement. "You never turned around once."</p>
<p>Nick felt heat climb his neck. Jackie was very quiet beside him, holding his milk carton with both hands.</p>
<p>"I play piano," Patty said. "And I sing. And I hum constantly, which drives everyone insane." As if to prove it, she started humming something — a melody that moved in a way Nick couldn't quite track, rising and turning back on itself, restless and certain at the same time. He didn't recognize it. It sounded like it belonged to her.</p>
<p>She stopped humming and looked at both of them. "So what are we working on?"</p>
<p>We. As if the three of them had already agreed on something.</p>
<p>Over the next five days they became the thing the other campers talked about. Not because they were the best musicians at Elbanobscot — there was a cellist from Newton who could make you cry and a drummer from Framingham who played like he had six arms — but because something happened when the three of them played together.</p>
<p>They found it on the third afternoon, in the practice meadow above the lake.</p>
<p>Ninety-two degrees at noon. Thunderheads over the reservoir by three, a hard twenty-minute rain that knocked the pollen down and dropped the air ten degrees. By four-thirty the sky was washed blue and the grass was steaming and everything smelled like wet pine and electricity.</p>
<p>Nick was noodling on his guitar, working through a progression he'd stolen from Joe Perry and bent into something his own fingers preferred. Jackie sat at the portable keyboard the camp had carried up from the barn under a tarp, adding chords underneath — not copying, not competing, building a floor for Nick's line to stand on, the way he always did.</p>
<p>Then Patty started singing.</p>
<p>She didn't ask what key they were in. She didn't wait for a verse or a chorus. She opened her mouth and a voice came out that was lower than you'd expect from a girl that thin, with a grain in it like fine sandpaper, a texture that caught on the melody and held it. She sang without words at first — vowel sounds weaving between Nick's guitar and Jackie's piano like a third rope braiding into a cord. Her voice found the spaces the instruments left open and filled them without crowding, and when she finally landed on a harmony it locked into place with a physical click, like a door latch falling home.</p>
<p>Nick stopped playing. Jackie stopped playing. The silence that followed had a different shape than the silence before.</p>
<p>"Don't stop," Patty said. "Play it again."</p>
<p>They played it again. Before the instruments came back in, she took a breath and nodded at them both, and what they found on the second pass — all three of them opening their mouths on the same held breath — was a plain C-major triad a cappella for one beat: Nick's root underneath, Jackie's G above, Patty's E clean and unshowy on top, and she laughed.</p>
<p>The laugh was short and surprised and belonged to no one. It belonged to the chord.</p>
<p>Then the guitar and the keyboard came back under it and all three of them leaned into the song — Nick driving the rhythm harder, Jackie pushing the chords into unexpected territory, Patty's voice climbing above both of them and then diving underneath, finding notes that shouldn't have worked but did. The sound carried down the slope and across the lake, and the lake sent it back, and for a few minutes the meadow held all three of them inside a single resonance that felt less like performing and more like breathing.</p>
<p>The sun was going down. The lake had turned the color of copper. The light caught Patty's hair and turned it gold.</p>
<p>Nick looked at Jackie. Jackie was watching Patty.</p>
<p>Not the way you watch a musician. The way you watch a person who has just changed the terms of your life without asking permission. Jackie's hands were still on the keys but he wasn't playing. He was just looking at her, and his face held an expression Nick had never seen on him before — open, unguarded, the careful Marsh composure cracked just enough to let something raw show through.</p>
<p>Nick looked away. He looked at the lake, at the sky, at his own hands on the guitar strings.</p>
<p>"Again," Patty said, and grinned.</p>
<p>They played until the mosquitoes drove them inside. They played in the music building after dinner — doors shut against the damp, instruments wiped down on the benches — working through songs they all knew. Patty leading them into Fleetwood Mac, Nick pulling them toward Aerosmith, Jackie insisting on Queen, the arguments becoming their own kind of music.</p>
<p>The night they found the chord, Nick couldn't sleep.</p>
<p>Cabin 7 had gone quiet by eleven — eight other boys in their bunks, breath settling into the loose unison of camp fatigue, pine-board walls ticking as the day's heat let go of the metal roof. Somewhere out past the reservoir a whip-poor-will called its own name on repeat. A bullfrog answered from the reedy end of the lake. Someone three cabins over was working through chord changes on an unplugged electric, the notes drifting through the screens with the lake damp and the peepers.</p>
<p>The boy two bunks down from Nick snored like a tractor — the low hitch and diesel catch of an engine trying to turn over in cold weather, once, twice, then the long roll.</p>
<p>"You awake?" Jackie's voice came down from the bunk across the aisle, pitched so it wouldn't carry.</p>
<p>"Yeah."</p>
<p>A pause. Then Jackie said, "There's sheet music in our attic."</p>
<p>Nick rolled onto his side. "Yours?"</p>
<p>"My father's. Boxes of it. Piano." The word landed with the weight of a thing Jackie had not said out loud before. "I found it last year looking for skis. He doesn't know I saw."</p>
<p>"He played?"</p>
<p>"He played." Jackie was quiet a minute. "He doesn't anymore. The Baldwin in the den hasn't been tuned since before I was born."</p>
<p>Nick thought about Dr. Marsh coming home from the hospital at seven and reading a journal in the wing chair and not looking at the piano in the same room with him.</p>
<p>"My dad's got a guitar in the garage," Nick said. "In his toolbox. I'm not supposed to know."</p>
<p>"You opened it?"</p>
<p>"No."</p>
<p>The tractor-boy snored. The whip-poor-will kept its count.</p>
<p>"I don't know what I'm going to do after high school," Nick said. It came out before he'd decided to say it. "I don't know if I can do anything."</p>
<p>"You can play."</p>
<p>"Yeah."</p>
<p>"That's something."</p>
<p>"Is it?"</p>
<p>Jackie didn't answer right away. The kid three cabins over had moved on to something Nick didn't know — a slow descending line, almost a lullaby, the low E string buzzing a little where it needed a new set.</p>
<p>"It's what we've got," Jackie said finally.</p>
<p>Somewhere out beyond Cabin 7 the lake did whatever lakes do at midnight. The screen door of the counselors' cabin banged once and didn't bang again. Nick lay on his back and listened to eight other boys breathing and the whip-poor-will counting and his best friend not sleeping.</p>
<p>He thought about Patty's E on top of the triad. He thought about her hands.</p>
<p>He was asleep before he could think about anything else.</p>
<p>The first week of August they turned fourteen — a week apart on paper, a shared birthday on the camp schedule because the counselor running the intensive didn't care about paperwork and the boys had asked to split the difference. Sheet cake in the dining hall. Candles. The Newton cellist played something classical while everybody clapped. Patty sat between them at the table and stole frosting off Nick's plate with her finger and didn't apologize.</p>
<p>They played on the last night of camp, sitting on the dock with their feet in the black water, Patty humming that same restless melody while Nick picked a quiet line on his acoustic and Jackie added soft chords on a guitar he'd signed out from the barn because the keyboard wouldn't survive the dew.</p>
<p>Three voices. Three instruments. The sound moved out across the water in the dark, mixing with the loon calls and the lap of small waves against the dock pilings, and if you were lying in your bunk in Cabin 4 or Cabin 9, half-asleep, you would have heard it and thought it was one voice, one instrument, one song.</p>
<p>The last morning, gravel parking by the flagpole.</p>
<p>Patty wrote her phone number on the back of a camp schedule and tore it in half. She gave one half to Nick and one half to Jackie.</p>
<p>"You'll have to talk to each other to get the whole number," she said.</p>
<p>Nick laughed. Jackie laughed.</p>
<p>A pickup truck idled at the edge of the lot — Cavicchio blue, hay in the bed, an older brother behind the wheel who didn't get out. Patty climbed into the back with her canvas bag, bare feet on the warm metal, and settled against the wheel well like she'd been doing it her whole life. The truck pulled onto Route 20 without a wave.</p>
<p>Grace's wagon was already there when Nick looked for it. Grace in the driver's seat, window down, same windbreaker. Nick loaded his guitar into the back and climbed in front. No seatbelt. She didn't ask how it was. She put the wagon in gear.</p>
<p>Jackie's Volvo was last. Stephen Marsh had the engine running, windows up, air conditioning on. Jackie got in the back with his duffel and his case and lifted two fingers to the rear glass as the wagon pulled away. Nick saw it in the side mirror. Jackie saw the wagon go. Neither of them mentioned Patty for the ride home.</p>
<p>Nick watched the camp access road shrink in the passenger window — pines, gravel, the practice meadow gone behind the trees — and smelled pine resin on his hands from the dock last night, not from sap on the strings, and felt the ghost of those two weeks rise up through his chest like a chord he'd forgotten he knew.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 13: "BREAKDOWN"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School. September 1975.</p>
<p>The cafeteria hit you before you saw it.</p>
<p>Fryer grease and industrial tomato sauce and the sweet chemical fog of whatever they sprayed on the tables between periods — a smell that got into your clothes and stayed there, so your mother would sniff your jacket that evening and say you smelled like school, which was another way of saying you smelled like everyone and no one. Three hundred kids talking at once made a sound with no words in it, just a continuous roar, punctuated by lunch trays hitting formica and the occasional shriek of a chair leg dragged across linoleum. The fluorescent lights buzzed at a frequency that lived behind your teeth.</p>
<p>Nick and Jackie walked in together that first Monday, brown bags in hand because the hot lunch line was forty minutes long and neither of them knew the system yet. They stood just inside the double doors and looked at the room the way you look at water when you can't see the bottom.</p>
<p>The room had its own geography.</p>
<p>Six tables sat on a raised platform at the back — eight inches higher than everything else, which was all it took. Textbooks stacked. Spiral notebooks open. Highlighters uncapped and lined up by color. One girl had a day planner. It was September. She was already marking dates in November.</p>
<p>The glass corridor between the cafeteria and the arts wing caught the afternoon light through dirty windows and turned everything the color of an old photograph. Someone was always rolling a cigarette. Someone was always sketching in a composition notebook. A boy with safety pins through his jacket collar was reading a paperback with no cover.</p>
<p>The middle was the big undifferentiated mass — loud, safe, forgettable. The center of the room and the center of the bell curve.</p>
<p>The two tables nearest the auto-shop corridor smelled like motor oil and Brakleen and the metallic sweetness of fresh-cut steel. Hands stained in the creases the way Nick's father's hands were stained — the kind of clean soap could reach and the kind it couldn't. They ate fast. Lunch was what happened between engines.</p>
<p>Nick and Jackie didn't know the names yet. They just knew the room was enormous and loud and that they needed to sit down somewhere before the standing itself became a statement.</p>
<p>"There," Jackie said, chin toward a half-empty table in the middle section.</p>
<p>They sat.</p>
<p>For two weeks that was the arrangement — Regs table by the water fountain, brown bags, talking about the band they were going to start, about Patty's voice at camp, about whether the community center on Route 20 would rent them a practice room after school. The cafeteria roared around them and they were an island in it, the way the garage had been an island, the way the practice meadow at Elbanobscot had been an island.</p>
<p>It lasted fourteen days.</p>
<p>Monday of the third week.</p>
<p>Nick came through the double doors and Jackie wasn't at their table — not at the water fountain, not in the middle section where they'd sat for fourteen days with their brown bags and their plans for a band. Nick scanned the glass corridor, then the raised platform at the back, and found him.</p>
<p>Jackie was up there, second table from the wall, next to a kid in a polo shirt who was showing him something in a textbook. Jackie's notebook was open. He was laughing.</p>
<p>Forty feet. Nick could have crossed it in ten seconds. He stood with his brown bag and looked at the distance the way you look at a door you know you aren't going to walk through.</p>
<p>He walked to the shop-corridor tables instead, where Carl Marquis was eating a sandwich with black grease worked into the knuckles. Carl looked up. Nick sat down. Carl went back to his sandwich.</p>
<p>"You got shop second period?" Carl said after a while.</p>
<p>"Third."</p>
<p>"Harrington?"</p>
<p>"Yeah."</p>
<p>Carl nodded. The conversation was over and it was enough.</p>
<p>Nick ate his lunch. The sandwich tasted like nothing.</p>
<p>Across the room, on the platform, Jackie was still talking to the kid in the polo shirt — David Bellenghi, though Nick didn't know the name yet. Jackie's back was angled slightly away from the main floor. Not fully turned. Just angled. The way you angle away from a window when the light's too bright.</p>
<p>Jackie never looked for him — never scanned the room, never checked the table by the water fountain — or maybe he had and chosen not to, and Nick couldn't tell which was worse, careless or careful, and both left him sitting at a table that smelled like motor oil, eating a sandwich he couldn't taste, forty feet from the kid he'd walked in with on the first Monday.</p>
<p>Near the milk coolers, at the end of a middle table, Patty Lally sat alone with an apple and a canvas bag on the bench beside her, wheat-colored hair falling past her shoulders, bare feet tucked under the bench even though the linoleum was cold. She smelled like something faintly animal and earthy that the other kids couldn't quite place — southeast Sudbury, the wind off the Cavicchio fields, a smell that lived in jackets and didn't wash out all the way. From where she sat she could see Nick at the shop tables with his grease-stained company and Jackie on the platform with his polo-shirt friend and the forty feet of formica and noise between them, and she bit her apple and watched without waving.</p>
<p>That night Jackie showed up at the garage on Dutton Road at six-thirty with his guitar, as if nothing had changed.</p>
<p>He tuned up, ran through a chord progression, and waited for Nick to come in on top of it the way Nick always did — and Nick did, his line finding Jackie's architecture the same fit it had been since the talent show, since camp, since the conservation trails when they didn't have names for what they were building.</p>
<p>They ran through three songs. The garage filled with sound and the sound was right.</p>
<p>In the gaps between songs, in the silences where they used to talk, something was different. The silence had weight now — the weight of two people not mentioning lunch.</p>
<p>"Good session," Jackie said when they finished. He said it the way you close a door.</p>
<p>"Yeah," Nick said. "Good session."</p>
<p>Jackie packed his guitar and rode his bike home through the September dark, up Dutton Road toward the north side where the lawns got wider and the porch lights came on by themselves at dusk. Nick stood in the garage doorway and watched the red reflector on Jackie's rear fender shrink to a point of light and then nothing before he went inside.</p>
<p>Grace was at the kitchen table paying bills with the calculator that printed on the little paper tape — click, whirr, click — the sound filling the room like a mechanical heartbeat counting down something. Dan's work boots stood by the door, leather cracked, dried plaster in the treads. Nick passed through without saying anything and climbed the stairs to his room.</p>
<p>On his dresser, folded under a stack of homework, was half of a camp schedule with a phone number written on the back in Patty's block print. The other half was somewhere Jackie kept. Nick had known that since the last morning at Elbanobscot. He hadn't dialed it. He hadn't asked Jackie if he had.</p>
<p>He picked up his guitar and played nothing — just held it, the body warm against his ribs, and listened to the house.</p>
<p>Outside, the leaves on Dutton Road were just starting to go pale at the edges.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 14: "BABA O'RILEY"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>April in Sudbury was all mud. Hop Brook ran brown under the Route 117 bridge, loud enough to hear from the Lincoln-Sudbury front walk, and the lawn in front of the school had gone to the color of a boot print that had filled with water and frozen and thawed a dozen times since March. The parking lot was a lattice of tire ruts. A kid came up the walk, stepped wrong, went in past the ankle of his sneaker, said a word his mother wouldn't like, pulled his foot out with a sucking sound, and kept walking. Nobody looked. April.</p>
<p>Seven-forty-five. First bell in four.</p>
<p>Carl Marquis's '66 Impala came in off Lincoln Road from the south, throwing gravel, the muffler louder than it had been yesterday. Nick was in the passenger seat, book bag between his feet, a quarter-eaten Hostess donut in his hand, his hair still wet from the shower he'd taken at six-thirty. The radio was on an AM station playing something neither good nor bad — noise to fill the seven miles from Marquis's driveway to the school lot. The speaker in the driver's door buzzed on the high notes. It had buzzed since Carl and his father put the dash back together in November. It was just the sound the car made now.</p>
<p>Nick got out. He slammed the door the way you slammed the door of a car like that — a second slam, because the first one never caught.</p>
<p>Up the walk, twenty yards away, a wood-paneled Buick Estate wagon pulled up to the curb. The wagon was always clean. Dr. Richard Marsh had Saturday-morning habits about wax and a chamois, and it showed. Jackie got out of the passenger side, closed the door with the small respectful click a kid closed a clean car with, and lifted his hand once to the driver's-side window without looking at his father's face. The Buick pulled away. Jackie stood on the curb a beat longer than he had to, adjusting the strap of his book bag, then moved up the walk.</p>
<p>His loafers were polished. The bottoms of his jeans were an inch dark with Sudbury mud because he had walked across the lawn yesterday to save thirty seconds. He noticed it now, the small pull of dried mud against the denim at every step.</p>
<p>Nick was fifteen yards to his left, coming in from the parking lot at a diagonal. He saw Jackie and his head turned before his mind caught up — old habit, muscle memory from a year when they had walked in together every Monday with brown bags and plans for a band. Then Nick looked down at his own boots, at the donut in his hand, at the cigarette butt somebody had ground out in the gravel by his toe, and kept walking. His line and Jackie's line converged toward the same set of double doors and stopped converging eight feet before the doors, because Jackie went toward the right-hand door and Nick went toward the left, and they passed through at the same moment through doors hinged at different walls, and neither one looked at the other, and then they were inside and the hallway noise took them and they went in separate directions without a sentence between them.</p>
<p>The bell rang. In the window of the front office, a secretary picked up a rotary phone and began to dial.</p>
<p>Third period. Bay three.</p>
<p>The auto shop sat at the far end of the east wing — cinderblock walls painted the color of instant mashed potatoes, corrugated steel doors that rolled up on greasy chains, a concrete floor that sloped a half-inch toward a drain Mr. Harrington had been meaning to rod out for six years. Bay one was lecture. Bays two and three were alive. Bay two had a '68 Impala up on jack stands, hood yawning open, radiator pulled. Bay three had Nick's project and Carl's — a small-block Chevy 327 on an engine stand, heads off, the valve train laid out on cardboard in the order the parts had come out.</p>
<p>The shop smelled like Brakleen and hot metal and the gritty orange hand-soap in the gallon pump by the wash sink. A transistor radio sat on the bench by the compressor, tuned to WBCN, low enough that Harrington wouldn't make them shut it off. A woman's voice was on the air talking about a record Aerosmith had coming in the fall, and the boys in bay three didn't know that the voice would be gone from the dial in a few years and wouldn't have made anything of it if they had.</p>
<p>Nick had a socket wrench and a head bolt that had decided it didn't want to come. He leaned into it. His wrists were oil-dark to the cuffs, and there was a smear of grease on his cheekbone from where he had scratched a mosquito bite an hour ago without thinking about his hands first. Carl was across the engine stand with a torque spec sheet in one hand and a cup of shop coffee in the other. The coffee was bad. Everybody said the coffee was bad. Everybody drank it.</p>
<p>"Break or lean on it," Carl said.</p>
<p>"I'm leaning on it."</p>
<p>"Lean harder. It's a bolt, not your grandmother."</p>
<p>Nick leaned. The bolt went a quarter turn and then another and then it broke free and came out in his hand clean, and he set it on the cardboard in its place in the line, and Carl made a small approving sound and went back to the torque sheet.</p>
<p>The door from the corridor opened and Anthony Rossi came through — Ant, to everyone — a junior who was not supposed to be in the shop during third period because he had study hall in the cafeteria. He had a folded green hall pass in his fingers and a face that said he had no intention of explaining himself to Harrington because Harrington had stopped asking. He came up the aisle, hands in the pockets of a Raytheon-blue work jacket that had been his uncle's before it was his, and stopped at the engine stand to look at the heads on the bench.</p>
<p>"You doing the valves today?"</p>
<p>"Reseating," Carl said.</p>
<p>"Harrington letting you grind em?"</p>
<p>"He said he'd do it. He's not doing it. So it's gonna be me doing it and him telling me I did it wrong."</p>
<p>Ant laughed — short, no teeth, the laugh of a kid who had been the youngest in a loud house and had learned to fit his amusement into other people's sentences. He owned, with his brother Frankie, a 1970 Nova that lived in the driveway of a split-level on Route 20 behind the sheet-metal shop where his father was foreman. The Nova had a rebuilt 350 and rust through the back quarter panels and a bench seat his mother had reupholstered herself in brown vinyl from a roll she'd found at a yard sale in Marlborough. He knew a carburetor the way a piano kid knew a scale. He was going to work at his uncle's garage on Boston Post Road when he graduated, and his family already knew it, and Ant had been fine with it since he was twelve.</p>
<p>"Friday night," Ant said. "We going or we not going?"</p>
<p>"Going where."</p>
<p>"Kevin said the trails. His brother's got that Yamaha. Ride til dark and then Store 24."</p>
<p>"I told my mother I'd be home by nine."</p>
<p>"Why."</p>
<p>"Cause I told her I would."</p>
<p>"You heard about Gary."</p>
<p>"Gary who."</p>
<p>"Gary Hagan. Broke his collarbone on the trails last weekend. Went over the handlebars on the big hill behind the transformer. His mother told him he wasn't gonna play JV baseball this spring, and he cried. Sixteen-year-old kid, crying at the kitchen table over JV baseball."</p>
<p>"Jesus," Nick said.</p>
<p>"Yeah. So — Friday night. Trails. But carefully."</p>
<p>"Carefully," Carl said, and made the word sound like the opposite of itself.</p>
<p>Harrington came out from his office at the end of bay one, coveralls oil-black at the knees and pockets the way they had been for thirty years, a cigarette tucked behind his ear he wasn't going to smoke until lunch. He gave Ant a look that was not a question so much as a timer. Ant pushed off the bench, folded the hall pass a little tighter, and started down the aisle.</p>
<p>"Nine o'clock," Ant called back.</p>
<p>"Nine o'clock," Nick said, and did not look up.</p>
<p>When the door closed, Carl tilted his head toward the engine. "Your mother's gonna let you out at nine."</p>
<p>"My mother's working the four-to-close at Friendly's. I'm letting myself out at nine."</p>
<p>Carl grinned. The grin stayed on the torque sheet and didn't find Nick's face.</p>
<p>The '66 Chevelle hood leaning against the wall gave back the radiator's clank as a faint metal shiver. Primer grey. A pencil mark on the fender where the paint guy had measured for the body line. The wall clock clicked up another minute, and the shop went back to its own sound — compressor, radio low, Harrington's pencil somewhere in bay one scratching figures that meant something to men who built things for a living.</p>
<p>Glass Corridor. Fourth period. Lunch A.</p>
<p>Lenny Henderson was sixteen and had been smoking for fourteen months, still in the phase where he was proud of it and not yet in the phase where he was tired of it. He sat on the floor of the Glass Corridor with his back against the brick dado wall, legs out straight, a paperback copy of *The Stranger* open on his thigh. The paperback had no cover. It had come off on the T in February when he'd stuffed the book in his jacket pocket with a roll of dimes and sat on it for forty minutes, and now it was just pages and a bent spine, which he preferred.</p>
<p>The Corridor ran twenty yards between the cafeteria and the arts wing, dirty glass on the south side and brick on the north, and the two o'clock April sun came through at an angle that turned the smoke into a physical thing — a column of soft yellow light in the middle of the hallway, ash motes moving through it the way plankton moves through a shaft of undersea sun. An ashtray on a metal stand at the cafeteria end, chrome top, overfilled. The custodian emptied it Monday.</p>
<p>Lenny was rolling a cigarette one-handed because he could. Left hand holding the book, right hand with the paper between the first two knuckles, the Top tobacco tin balanced on his thigh, and he shook tobacco into the paper with a tilt that was almost elegant. He rolled, licked, sealed, tucked the cigarette behind his ear next to a pencil.</p>
<p>Two dado sections down, Maggie Doherty sat with her knees up and her head against the glass. Her leather jacket was cracked at the elbows, bought from a cousin in Dorchester last summer for twenty dollars. She was writing in a composition book with a ballpoint pen that kept skipping. Farther down, a junior named Walt was reading a paperback Dostoevsky in worse shape than Lenny's Camus. The three of them weren't talking. The point of the Corridor was you could be with other people without having to perform being with other people. The smoke did the work.</p>
<p>A chemistry teacher came through from the cafeteria end carrying a stack of lab reports, on her way to the arts wing. She walked past the ashtray, past Lenny, past Maggie, past Walt, without turning her head. Heels on waxed linoleum. She was gone through the far door in seven seconds. The ashes from her passing shifted in the yellow sunlight and resettled. Lenny took the cigarette from behind his ear and lit it with a Zippo that had been his grandfather's. He drew, held, let it out toward the glass. The glass fogged a square of itself and cleared.</p>
<p>Lenny Henderson lived on Goodman's Hill Road in a five-bedroom colonial with a flagstone walk and a tennis court behind the garage. His father was a dermatologist with an office in Newton and a second house on the Cape. The tennis court had been resurfaced in October. Lenny had not played on it since he was eleven, when he had gone one day without being told to and beaten his father in three sets, and his father had smiled and hugged him and then never asked him to play again.</p>
<p>Nobody in the Corridor knew any of this. Maggie had been to Lenny's house exactly once, the previous October, on a Friday when the family was in Chatham, and Lenny had taken her out the back slider to the court and they had stood on the baseline in the dark with their hands in their pockets and Maggie had said *is this yours* and Lenny had said *it's the house's* and they had gone back inside. That was as close as any Rat had come to the Henderson foyer. Lenny had done the math on what it would cost him in the Corridor if Goodman's Hill were known, and he had decided the Corridor was the first thing in his life he had chosen, and he was not going to let Goodman's Hill take it from him.</p>
<p>"What time is Hagan home," Maggie said without looking up.</p>
<p>"I heard four."</p>
<p>"His mom letting anyone see him."</p>
<p>"His mom is mad at the trails, at the Yamaha, at Kevin, at the transformer, and at the state of Massachusetts for building a dirt road behind her house."</p>
<p>Maggie laughed without moving. "Tell him to call me."</p>
<p>"He can't hold a phone, Mag. He broke his collarbone."</p>
<p>"He's got another arm."</p>
<p>Lenny smiled. He hadn't smiled in twenty minutes. "I'll tell him."</p>
<p>The fire alarm at the far end of the Corridor was behind a glass panel with a little red hammer attached to a chain. Someone had set it off in December and nobody had ever proved it. The kids in the Corridor knew who it had been. The kids in the Corridor were not going to say. Lenny let smoke out toward the glass square he had fogged before, and the smoke moved into the sunbeam, and Camus lay open on his thigh at a page he had stopped reading because the sentence had been good enough that he wanted to hold it for a minute.</p>
<p>Raised platform. Same period.</p>
<p>The Rampers' tables sat eight inches above the main floor on a section of poured concrete some architect in 1960 had thought would be used for public speaking. Six long tables. Folding chairs. A sight line of the whole cafeteria that nobody on the platform used except to check whether the hot lunch line was long.</p>
<p>Jackie sat at the second table from the wall with his brown bag open in front of him. Inside — his mother had packed it at six-fifteen — was a chicken salad sandwich on wheat with the crusts cut off, a peeled orange in a baggie segment by segment, two Pepperidge Farm Milanos in a napkin folded into a tight square. His mother was like that. His mother would be like that forever.</p>
<p>Kathy Bellenghi was three chairs down showing her day planner to a sophomore named Brooke, saying *the Junior Prom is literally six weeks away*, the third time in the lunch period she had used the word *literally* incorrectly and the fourth time today, and Jackie had started to count out of fondness more than irritation. Her highlighters were lined up on the formica in the order of the colors of the rainbow. She was filling a square on the sixth of May with pink. The prom.</p>
<p>Across from Jackie, Donnie Rossi was eating a meatball sub. Three sports this year — soccer, basketball, baseball — and the knuckle-scrape on his right hand from a headfirst slide on Feeley's mud Wednesday. The sub came from his uncle's deli off Concord Road, wrapped in white butcher paper saturated with sauce and sitting on an open section of the *Boston Globe* sports page so the sauce wouldn't stain the formica. Donnie did everything fast. His dress shirt had been ironed on Sunday night by a mother who woke at five-thirty six days a week to work the breakfast shift at the Friendly's on Route 20, and by Friday the shirt was a different object from the one that had come off the board — cuff-rolled, collar limp, a stripe of sauce at the second button.</p>
<p>"So, Doctor," Donnie said. He had called Jackie Doctor since the first week of September, and Jackie had stopped trying to make him stop. "What's the answer."</p>
<p>"The answer to what."</p>
<p>"Am I gonna ace the calc quiz sixth period."</p>
<p>"Did you study."</p>
<p>"I studied enough."</p>
<p>"Then you're gonna do fine."</p>
<p>"That's a non-answer. Give me an expert opinion."</p>
<p>Jackie set down his sandwich. "Expert opinion. You're gonna get a B-minus. You're gonna tell your mother you got a B-plus. She's gonna know you're lying but not make you take it back because she wants you to feel good about it."</p>
<p>"See," Donnie said to Kathy, who wasn't listening. "This is why I talk to the Doctor. He knows my mother."</p>
<p>"Everybody knows your mother, Donnie," Kathy said without looking up. "Your mother works the counter at Friendly's. I had pancakes from your mother's hand on Sunday morning."</p>
<p>"And were they good pancakes."</p>
<p>"They were fantastic pancakes."</p>
<p>"That's all I need to hear."</p>
<p>He went back to the sub. Jackie watched him a beat longer than he meant to. Donnie's right cuff had sauce on it now too. His mother would wash it tonight after the four-to-close, and it would be on the board by Monday morning, and Donnie would put it on with his eyes half-closed the way any kid put on a Monday shirt.</p>
<p>David Bellenghi was at Jackie's right, reading aloud from the Junior Prom ticket that had been mimeographed and handed out in homeroom, reading the part that said *semi-formal*, and laughing.</p>
<p>"Semi-formal means what, exactly."</p>
<p>"Means you can wear your father's tie," Donnie said.</p>
<p>"I don't own a tie."</p>
<p>"You can wear my father's tie. He's got three. One of them's paisley. You'll look like a lounge act but you'll be in regulation."</p>
<p>David laughed. Kathy did too, absently. Jackie did a small thing with his mouth that was the piano kid's version of a laugh.</p>
<p>Below the platform, at the milk coolers, a girl in mud-brown jeans and a red-checked flannel three sizes too big — a brother's flannel, cuffs rolled to the wrists, hem at the thighs — was lifting a half-pint of whole milk out of the case. A flute case was tucked under her elbow. Her hair was the color of wheat and uncombed at the back, and she was not looking at anyone. Patty Lally paid the cafeteria lady with a folded dollar and turned and walked toward the side door that went to the music practice rooms, and was gone before she could have been expected to register on anyone. Jackie's eye tracked her for two seconds, and then he was back to Donnie, and Donnie had said something that had made Kathy laugh, and the platform went on being the platform.</p>
<p>The bell would ring in four minutes. Kathy capped her pink marker. Donnie balled up his butcher paper with the sauce-stained *Globe* sports page inside it and carried the bundle to the trash can in a single walking motion, and on his way back he flicked the back of Jackie's neck with a forefinger as a hello and as a goodbye, and Jackie did not move.</p>
<p>After school the parking lot was mud and exhaust again, tire ruts filling with meltwater, kids moving toward buses and cars and the trailheads behind the shop wing where the dirt bikes would fire up by four-thirty. Nick came out the east door with grease still on his cuffs and Carl beside him talking about the valve job. Jackie came out the west doors with David Bellenghi and a group from the platform, loafers careful on the gravel. They crossed the same lot without crossing paths, the way they had crossed the same doors that morning, and the school went on humming behind them — fluorescent ballast, locker slams, a gym class whistle — the ordinary noise of a town that had sorted its children into rooms and expected them to stay there until June.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 15: "LANDSLIDE"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Grace was up at five-thirty. She filled the coffee pot and set it on the burner and stood at the counter while the house was still dark around her — November dark, the kind that pressed against the kitchen windows like it wanted in.</p>
<p>She made Dan's lunch the way she made it every morning. Tuna on white bread, the can opened and drained and mixed with mayonnaise and a little celery salt, the way Dan's mother had made it for Dan's father in this same kitchen for forty years before Grace ever stood at this counter. She spread the tuna with a butter knife, her hands moving without thought — knuckles swollen from hot water and fryer grease, the skin across the backs cracked in fine white lines like the glaze on old china. She wrapped the sandwich in wax paper, folded the edges tight, wrapped it again in aluminum foil. An apple. Two oatmeal cookies from Sunday night. Brown paper bag. Thermos of coffee, black.</p>
<p>Dan came down at six-fifteen. Work boots, jeans, flannel shirt. He poured coffee, drank half of it standing at the counter, set the mug in the sink.</p>
<p>"Let him sleep," he said, meaning Nick, who was upstairs in a sixteen-year-old's coma after staying up past midnight in the garage working through a chord change he couldn't get clean. "He's got trig first period."</p>
<p>Grace nodded. She was wiping the counter where the tuna can had left a ring of oil.</p>
<p>Dan picked up the lunch bag and the thermos. He kissed her — coffee on his mouth, chin rough because he never shaved before work, sawdust in his collar even fresh from the shower, the smell gotten into his skin years ago the way fryer oil was in hers. "Home by six," he said.</p>
<p>"Drive safe," she said.</p>
<p>The truck's headlights moved down Dutton Road and disappeared where the road bent toward Route 20. She rinsed the tuna can and poured herself coffee and sat at the table while the sky went from black to gray. A Tuesday. Ordinary. That was the thing that would break her in half later — how much it resembled every other morning, how there was nothing in it to grab onto and say here, here is where I should have known.</p>
<p>Dan had a 9/16ths socket wrench in his back pocket the way other men carried a wallet. Nineteen years in the ironworkers' union, never a lost-time injury, never a citation. He was on a tower job in the financial district — steel going up between the Old State House and the harbor, the crews called it by the street address the way crews always did. He was in the construction trailer below the thirty-first floor, blueprints spread on a plywood desk, the wax paper from Grace's sandwich peeled back, the tuna half-eaten beside his thermos.</p>
<p>At 10:17 AM a crane cable snapped.</p>
<p>The replacement had been scheduled two weeks earlier. Somebody in an office had looked at a number on a spreadsheet and decided the cable could hold a little longer. Four steel I-beams dropped. The roof of the trailer came apart like paper.</p>
<p>The call came to Friendly's at 11:47.</p>
<p>Grace was on the lunch line, apron pockets full of Sweet 'N Low and folded green guest checks, pencil behind her ear, name tag reading GRACE in the typeface they'd used since Eisenhower. Tom Andrews came out of the back office and stood at the edge of the dining room with a look on his face that made her hands stop moving.</p>
<p>She followed him to the office. The phone receiver was on the desk. She picked it up. Her hands — the same hands that had wrapped wax paper six hours ago — went white around the plastic.</p>
<p>A man's voice. Boston Construction. An accident. Your husband. I'm sorry.</p>
<p>Tom drove her to the hospital. Grace sat in the passenger seat still in her uniform, name tag crooked over her left breast, and looked out at Route 9 and the strip malls and the trees bare now, every branch visible against the white sky like cracks in something. She didn't cry. She couldn't yet.</p>
<p>At Lincoln-Sudbury, Nick was in trigonometry.</p>
<p>Mrs. Delano was at the board, chalk tapping in a rhythm his brain sorted into music anyway because that was what his brain did. The radiator clanked. A pencil rolled off a desk and hit the floor with a sound like a rim shot on a snare.</p>
<p>The classroom door opened.</p>
<p>Mrs. Patterson stood in the doorway — low-heeled shoes the color of oatmeal, gray cardigan buttoned to the neck, the look adults got when they'd been told something terrible and were trying to carry it across a room without dropping it.</p>
<p>"Nick Johnson? Could you come with me, please?"</p>
<p>The hallway was empty and bright. Fluorescent tubes buzzing at sixty cycles. Linoleum reflecting light in a long pale stripe. Mrs. Patterson's shoes clicked. Nick's sneakers made no sound. He followed her past the trophy case, past the gym doors, past the water fountain with its permanent puddle — all of it too sharp, too detailed, the way things got when your body knew something your mind hadn't caught up to yet.</p>
<p>Grace was on the brown couch in the principal's office. Still in her Friendly's uniform. Her face was a color Nick had never seen on a living person.</p>
<p>"Mom?"</p>
<p>"Sit down, Nick."</p>
<p>He sat.</p>
<p>"There was an accident at Daddy's job. A cable broke." She was looking at a point somewhere past his left shoulder, outside the building, outside the town. "Some beams fell. He's gone, Nick."</p>
<p>He didn't cry. Not then. He looked at her hands in her lap — cracked knuckles, fryer burns, wedding ring — and felt the world tilt and resettle into a configuration where Dan Johnson was not in it.</p>
<p>Closed casket. The doctor used the word trauma the way you'd use a term in a manual. Grace signed papers. Date of birth. Social security number. Next of kin.</p>
<p>Nick sat in a plastic chair in a hallway that smelled like floor cleaner and something underneath that floor cleaner existed to cover up. He counted the ceiling tiles. Sixty-four. He counted them again. Sixty-four.</p>
<p>The company would pay fifty thousand dollars, the minimum required by law. The insurance company would fight the claim for eight months. The Globe ran three paragraphs on page three of the Metro section — industrial accident, union man, survived by wife and son — and by the following Sunday the story was gone.</p>
<p>Tom Andrews drove them home from the hospital that first day. Grace went into the bedroom and closed the door the way she closed doors when the world had done something she couldn't fix with her hands — slow, precise, the latch settling like a decision. Nick stood in the kitchen where six hours ago his father had been alive, drinking coffee, saying let him sleep. The mug was still in the sink. Nick picked it up. It was cold. He washed it and put it on the drying rack and stood there with the water running over his hands until the water went cold too.</p>
<p>The funeral was Saturday. St. Mary's. Union men from the site sat in the back pews in their one good suit, hands in their laps, faces set in the expression of working men who understood the next body could be theirs.</p>
<p>Grace received every handshake with a stillness people would later call strength. Nick stood beside her in Dan's old sport coat, shoulders too wide, sleeves past his wrists, and shook every hand offered and held himself together the way cracked glass holds water — carefully, and only for so long.</p>
<p>Jackie came with his parents. He stood at the back after the service. When Nick finally saw him, Jackie didn't say anything. Nick nodded once. Six feet of candle wax and chrysanthemums between them, and the distance already beginning to mean something it hadn't meant a week ago.</p>
<p>Three days after the funeral, Nick went into the garage.</p>
<p>The door stuck the way it always stuck, wood swollen with November damp, and he had to lean his shoulder into it the way Dan used to, and the motion of it — his body repeating his father's body — sent something through him he didn't have a name for.</p>
<p>The garage smelled like sawdust. It would always smell like sawdust now. Dan's tools hung on the pegboard where he had placed them, each one outlined in black marker — wrenches in descending order, screwdrivers in their rack, the socket set open in its case with an empty slot where the 9/16ths used to live. Nick's guitar sat in its case on the milk crate in the corner. He didn't touch it.</p>
<p>Dan's red metal toolbox sat on the workbench, heavy and scarred, paint chipped at the corners. Nick had never opened it. You didn't open another man's toolbox the same way you didn't read another man's mail.</p>
<p>He opened it.</p>
<p>The top tray held what you'd expect — wrenches, screwdrivers, tape measure, a roll of electrical tape gone stiff with age. Nick lifted the tray and set it on the bench.</p>
<p>Underneath, in the bottom of the box, he found what he did not expect.</p>
<p>Three guitar picks, Fender mediums, tortoiseshell, worn smooth at the edges. A stack of handwritten sheet music on staff paper — Dan's block print, notes drawn with the same precision he used on blueprints. "Wish You Were Here." "Landslide." Chords in pencil, fingering in the margins — capo third fret, hammer-on to the suspended fourth.</p>
<p>Under the music, a spiral notebook, cover soft with handling, wire binding bent from being shoved into the box a hundred times. Nick opened it.</p>
<p>Pages of chord progressions in Dan's hand — not copied, original, moving from major to minor in ways that surprised Nick's ear even reading them off the page. A verse crossed out and rewritten and crossed out again:</p>
<p>Sawdust hands and a borrowed name The house remembers what I became</p>
<p>Nick sat on the concrete floor with the notebook in his lap and the picks in his palm. He could hear Dan in the progressions the way he'd heard him through the bedroom wall at thirteen — patient, unhurried, playing after the house decided everyone was asleep. He could hear him in the garage at six-forty, pressing Nick's ring finger flat against the fret wire, saying *feel that*, saying *there, you had it*. Two different rooms. Two different hours. Same hands.</p>
<p>When Grace found him an hour later, he was holding the notebook open to the chord chart for "Landslide."</p>
<p>"You knew," Nick said.</p>
<p>"He played after I went to sleep." She leaned against the doorframe. Her voice was quiet. "I'd hear it through the floor. He was good, Nick."</p>
<p>"Why didn't he—"</p>
<p>"Because we needed the money." She wiped her eyes with the back of her hand. "Because where he came from, music wasn't a job. He gave it to you instead. Everything he couldn't let himself have, he made sure you had the room for."</p>
<p>Grace reached over and closed the toolbox lid. The latch clicked shut — quiet and final.</p>
<p>Nick stayed on the concrete floor with the notebook still in his hands, the picks in his jacket pocket, the garage smelling like sawdust, the empty socket slot on the pegboard catching the light from the bare bulb overhead.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 16: "RUNNING ON EMPTY"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Three hours and change.</p>
<p>The ON AIR sign blinks red. Studio B smells like hot dust and old carpet and the bourbon in the paper cup at my elbow — Heaven Hill, two fingers, the kind of pour you measure with your thumb. The cassette on the console is warm from my palm. Maxell, ninety minutes, label in ballpoint: *Nick/Jackie — May '79 — NOT AUTHORIZED*. I keep picking it up and putting it down. The plastic doesn't get lighter. Nothing does.</p>
<p>Behind the glass, the engineer they've assigned me — Bruins hoodie, laptop open, Red Bull sweating a ring onto the board — thinks music starts when somebody opens a DAW. He doesn't know he's working my last shift. They drew straws for tonight. I drew short.</p>
<p>On the turntable behind me, the station's farewell record is cued. Pink Floyd. *Shine On You Crazy Diamond.* The corporate playbook says that's how WBCN goes out — graceful, elegiac, the kind of ending you can put in a press release.</p>
<p>I reach for the needle. I don't touch it.</p>
<p>You just heard how a morning ends.</p>
<p>November 1977. Dan Johnson on a job site in Boston. Cable. Beams. The kind of death that makes the Metro section and disappears by Sunday. I won't walk you through the funeral again. You know about the closed lid and the cold coffee mug and the latch on the red toolbox clicking shut.</p>
<p>What you don't know yet is what happened to the two boys after the lid closed.</p>
<p>The *Globe* gave Dan a paragraph on page three. Wedged between a fender-bender on Route 9 and a three-alarm in Framingham.
