They misspelled the last name — Johnson with an *s*, or an extra letter, I don't remember which, only that Grace saw it at Friendly's during her break and folded the page so the name faced the table and kept pouring coffee.</p>
<p>That's what a working man's death buys you in print. A paragraph and a typo.</p>
<p>Nick was sixteen.</p>
<p>Grace went back to Friendly's the next morning. Not because she was tough. Because the mortgage doesn't wait for grief, and when you don't have a cushion, sorrow is a luxury you clock in for. You carry your dead to work with you and you smile when booth four asks for more decaf.</p>
<p>Nick stopped talking.</p>
<p>The garage spoke for him. Dutton Road, concrete cold through his socks, Dan's tools still on the pegboard in the order Dan left them — Phillips-heads in descending size, the coffee can of sixteen-penny nails, the empty slot where the 9/16ths socket used to live before it went to a job site and never came home. Nick played until his fingers split and then he played some more. Not practicing. Bleeding pressure the only way he knew.</p>
<p>Jackie showed up in the doorway. Clean jeans. One hand on the jamb. Homework in his other hand, or an offer to sit and not say anything — I heard both versions over the years and they all sound like the same boy trying to be kind.</p>
<p>Nick didn't look up.</p>
<p>What he said — Jackie told me later, at the Paradise, like a man reading a charge sheet — was four words.</p>
<p>"I don't need your pity."</p>
<p>Not shouted. Not cruel. True enough to cut.</p>
<p>Jackie left. Came back the next week. Nick sent him home again. By spring of '78 they were nodding in the Lincoln-Sudbury corridor like people who'd met once at a party and couldn't remember why they'd liked each other. The cafeteria told on them without saying a word. Nick at the shop tables with grease on his cuffs. Jackie on the platform with a polo shirt and a group that did homework out loud. Same room. Forty feet of linoleum. Different doors.</p>
<p>Friendships don't always end in fights. Sometimes they end in a sentence you can't take back because it was accurate.</p>
<p>Two hours left.</p>
<p>You want a villain for this part? I can't give you one. Jackie didn't do anything wrong. He showed up. What he couldn't know — his father still at the kitchen table every morning, the Steinway lid half closed — was that if Nick accepted help from the kid on the other side of Hop Brook, Nick was admitting the brook existed. And the brook was the thing he couldn't afford to see.</p>
<p>So he sent him home. And the day after that. And the day after that.</p>
<p>John Lennon said it late in 1980, a few months before he went. About Bruce. *God help Bruce Springsteen when they decide he's no longer God.*</p>
<p>I've watched that prophecy come true in slow motion for thirty years. Not just Bruce. All of them. The machinery that lifts kids up is the same machinery that grinds them down. Nick and Jackie never even got lifted. The machinery went to work on them before anyone outside that garage heard a note worth keeping.</p>
<p>I look at the Pink Floyd LP. I look at the cassette.</p>
<p>I've been picking this tape up and putting it down for thirty years. It's the weight of something that should have flown — hollow bones in your hand, somebody telling you this thing used to fly.</p>
<p>Plenty of time left to get it wrong one last time. Plenty of time to get it right.</p>
<p>Before I tell you what I did with that tape — and what I didn't do — you need to stand on a piece of wood over Hop Brook on an October afternoon in 1978, oak leaves in the water, and watch two boys try to have a conversation their bodies remember even when their mouths won't.</p>
<p>The bridge is next.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 17: "BRIDGE OVER TROUBLED WATER"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>The second Saturday in October, 1978. Eleven months since the closed lid on Dutton Road — near enough to a year that Nick had stopped counting and started rounding. The woods between the Johnson house and Cavicchio's back field were conservation land now, the old stone walls running through them waist-high, moss-headed, the farmers who'd stacked them a century gone and the sheep gone longer than that.</p>
<p>Nick led on the CR125. He'd rebuilt it over two summers out of scrap Dan had let him haul home from the dump — a '72 frame, a '74 engine set in one August Saturday with a jack and a two-by-four, which was how you did it if you were Dan and his son. The pull-start took three today before the plug cleared. The bark of the two-stroke came up through his wrist thin at idle, angry at half-throttle, the pre-mix sweet in the cold air. The grips had worn smooth to the shape of his hands. The handguards were Cavicchio-mud, spring-brown, because Nick didn't wash a bike for weather he'd just put back in the weather.</p>
<p>The trail was their runway. You rode it because you owned it, and you owned it because nobody else wanted it, and you didn't own much else.</p>
<p>Behind him: Stephen Murphy on a YZ80 with a bent kickstand, which meant Stephen parked it leaned against whatever tree was nearest. Behind Stephen: Mark Pianka, who'd grown four inches over the summer and whose jacket sleeves rode up past the wristbones, so his thumbs showed pale at the throttle when he came on the pipe. Nick didn't call them a rhythm section. He just knew what a rhythm section felt like at his back.</p>
<p>The trail climbed a low rise through the pines and opened on the bridge.</p>
<p>The bridge was older than either family's time in town. A CCC crew had put it in for a fire road in the thirties; some county man had patched the deck in the fifties with a sheet of diamond-plate steel where a plank had rotted through, bolted into the oak with carriage bolts gone orange at the heads. In spring the brook under it ran loud as a kitchen sink. In October Hop Brook ran amber and thin, and you couldn't hear it except right under the bridge, where a flat slate rock split the current and made it say something.</p>
<p>Jackie was on the bridge.</p>
<p>Nick rolled the throttle off. The CR125 dropped to an idle and the idle filled the clearing. Jackie had both hands in the pockets of a Boston University windbreaker — his father's, Nick had known the jacket since they were twelve — and he was looking at the water like the water might answer something. He looked up when the engine came through the pines.</p>
<p>For a beat neither of them moved.</p>
<p>Nick's front tire was four feet from Jackie's loafers. Stephen and Mark had rolled up behind and let their engines idle down to the same nothing-sound. Three bikes coughing in a clearing and the brook under its rock.</p>
<p>"You need to move," Nick said.</p>
<p>"It's a walking bridge," Jackie said, not moving.</p>
<p>The almost happened then. The corners of both their mouths did a thing neither of them had asked them to do. Nick saw it on Jackie and Jackie saw it on Nick and for one half-count of the pipe they were thirteen again in a garage that smelled like sawdust, running a change that nobody in Sudbury could play but the two of them, together.</p>
<p>Then the footsteps came down the path.</p>
<p>Dr. Richard Marsh walked the conservation loop every Saturday in every month that wasn't January. He walked it in pressed khakis and rubber-soled Rockports and, today, the bottle-green half-zip he kept for mornings the windbreaker wasn't on its hook. His son had borrowed the windbreaker without asking. Richard had seen the absence on the hook and felt a small pleased thing and taken the half-zip down without comment.</p>
<p>He came up the path and saw his son on the bridge and three dirt bikes across from him, and his first thought was protective and clean. He came onto the plank deck. His eyes moved over the four boys the way they moved over an X-ray — pattern first, detail after. The lead boy he recognized. The Johnson boy. He had been in the house once, four years ago, holding a Coca-Cola in both hands the way a boy holds a Coca-Cola when he has not been in many houses like this one.</p>
<p>Richard's eyes moved down to the boy's left hand on the throttle grip.</p>
<p>The fingertips were callused in a line across the tops — not on the pads, on the very tops, the way a year of pressing steel strings into a fretboard too tall for a boy's hand shaped the skin and kept shaping it. Richard was not a guitarist. He had never owned one. But a musician's hand was a musician's hand; he had lived in conservatory practice rooms long enough to know the shape of sustained commitment even when it wore a different grain than his own. The boy's thumb rode loose at the heel of the palm. The first knuckle carried a little white scar, not from labor. Something moved in Richard's chest that he had not invited and did not name. He looked away.</p>
<p>"Everything all right, Jackson?"</p>
<p>His son didn't answer right away.</p>
<p>"These boys bothering you, son?"</p>
<p>*These boys.*</p>
<p>Jackie felt the two words land the way a bruise lands — not at the surface, underneath. His mouth closed down on the thing that had almost happened, and his face put itself on the way he put it on at Mrs. Caldwell's Sunday recitals, the face his father thought was composure.</p>
<p>His lungs were tight with cold air. Pine sap had come off a low branch onto the khakis on his left thigh, a dark streak. His father's Old Spice came off the windbreaker in small warm waves when he moved — the smell of every morning he'd watched his father leave the house — and the smell now felt like a hand on his shoulder he had not asked for.</p>
<p>"No, Dad," Jackie said. "I was just telling them it's a walking bridge."</p>
<p>The almost-smile was gone. He had felt it go. Some muscle at the corner of his mouth that the body had kept ready for Nick for five years gave up its readiness, and the face was the face again.</p>
<p>He stepped aside.</p>
<p>He didn't decide to step aside. His legs did it before he did. Two measured steps to the right, the planks spongy under the loafers, his weight coming off the centerline of the bridge and onto the downstream rail — the same two steps he'd learned for recital wings and cocktail-party doorways.</p>
<p>Nick heard *these boys* through the CR125 idle and heard it clean.</p>
<p>Something in his chest wanted to kill the engine and step off the bike and say *Jackie, it's us.* Something else in his chest wanted to put a fist into the Boston University windbreaker. The thing that won was neither. His right wrist tightened on the throttle half a turn, and the pipe bark jumped from nothing to something, and his jaw hinge tightened against itself, and the inside of his cheek cut against a molar and he tasted the small bright copper of his own blood.</p>
<p>He opened the throttle.</p>
<p>The front tire rolled onto the metal plate.</p>
<p>The plate rang.</p>
<p>One note, clean — the note a horseshoe makes dropped on a rail — the whole bridge saying it at once. Then the oak planks took the weight and groaned, the deep dry groan of old white-oak under a load it had not been asked to take, and under the planks the stringers shook, and from the underside of the stringers a fine red rain-rust sifted down into the amber water.</p>
<p>Stephen came next. The plate rang. The planks groaned. Mark came last. The plate rang a third time, flatter, because the plate was warm now.</p>
<p>Four bikes on a walking bridge. Three rings. Three groans. The stringers complaining in a register Nick felt up through the footpegs and into his shins.</p>
<p>He did not look back.</p>
<p>On the far bank the trail opened into the fire road, and the fire road ran down toward Cavicchio's back field, and the corn there was cut to stubble and the crows were working the rows in that patient black way crows work a field in October. Nick rolled through the stubble edge and opened the throttle, and the engine bark got smaller in the woods behind him, and then there was no bark at all — just the small flat sound of Hop Brook under its rock, and the smell of hot two-stroke oil staying in the pines the way a thing stays after the thing that made it has gone.</p>
<p>Stephen peeled off at the Y for home. Mark peeled off at Dutton. Nick did not go home.</p>
<p>He circled back the long way — past the stone wall where the sheep had been and weren't, up the rise through the pines — and came down on the bridge from the south side, alone, and cut the engine.</p>
<p>The silence came back with the smell of hot oil.</p>
<p>Jackie was gone. Dr. Marsh was gone. The plate where Nick's tire had rung it was warm still — he could feel it through the sole of his boot when he set the foot down — and a little dust of rain-rust lay in a half-moon at the downstream edge of the plate where the stringers had shaken it loose.</p>
<p>He sat the bike and looked at his left hand on the grip.</p>
<p>Nobody had ever looked at his hand the way the man in the half-zip had looked at it. He did not know what the look had meant.</p>
<p>In his jacket pocket, three Fender mediums — tortoiseshell, worn smooth at the edges — clicked against each other when he shifted his weight. He set his palm flat over the pocket to keep them quiet and rode home the long way, past the corn stubble, with the crows lifting and settling behind him in twos.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 18: "ROCK 'N' ROLL FANTASY"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Monday, October 16, 1978. Seven-thirty in the morning, and the music room at Lincoln-Sudbury still smelled of Friday's varnish — the janitor had refinished the risers over the weekend and the air hung sharp with turpentine. Cinder block walls the color of canned peas. Fluorescents with one tube stuttering. A faded Boston Symphony poster curling off its thumbtacks near the door.</p>
<p>And beside it, something brand new.</p>
<p>The poster was a photocopy — that soft Xerox black where the ink had spread a hair into the paper, the edges of the letters breathed-on. Four push-pins, one at each corner, punched straight through legal-sized paper. The Lincoln-Sudbury crest sat slightly off-center, canted two degrees off true, as if the kid on the copier had been in a hurry.</p>
<p>BATTLE OF THE BANDS May 16, 1979 Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School Grand Prize: $500 — Professional Recording Session Sponsored by WBCN — The Rock of Boston</p>
<p>Nick Johnson came in through the west door at 7:31, on his way to first-period shop. He was reading it by 7:32. Jackie Marsh came in through the east door at 7:33, having left his sheet music in the practice room the night before, and saw Nick before he saw the poster. He read the poster anyway. They stood a foot apart at the bulletin board and their shoulders did not quite touch.</p>
<p>Five hundred dollars. Grace Johnson made two sixty-five an hour at the Friendly's on Route 20, plus tips on a good Sunday. A month of shifts would not close that number. It was also the amount Jackie's father spent on a weekend in Chatham without remarking on it — which meant the figure read one way to the boy coming in through the west door and another way entirely to the boy coming in through the east.</p>
<p>From his desk, Robert G. Wentworth watched the two boys read. He did not stand. He kept the red pencil in his hand and kept it moving over the scale sheets in front of him, as if the check marks he was making were the reason he was looking up between them. He had seen students read a poster before. He had never seen two students read one the way Nick and Jackie were reading this one — as if the paper might, if looked at long enough, tell them what to do about each other.</p>
<p>"Gentlemen," he said. "I see you've noticed the announcement."</p>
<p>Neither boy moved.</p>
<p>"Band names due Friday. Eight slots."</p>
<p>Jackie spoke first, to the poster. "Queen."</p>
<p>Nick spoke to the poster. "Aerosmith."</p>
<p>Wentworth set the pencil down. "The competition is meant to celebrate —"</p>
<p>"It's about the music," Nick said.</p>
<p>"The music," Jackie said. "Not the story around it."</p>
<p>"Right."</p>
<p>"Just the sound."</p>
<p>"Just what you can do."</p>
<p>Nick turned his head a half-inch. Jackie turned his. For a beat the two of them stood with their faces angled toward each other without quite facing each other, the way two dogs stand before they decide whether they know one another.</p>
<p>Wentworth opened the top drawer of his desk.</p>
<p>"Wait," he said. "Hold on a minute."</p>
<p>Under a stack of school-issue staff paper there was a manila envelope that had been in that drawer since September. In the envelope was a photograph. His fingers found it the way they used to find the mouthpiece of a trumpet in the dark. He slid it out and laid it on the blotter and flattened it with the heel of his hand.</p>
<p>Ephraim Curtis Middle School Talent Show, May 15, 1975. Two boys standing on a gymnasium stage draped in crepe paper. Thirteen years old, one guitar each, matching too-big sport coats borrowed from fathers. Nick's hair a little longer then. Jackie's a little shorter. They had their free arms around each other's shoulders and they were grinning the kind of grin that gets you photographed — a grin that had not yet learned to be careful about anything.</p>
<p>"I was there," Wentworth said. "You boys played 'Walk This Way' into 'Bohemian Rhapsody' as a mashup that had no business working. It worked."</p>
<p>He turned the photograph toward them.</p>
<p>The music room clock, which lost forty seconds a day, ticked.</p>
<p>Nick's face did something — a small collapse at the jaw, a flicker above the eyes — that neither Wentworth nor Jackie could have put into a word, but Jackie saw it, and Jackie's hand came halfway out of his pocket and stopped and went back in. The fluorescent in the corner buzzed. Down the hall a locker slammed.</p>
<p>Then Nick's eyes came off the photograph and went back to the poster, and Jackie's followed a half-second behind.</p>
<p>"That was middle school," Nick said.</p>
<p>"We're different."</p>
<p>"Yeah."</p>
<p>The bell rang. Thirty seconds from a corridor of bodies.</p>
<p>"May the best band win," Nick said, because the other sentences did not have a way to be said.</p>
<p>"Count on it."</p>
<p>They left through the doors they had come in by. Wentworth sat with the photograph on the blotter in front of him, and did not put it away.</p>
<p>He did not put it away for forty minutes.</p>
<p>The first-period kids filed in past him and he took attendance by ear, counting the thumps of trumpet cases on the tile without looking up. The photograph lay where he had set it down, two boys with their arms around each other's shoulders, and beside it, because he had opened the drawer far enough, lay the other objects that had been in it for twenty years.</p>
<p>A dried-out reed in a cardboard slip, last damp in 1963.</p>
<p>A program, folded twice along old creases, from Jordan Hall, March 8, 1958: New England Conservatory Undergraduate Showcase, Robert G. Wentworth, trumpet, Hindemith Sonata, second movement. The paper had gone the color of weak tea at the folds.</p>
<p>A metronome that did not work.</p>
<p>He looked at the photograph of Nick and Jackie and felt the gap open a half-inch wider — the same gap between Jordan Hall and the Lincoln-Sudbury W-2, between the Hindemith in the hall closet and the red pencil on his desk. He had lived in that gap a long time.</p>
<p>The call letters on the poster outside his office — WBCN — still carried Maxanne Sartori's signature on the sponsorship line. She was the one who had played the Neighborhoods out of Upton when nobody else would. Wentworth had the thought and then put it down, the way he put most thoughts down, and closed the drawer without locking it.</p>
<p>By third period someone had pinned a second copy above the cafeteria milk coolers. The toner on both smelled faintly of ozone. Nick saw it on his way to lunch and did not stop walking. Jackie saw it on his way to the platform tables and did not stop either.</p>
<p>That night, Nick stayed in the garage on Dutton Road past midnight with the tube amp humming and the opening of "Dream On" under his fingers for the fortieth time — the way you grind a thing down until it either gives up its secret or breaks your hand. The concrete was cold through his sneakers. The pegboard on the wall was his father's. He did not stop.</p>
<p>In the music room at L-S, at five-past-six that same evening, Jackie Marsh held the high note in "Somebody to Love" until his throat gave out, then drank from the tap over the utility sink and tried it again. The fluorescent stuttered. His voice, when it came back, was raw at the top. He went again.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 19: "BORN TO RUN"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>The L-S cafeteria smelled like fish sticks and canned tomato sauce and the plastic tang of trays still hot from the dishwasher. Friday menu. Above the steam tables a fluorescent ballast hummed at a frequency Nick's ear sorted, against his will, into a flat B-natural — the note it had hummed at every lunch for four years, the school's own pedal tone. He had his back to the cinder block wall and a tuna sandwich in wax paper Grace had made at five-forty that morning before the pickup left for Friendly's on Route 20.</p>
<p>Stephen Murphy sat across from him with a chocolate milk. Mark Pianka drummed the edge of the formica with two fingers, unaware, the way he drummed everything. Three weeks since the Battle of the Bands poster had gone up, and the cafeteria had sorted itself without anyone moving a chair. Jackie Marsh's table was two rows over on the raised platform — the college-bound kids, the ones who said SAT the way other kids said Budweiser.</p>
<p>Carl Marquis had a Panasonic boom box on the bench beside him, volume low, the tape inside playing "Sweet Emotion" through a speaker the size of a lunch tray. Against policy. Nobody enforced it on Fridays when the lunch monitors were counting fish sticks. The bass line walked under the roar of three hundred voices and Nick's ear followed it the way his ear followed everything that sounded like his side of the argument.</p>
<p>Jackie stood up.</p>
<p>The scrape of his chair on linoleum went through the room like a struck cymbal. Carl's finger came off the pause button without thinking. The Aerosmith tape died mid-riff. A hush traveled outward in rings — first the kids who had been facing the platform, then the tables alongside, then the back benches where the Ramper girls sat, one of them mid-sentence with her hand lifted. Her hand stayed lifted. Stephen Murphy set his chocolate milk down very carefully, the way you set down a glass you've just learned was full of something breakable.</p>
<p>"'Somebody to Love,'" Jackie said.</p>
<p>He sang.</p>
<p>In the music room his voice had a ceiling. Here it didn't. The cinder block threw everything back at harder angles — a slap rather than a bloom — and Jackie rode the slap, Freddie Mercury through a kid trained since he was eight, operatic at the top, and here and there the trained edge frayed because he was seventeen and because you cannot really sing Queen at lunch without the frayed edge showing. David Bellenghi, beside him in a polo shirt, fumbled into the harmony a half-beat late. The fish-stick steam rose past Jackie in the light. A lunch lady came to the kitchen doorway holding a pair of tongs and did not move again.</p>
<p>When Jackie hit the last high one he had to push, but he got there, and the note held against the block walls and came back at him from the other side.</p>
<p>Nick set down his sandwich and stood up.</p>
<p>"'Dream On,'" he said.</p>
<p>No guitar. Nothing in his hands. His throat was dry. He swallowed and his ear found the note from the hum in the ballast above him and he stepped onto it like a man stepping onto a porch. Stephen Murphy came in under him, third below, rough — not good, honest. Mark Pianka flattened his hands on the formica and tapped the opening figure of the piano part, right hand arpeggio against the tabletop, fingers finding keys that were not there.</p>
<p>Carl hit his tray once with the edge of a fork — one sharp ring on the downbeat, then he stopped, embarrassed, and went back to holding the fork like a fork.</p>
<p>Nick sang. His voice was not trained. It went where he had dragged it — out past a closed lid on a red toolbox and back, past a hundred nights in a garage learning what his fingers could be made to do. On the long word — *dream* — the cafeteria went still. He went up for the top of it — *dream until your dreams come true* — and he pushed, and he got there, and the high note was thinner than Jackie's and rawer than Jackie's and nobody in that cafeteria mistook it for anything but what it was.</p>
<p>The lunch lady with the tongs still had not moved.</p>
<p>Then the noise came — not choreographed, not a number, just three hundred teenagers who had been holding their breath and couldn't anymore. Lockers would slam in the hall. Somebody would tell somebody else. That was later.</p>
<p>"Sit down." Mr. Wentworth stood at the end of the shop-corridor tables, hands in the pockets of his corduroy jacket, the music teacher's face set in the expression of a man trying not to grin. "Both of you. Principal's office. Now."</p>
<p>Brad Sargent had been principal at L-S for twenty years and vice principal for eight before that. His office had a gray metal desk and a carpet the color of wet oatmeal that smelled like thirty years of industrial solvent laid over thirty years of whatever the solvent was laid over. On the desk sat a framed photograph of two kids in a canoe, a pen cup of Bic Clics, and a white ceramic coffee mug that read KEEFE TECH COUGARS — BASEBALL '63 in green letters faded at the rim where his thumb had gone a thousand times.</p>
<p>Nick sat in one chair. Jackie sat in the other. The chairs were molded plastic and exactly the same height.</p>
<p>"I have reports," Sargent said. He looked at a manila folder on his desk and did not open it. "Kids skipping class to rehearse. An argument in C-wing last Thursday. Mrs. Fiore says she can't get through homeroom announcements because of you." He tipped the mug an inch, set it back. "And now I hear singing in the cafeteria."</p>
<p>"We were —" Jackie began.</p>
<p>"I don't want to know what you were." Sargent leaned back. The chair creaked. A fluorescent tube in the ceiling ticked like a cheap watch. "I got a fire marshal who signed off on the auditorium for May sixteenth. I got a station calling my office about a sponsorship. I got a superintendent who wants to know why his senior class is using the cafeteria as a rehearsal hall." He looked at Nick. He looked at Jackie. "You want me to cancel it?"</p>
<p>"No, sir," they said. Same word. Same moment.</p>
<p>Sargent almost smiled.</p>
<p>"Then off school property. You want to sing at each other, do it somewhere my secretary can't hear it. You have until May sixteenth. After that I don't care what you do." He reached for the mug. "Get out of my office."</p>
<p>In the hallway the lockers were pale green and their sneakers did not quite squeak on the waxed floor. They walked side by side because the hallway was narrow. Jackie's shoulder was an inch from Nick's shoulder and did not touch it.</p>
<p>Near the milk coolers, Patty Lally watched them pass with her canvas bag on her hip and her apple half-eaten. She did not wave. She had heard every note from where she sat — the Queen slap off the block walls, the raw *dream* that wasn't thinner to her, just true — and she knew what the hallway was going to do to them before they reached the doors.</p>
<p>"It's on," Nick said.</p>
<p>"Set list coming?"</p>
<p>"Yeah. Yours?"</p>
<p>"Yeah."</p>
<p>They reached the fork in the corridor. Jackie pushed left toward the arts wing. Nick pushed right toward the shop doors. Forty feet of linoleum again, only vertical this time.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 20: "HOTEL CALIFORNIA"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Early April, 1979. The Howard Miller grandfather clock in the hall struck six-thirty and the Marshes sat. That was how it worked on Maple Street in southwest Sudbury — the clock began to strike, and by the fourth tone the four of them were at the table with napkins across their laps. The tick kept going after the chime. Seventy-two to the minute. Andante.</p>
<p>The table was mahogany, the grain running the long way, a single dark whorl near the head like a water-stain from someone else's grief. The silver had belonged to Eileen's father — W.B.H. engraved at the shaft end of each knife, W. B. Hollister, a Beacon Hill man who had lived long enough to see his daughter marry a psychiatrist and long enough to let her have the forks. The money in this house had come from her side. Jackie had worked that out by eleven without anyone telling him. Richard had worked out something else — that Hollister forks and a Concord address were a starting line, not a finish, and that the old money in the northwest quarter of Sudbury still looked through people who had only arrived by marriage.</p>
<p>Eileen brought the plates in on a tray. Chicken piccata. The capers sat in a thin gold sauce that caught the chandelier light and threw it back in small wet stars. The chicken pounded thin as stationery and laid in overlapping half-moons. Against the long wall, between the windows and the sideboard, stood the walnut upright Jackie had never seen his father touch. Its ivories had yellowed. Its music stand held an open hymnal nobody sang out of. Above it hung a Rothko reproduction — deep red over deep black, tasteful and just off-center in a way that pretended not to be. The brass switchplate by the door was polished Tuesdays.</p>
<p>"Jackson received his acceptance letter," Eileen said. She had said it twice already today — to Mrs. Caldwell on the phone, to Jackie in the kitchen. This was the performance. "Early admission. Juilliard. Full scholarship."</p>
<p>"Congratulations," Elizabeth said.</p>
<p>"Thank you," Jackie said.</p>
<p>Dr. Marsh set down his wine glass. The base touched the mahogany and made a small definite sound. "We're proud, Jackson." He cut a piece of the chicken with the same clean motion he'd used for twenty years. "I assume you'll keep with Mr. Holloway through graduation. No reason to let the hands go soft before fall."</p>
<p>"Of course."</p>
<p>"The Jordan Hall recital is April twentieth. The program coordinator telephoned on Thursday. They want the Nocturne in E-flat. Opus nine, number two. The one you took to Tanglewood."</p>
<p>Jackie put his fork down. The capers moved in their sauce.</p>
<p>April twentieth was sixteen days out. The Battle of the Bands at Lincoln-Sudbury was May sixteenth — five weeks after the recital, five weeks of Chopin in the cathedral acoustic of Jordan Hall and then Freddie Mercury's gospel choir stacked over A-flat. He could feel both pieces in different parts of his body. The Chopin was in the first and fourth fingers of his left hand. The Queen was in his throat.</p>
<p>"I can do both," Jackie said.</p>
<p>"Do both."</p>
<p>"The recital and the competition."</p>
<p>Dr. Marsh looked at him. Not unkindly. That was the thing — the look never went unkind. "The recital draws reviewers from the Globe, Jackson. Faculty from the Conservatory. That is a career. The other — "</p>
<p>"Is also real."</p>
<p>The words came out faster than he'd meant them. The word *real* sat in the air with the capers and the lemon and the tick of the clock in the hall.</p>
<p>Jackie watched his father's hand go to the wine glass.</p>
<p>Dr. Marsh's fingers closed around the stem. On the word he had not yet spoken but was about to, his first three fingertips curled in against the crystal in a shape the stem did not require — the shape of a hand laid across three strings and pressed. It lasted the breath before the word and was gone when the word came.</p>
<p>"Jackson. This is a high school competition. Sponsored by a rock station. It is beneath your level."</p>
<p>Jackie did not answer.</p>
<p>Eileen's smile did the thing it did, the tightening at the inside corners. "Sweetheart. Your father only means — "</p>
<p>"May I be excused?" Elizabeth said.</p>
<p>"Finish your beans," Eileen said.</p>
<p>Elizabeth ate three beans the way a person counts steps on a staircase she does not want to climb. Then she folded her napkin and left. Her door upstairs did not quite slam. The clock ticked.</p>
<p>"We'll discuss this later," Dr. Marsh said. "When everyone has had time."</p>
<p>"Yes, sir."</p>
<p>Jackie finished the chicken because he had been raised to finish the chicken. Under the table the first and fourth fingers of his left hand walked the Nocturne on his thigh through the denim, andante, seventy-two. His throat remembered the Queen chorus without being asked.</p>
<p>Upstairs, the hallway carpet deadened everything. His door closed behind him with the soft brass click of a door hung correctly.</p>
<p>The Henle edition of the Nocturne was open on the Steinway's music stand — E-flat major, Opus 9 No. 2, the *Andante* marking in the engraver's italic. On the shelf above the desk sat the Tanglewood trophy: a brass treble clef on walnut, *Young Artists — First Prize — Piano — 1977*. On the desk lay the Juilliard envelope. Cream laid paper. *The Juilliard School, 144 West 66th Street, New York, NY 10023*. Opened with a letter knife along the top edge, not torn.</p>
<p>Jackie sat on the bed. He did not go to the piano yet.</p>
<p>In the bottom desk drawer, under the old Curtis programs, lay a small Panasonic transistor the size of a deck of cards. The dial was taped at 104.1 with a strip of electrical tape so it would not wander off WBCN in the night. His father did not permit rock stations in the house. Jackie listened at a whisper after midnight with the radio under the pillow, and Maxanne's voice came through it low and steady and went somewhere in his head and stayed.</p>
<p>A knock on the door. "It's me."</p>
<p>"Come in."</p>
<p>Elizabeth was thirteen and small for it. She wore her plaid uniform skirt and one sock to the knee and one sock rolled at the ankle, the asymmetry absolutely deliberate. She sat on the bed cross-legged. Her thumbnail found a loose thread in the chenille bedspread and began to work at it — the slow careful plucking their mother did at the pearls at her throat during the Globe crossword.</p>
<p>"You're going to do the Battle of the Bands anyway," she said.</p>
<p>"Yeah."</p>
<p>"Even though he'll lose his mind."</p>
<p>"Yeah."</p>
<p>Her thumbnail kept at the thread. "Is it because of Nick Johnson?"</p>
<p>Jackie's hand went still on the bedspread. The Juilliard envelope shifted half an inch under his elbow, paper whispering against paper. A small physical thing. He felt it without moving.</p>
<p>"It's complicated," he said.</p>
<p>"You guys used to be best friends."</p>
<p>"Yeah."</p>
<p>"What happened? I always wanted to know but I was afraid to ask."</p>
<p>Jackie looked past her at the window. Maple Street at dusk. The streetlights were coming on in sequence — the Caldwells', then the Petersons', then the Schiffs', each one striking a second behind the last, a small orderly arpeggio of sodium glow down a row of hedges clipped to the same height.</p>
<p>"We grew up," Jackie said. "People change."</p>
<p>"That's what adults say when they don't want to explain things."</p>
<p>The corner of his mouth moved. A breath caught and came out as nothing.</p>
<p>"Yeah," he said. "It is."</p>
<p>Elizabeth pulled the loose thread another eighth of an inch. She did not look at him. The thread came free in her thumbnail and she rolled it into a small tight bead between finger and thumb.</p>
<p>"I heard you practicing last night," she said. "After they went to bed. That Queen song. The one."</p>
<p>"'Somebody to Love.'"</p>
<p>"You sounded different."</p>
<p>"Different how."</p>
<p>She thought about it. She thought about it a long time. The clock downstairs kept its andante. The sodium lamps on Maple Street came on in their order.</p>
<p>"Like you actually cared about the notes," she said, "instead of just hitting them right."</p>
<p>The silence held. Jackie did not fill it. Elizabeth did not fill it. The Juilliard envelope lay under his elbow with its Manhattan address pressed into the paper. Neither of them broke it.</p>
<p>"Don't tell Dad," Jackie said at last.</p>
<p>"Obviously."</p>
<p>She stayed a minute longer. Then she got up and went to the door with her one sock up and her one sock down, and she paused with her hand on the knob.</p>
<p>"Jackie. I hope you win the other one. Not because of Juilliard. Because last night you sounded like a person."</p>
<p>The door closed behind her.</p>
<p>Jackie sat where he was until the hallway carpet stopped conducting her footsteps. Then he went to the desk.</p>
<p>The Curtis talent-show photograph stood in a small silver frame next to the lamp. May 1975. Two thirteen-year-old boys on the auditorium stage — Jackie in a clip-on tie, Nick in a flannel shirt with the cuffs rolled — a plywood trophy held between them at chest height, their free arms around each other's shoulders. Nick's grin had a tooth missing. They had figured out, in that photograph, the only thing either of them had ever figured out.</p>
<p>He held it a long time. The desk lamp threw his own seventeen-year-old reflection faintly on the glass over the thirteen-year-old grin.</p>
<p>He opened the bottom desk drawer, laid the photograph face down on the stack of old programs, and closed the drawer.</p>
<p>The house was quiet. The piano was not playing itself.</p>
<p>Jackie crossed to the Steinway and sat and set the Henle edition square on the stand and put his hands on the keys. The fingers found the E-flat chord at the top of the page without being asked, the left hand low and open, the right lifting for the andante. His throat, on its own, remembered the Queen chorus and did not sing it.</p>
<p>He began to play the Chopin.</p>
<p>Downstairs, Eileen Marsh went into the small back bedroom she had not invited anyone into since 1966 and closed the door behind her.</p>
<p>The room had a single north window and a floor lamp with a cracked ceramic base she had meant to replace for eleven years. Along the far wall, a deep utility shelf held four canvases turned to the wall, the backs of their stretcher bars the only thing a visitor could have seen if there had been visitors. The topmost was dated in pencil on its stretcher, *9/23/65*. Beside the shelf stood her old easel, the oil stains on the bar the color of winter sunset under forty years of skin cells and dust. Her palette knife lay where she had put it down on the kitchen of a third-floor Huntington Avenue walk-up in the fall of 1959, a week before the acceptance letter from Richard's department chair changed her address for good.</p>
<p>She did not open a canvas. She did not touch the knife. She stood in the middle of the room with her arms loose at her sides and she looked at all of it the way a widow looks at her dead husband's coat closet — an inventory of a life she had agreed to put down, conducted in private so the agreement would hold.</p>
<p>Through the door and up the stairs, the Chopin came to her in fragments. The E-flat opening. The lift into the andante. A measure Jackie played twice. Then a third time. Then the return. When he played the first phrase of anything lyrical her right hand lifted involuntarily to her sternum as if to press a brush back against a canvas that was not there.</p>
<p>She crossed to the easel and stood in front of it. She laid her right hand flat on the wood of the bar where the oil stains were, and the hand remembered. It did the small unconscious thing a hand does when it has held a brush for five hundred hours — the thumb lifted, the first two fingertips curled, the pinky steadied against the heel of the palm — and for half a breath she was twenty-two, standing in a narrow Cambridge kitchen with a canvas on the sink counter and the afternoon light coming sideways across a still life she would never finish. The hand stayed there. It did not move.</p>
<p>From the study across the downstairs hall she heard the particular sound Richard made when he was trying to get up from his chair and the chair was not cooperating — the soft grunt, the scrape of the leg against the floorboard, the pause, the second attempt. He would call her in a moment. He did not like being heard calling her; the call would be quiet.</p>
<p>"Eileen?"</p>
<p>She did not answer immediately. The hand was still on the bar. She looked at the four canvases turned to the wall and she looked at the palette knife and she looked at the easel her hand was flat against, and then she lifted the hand away with the small care a person uses laying down something that did not belong to her in the first place. She turned off the floor lamp. She left the room and closed the door behind her, and her footsteps across the hall were even and unhurried, the footsteps of a woman on her way to the study.</p>
<p>"I'm here," she said. "I'm coming."</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 21: "ANOTHER BRICK IN THE WALL"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>By the last week of April the town had made up its mind without anyone calling a vote.</p>
<p>Grace Johnson felt it first at the Star Market on Route 20 with a ten-pound sack of Maine potatoes in the crook of her arm. The plastic netting was cutting a red line across the inside of her elbow and she was waiting her turn at register three. The cashier — Donna, the daughter of the older Donna who had worked this same register since Grace was a girl — rang the potatoes through without looking at them.</p>
<p>"Nick nervous?"</p>
<p>"He's fine."</p>
<p>"My Tommy says the whole school's picking sides."</p>
<p>"Tommy's a sophomore."</p>
<p>"Still."</p>
<p>She took her change in quarters because Donna was out of ones, and on the way to the door the pharmacist behind the raised counter of the Osco lifted two fingers and called across the shampoo aisle — *tell him good luck for us, Grace* — the way he might have said it at a wake. Grace lifted two fingers back. She did not break stride.</p>
<p>She put the potatoes in the back of the wagon and stood for a second with her hand on the tailgate, looking at the parking lot. A boy maybe nine years old was pushing a line of carts toward the corral. Two old men were talking by the ice machine. Nothing was happening. Everything was happening.</p>
<p>Tuesday was her one day a week off from Friendly's and she sat at the Sudbury Diner on Route 20 the way she had sat there every Tuesday for six years, in the back booth where the vinyl was split along the seam and the crack had been mended with a piece of black electrical tape. The Globe was open in front of her. The Red Sox had lost to the Yankees again.</p>
<p>The voices came over the top of the newsprint.</p>
<p>*— real talent versus noise —*</p>
<p>*— Marsh boy's had lessons since he was six —*</p>
<p>*— heart doesn't win anything —*</p>
<p>She did not look up. She turned the page. A Bradlees ad for lawn chairs.</p>
<p>"Grace."</p>
<p>Mary Murphy sat down across from her without asking. Mary's husband drove for the post office and had a brown Chevy Impala with a dented right-rear quarter panel where he'd backed it into the mailbox at the Fairbank Road turnaround in March and never gotten it fixed, and you could see the car from the diner window right now, the dent catching the late sun. Their son Stephen played bass in Nick's band.</p>
<p>"He holding up?"</p>
<p>"He's fine."</p>
<p>"Stephen says they're in the garage six hours a night."</p>
<p>"Something like that."</p>
<p>Mary leaned in. Her voice dropped half an inch. "Frank at the barber shop's got a pool going."</p>
<p>"On what."</p>
<p>"On Nick."</p>
<p>"Jesus, Mary."</p>
<p>"My Dennis put ten dollars down."</p>
<p>The waitress came by with the pot. The button over her apron pocket was a white circle with red letters — **WBCN 104.1 — THE ROCK OF BOSTON** — with the peace-sign logo underneath, slightly crooked where the pin had gone in a dozen times. She topped Grace's cup without asking, topped Mary's, moved on to the booth behind.</p>
<p>"The Weekly ran it again yesterday," Mary said. "Front page."</p>
<p>Grace had seen it. The photograph was Nick in the garage, three-quarters to the light from the open door, Dan's tools outlined in black marker on the pegboard behind him and the 1975 Aerosmith calendar hanging exactly where it had hung for four years. *BATTLE OF THE BANDS: TWO STUDENTS, TWO WORLDS, ONE STAGE.* Sponsored in part, the last paragraph said, by WBCN-FM. The reporter had called three times before Grace picked up. She had given him seven sentences.</p>
<p>"Dr. Marsh didn't like it," Mary said. "My neighbor works in the office. He called the school. Said they were turning a talent show into class warfare." Mary put quotes around *class warfare* with two fingers on each hand.</p>
<p>Grace sipped her coffee.</p>
<p>"There's a PTA meeting Thursday night. Emergency session. He's going to try to cancel it."</p>
<p>Grace set the cup down.</p>
<p>"Cancel it."</p>
<p>"Postpone until fall. For the kids' own good. You know the song."</p>
<p>"The Battle's in two weeks."</p>
<p>"I know."</p>
<p>Grace looked across the booth at Mary Murphy. Fifteen years they had lived three streets apart. Fifteen years of Little League and parent-teacher nights and hand-me-down snowsuits. Mary's house was the same house as Grace's house — the same square of chain-link, the same sagging gutter over the back door — paid for one month at a time.</p>
<p>"I work Thursday," Grace said.</p>
<p>"Get it off."</p>
<p>"I can't."</p>
<p>"Grace."</p>
<p>"I'll get it off."</p>
<p>Thursday the Lincoln-Sudbury library smelled the way it had smelled in 1953 — lemon oil the librarian rubbed into the oak shelves every morning before the doors opened, varnish gone amber on the long card catalog by the reference desk, paper and dust and the faint electric warmth of the fluorescent tubes overhead. Grace had checked out a copy of *The Yearling* from these shelves in the spring she turned fifteen. She had forgotten until she stepped through the door and the smell came up and found her.</p>
<p>She did not think about it for long. She did not have time.</p>
<p>The room was full. The good oak chairs filled first, in rows facing the librarian's desk; then the metal folding chairs along the walls, scraped out of the supply closet; then parents standing in the doorway. Someone's baby was fussing at the back. The PA system crackled once — a cough of static — and went quiet. Principal Sargent sat at the front with his hands flat on a legal pad, the expression of a man who had not asked for this.</p>
<p>Grace came straight from work. She stood along the side wall next to a radiator that was ticking. Brown polyester uniform. *GRACE — MANAGER* over her left breast. The smell of fryer oil was on her whether she wanted it to be or not.</p>
<p>Dr. Marsh sat in the front row with his wife. Blazer, gray slacks, the easy posture of a man who had sat on a dozen nonprofit boards and knew how long to wait before raising a hand. He raised it.</p>
<p>"Thank you, Brad." He used the first name the way a man uses a key he has been given. "I think we all want the same thing. And I think we all know that this — competition — has become something none of us signed up for. Students skipping class. Arguments in the hallway. A newspaper poll asking seventeen-year-olds to pick a side as if this were a selectman's race."</p>
<p>Around the room, heads moved. A woman two chairs over pressed her lips together and nodded once. A man in a Red Sox windbreaker folded his arms across his chest.</p>
<p>"What troubles me most," Dr. Marsh said, "is that we have let a talent show turn into a referendum. On what, I'll let each of you decide. I am asking this board to postpone the Battle of the Bands until the fall, when cooler heads —"</p>
<p>Grace said, "Excuse me."</p>
<p>Her voice was not loud. It carried because the room had gone still for it.</p>
<p>Principal Sargent turned his head. "Mrs. Johnson."</p>
<p>She walked down the center aisle between the oak chairs. She was conscious of her sneakers on the waxed floor and the fryer smell coming off her uniform and the fact that she had not had a sentence ready when she left her car. She stopped at the front. She did not go behind the little lectern Sargent had set up. She put her right hand on the back of the nearest oak chair and gripped it. Her knuckles went white around the wood.</p>
<p>Dr. Marsh, half-risen, sat back down. His right hand lifted and moved to his left breast pocket as if to take something out of it. The pocket was empty. His hand rested there a second and then came down to his knee.</p>
<p>"I'm Grace Johnson," Grace said. "Nick's mother. I work at Friendly's. I worked a double today."</p>
<p>She stopped. The coffee she had drunk in the car an hour ago had gone cold in her mouth and she worked it down once before she went on.</p>
<p>"Dr. Marsh is right. This stopped being about a talent show. Our kids didn't do that. We did."</p>
<p>A chair creaked at the back.</p>
<p>"My son plays a twenty-five-dollar guitar in a garage his father built. His father is dead. He works Saturdays at a body shop for strings. Two weeks from now he gets to stand on a stage and play for five hundred dollars and a day in a studio. That's not nothing. That's the whole thing."</p>
<p>She looked at Dr. Marsh.</p>
<p>"You want to postpone it until fall. Fall is when Jackie goes to conservatory. Fall is when my son is fixing cars."</p>
<p>Someone in the metal folding chairs said, quiet, *that's right.*</p>
<p>Grace's hand was still on the oak chair. She could feel the grain under her thumb. The baby at the back had stopped fussing.</p>
<p>"Let them play," she said. "Win or lose. Let them play."</p>
<p>She walked down the aisle and out the double doors and into the parking lot. The sodium lights had come on. Her hands were shaking by the time she got the key into the door of the wagon, and she sat behind the wheel with both hands on it for a minute before she turned the ignition.</p>
<p>She drove home down Route 20 past the lit sign of the Star Market and the dark sign of the feed store, and the speedometer needle sat an even five miles over the limit the whole way, because that was the speed she drove.</p>
<p>Mary called at ten-forty.</p>
<p>"Forty-two to eleven."</p>
<p>"Alright."</p>
<p>"Grace. You should've seen his face."</p>
<p>"I'm glad."</p>
<p>"Brad called the vote five minutes after you walked out. Wasn't even close."</p>
<p>"Alright, Mary."</p>
<p>"That was the bravest —"</p>
<p>"I have to be there at five-forty."</p>
<p>A pause on the line.</p>
<p>"Get some sleep," Mary said.</p>
<p>"You too."</p>
<p>Grace set the receiver back in the cradle of the rotary phone on the kitchen wall, and the small internal click of the hanger-bar dropping into place was the last sound in the house. The kitchen clock ticked. Upstairs the garage amp was off. She stood with her hand still on the receiver for a second, then she turned off the kitchen light and went up the stairs in the dark the way she had gone up them ten thousand times, her right hand on the banister where the varnish had worn thin to the grain under three generations of Johnson hands and her own.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 22: "WON'T GET FOOLED AGAIN"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Two hours and eighteen minutes.</p>
<p>Grace Johnson just walked out of a PTA meeting in her Friendly's uniform and told a room full of professionals what they didn't want to hear. Forty-two to eleven. I won't replay her speech — you heard what oak sounds like when someone grips it hard enough.</p>
<p>What you haven't heard yet is how the Battle got its rules. That happened earlier — January 1979, third floor above Studio B, a man in Italian shoes and a portfolio full of charts. Not three years before. Not with two boys' names in my pocket. I didn't know their names yet.</p>
<p>The ON AIR sign pulses. The engineer behind the glass has headphones on and eyes on his phone. I have a paper cup of coffee gone cold and the legal pad photocopy in the folder Tom Andrews filed under *L-S Battle — '79* and forgot about until I asked for it last week.</p>
<p>Won't get fooled again. Pete Townshend wrote that for people who keep signing the same contract with different ink. I signed mine in January. The Battle was still four months out.</p>
<p>WBCN's management office sat on the third floor above the studios — a room that had once been somebody's idea of cool and had aged into somebody's idea of a health code violation. Bruise-colored carpet. Gold records on the walls at angles that suggested whoever mounted them had been in a hurry or half in the bag. Aerosmith. J. Geils. Boston. The Cars.</p>
<p>The conference table was pressboard buckled in the middle, ring-stained from ten years of arguments about what rock and roll owed its audience.</p>
<p>Ten AM. January 1979.</p>
<p>Charlie Morris sat at the head — station manager, joint-smoker, believer in letting DJs run free. To his right: Gerald Kowalski. New Program Director, six weeks in from New York. Late thirties, suit in a building where nobody wore suits, leather portfolio the color of money, shoes polished to a funhouse shine.</p>
<p>Tom Andrews sat across from me. Tom — at WBCN since '71, who'd broken J. Geils when Peter Wolf was still his overnight roommate. Tom caught my eye. The smallest nod. *Hang on.*</p>
<p>"Let's talk about the future," Kowalski said.</p>
<p>Charts. Graphs. Demographic columns — 18-24 down twelve percent, automotive sponsors nervous, Clear Channel swallowing independents like pythons swallowing rabbits.</p>
<p>"We need the 18-34 demo," he said. "Or we lose the dollars that keep this station independent."</p>
<p>Charlie tapped his pen. "Gerald has a proposal."</p>
<p>Kowalski spread a single sheet. **WBCN COMMUNITY ENGAGEMENT INITIATIVE — BATTLE OF THE BANDS.**</p>
<p>"Regional high school competition. We brand it. Lincoln-Sudbury Regional to start — their music teacher, Bob Wentworth, is already enthusiastic. Spring '79. Winner gets five hundred dollars and a recording session. Sponsorship packages — Coca-Cola, local dealerships. Repeatable every spring."</p>
<p>Silence. Four seconds.</p>
<p>"Annual franchise," Tom Andrews said. Not a question.</p>
<p>"The data supports it," Kowalski said. "WJLB in Detroit. KROQ in LA. Fourteen percent lift in target demos within two rating periods."</p>
<p>I looked at Tom. In that look: the fork in the road.</p>
<p>"What if we use it to actually find talent?" I said. "Not a brand exercise. Discovery. We send DJs who know the difference between a kid who can play and a kid who can make you feel something."</p>
<p>Tom leaned forward. "If we put our name on it, it has to be authentic. WBCN discovers talent. That's the brand."</p>
<p>Kowalski watched us the way a chess player watches an opponent move where he wants them. "I agree completely. Discovery. Authenticity. That's the narrative we sell."</p>
<p>When the man in the suit agrees with the rebels, the rebels just walked into the cage.</p>
<p>"First Battle — May sixteenth," he said. "Lincoln-Sudbury. Maxanne, I want you to judge. You're the face of WBCN discovery."</p>
<p>"I'll judge," I said. "But I'm writing the rules with Tom."</p>
<p>"Of course. Friday."</p>
<p>Kowalski gathered his portfolio and stood. At the door he paused.</p>
<p>"One more thing. This needs to work. First time, every time. The sponsors are watching." He smiled. "Make it pay."</p>
<p>He said it the way you'd tell a vending machine to deliver the soda after you've already put in the coins.</p>
<p>The door closed. Charlie lit a joint. Tom rubbed his eyes.</p>
<p>"If we write the rules," Tom said. "If we keep the sponsors at arm's length and the kids at the center."</p>
<p>"Then we write the rules," I said.</p>
<p>That night. Brookline apartment. Kitchen table. Legal pad. Red wine — one glass, not a bottle.</p>
<p>Entry requirements. Judging criteria — originality and emotional connection weighted double. Prize: five hundred dollars and a Studio C session for the winner.</p>
<p>Article 7, Section 3: *In the event of a tie in the final round, tied contestants shall share the studio recording session equally, collaborating on material of their choosing.*</p>
<p>I read it back. Boring. The kind of line sponsors like — cooperation, youth, no ugly single winner on a press release. Ties happen in contests. I'd seen them. I'd also seen what happens when two kids from opposite sides of a brook land on the same stage and the room wants a victor. The clause was in the closet with everything else — class, spectacle, the machinery that turns music into content. I wasn't thinking about two names. I didn't have two names yet. I was thinking about *make it pay* and a station that needed to survive.</p>
<p>I added the line. Initialled the page. Filed the pad.</p>
<p>I did not know I was writing the door we'd walk through in May. I knew I'd signed another contract with different ink.</p>
<p>Two hours and eleven minutes.</p>
<p>The photocopy sits in the folder. Article 7, Section 3 in Times New Roman, third page, between judging weights and liability waiver.</p>
<p>I don't play Queen in the booth — not yet. I cue nothing. I let the dead air sit three seconds, the way we used to before a track we believed in.</p>
<p>"That's the meeting," I say. "January. Not '76. Kowalski and his charts. Me thinking I could keep it real."</p>
<p>I glance at the cassette on the console. I don't pick it up.</p>
<p>"The tie language was one boring line. Standard packet stuff. I wasn't aiming it at Nick Johnson or Jackie Marsh — I hadn't met them. What I did was worse and smaller: I helped build a game where a tie could be useful. Where nobody had to lose ugly in front of a town that was already picking sides."</p>
<p>The engineer looks up. Dead air on the meter. He doesn't say anything.</p>
<p>"In May I'll tell you what I did with that line when it mattered. Tonight I'm telling you where it came from."</p>
<p>I lean into the microphone.</p>
<p>"The Battle is six weeks out. Grace just won her vote. The boys are in their garages. And the rules are already on the copier at Lincoln-Sudbury, third page down, the one nobody reads until they need an exit."</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 23: "WORKING CLASS HERO"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Saturday morning, May 1979. Eight days to the Battle.</p>
<p>Grace Johnson carried the two pots the way she had carried them for twenty-three years: regular in her right hand, orange collar on the glass; decaf in her left, brown collar. Dr. Taub drank decaf. Had drunk decaf since 1956, the year she started and he was still new to his practice, thirty-two and just back from Korea with his wire-framed glasses and a cardigan his wife had knit him and a bedside manner he had not yet grown into. Grace went to his table with the brown-collared pot already tilted, because at Friendly's on Route 20 you did not ask Dr. Taub which coffee he wanted. You poured the decaf and you said good morning, Dr. Taub, and you did not stand any longer at his booth than the pour required.</p>
<p>Booth twelve was the booth by the window. The window looked out on the parking lot and the Boston Post Road traffic going east toward Wayland, and the Impala in the second row and Grace's wood-paneled '67 station wagon in the first. Above her, the fluorescent flickered the way it had flickered since Tom Andrews had taken over in '71 and failed to fix it. The ice cream case against the back wall hummed through its blue light. MINT CHOCOLATE CHIP, the little cardboard flag said in its Friendly's-green script, stuck into a tub gone frost-bristled at the edges. At booth seven a kid was working on a Jim Dandy with a long spoon. A booth down from him, two cops split a Fishamajig basket. The air was what the air at Friendly's always was — fryer oil laid over coffee laid over the bleach on the rag in her apron pocket.</p>
<p>Behind the counter Alma was working the ice-cream end. Every scoop the same motion — dip the scoop in the jar of hot water by the tub, shake once, cut the ice cream flat across the top, roll, plate. The scoop went back into the hot water with a small ringing clink against the glass, the sound the morning had every forty seconds, steady as a metronome. Over the pass-through Tom had put up the Saturday chalkboard: TUNA MELT $1.55 / FRIBBLE $0.85. The chalk had been rewritten enough times that the green board showed through in the loops of the Fs. Grace's apron pocket held a ticket book, two pens, a quarter-roll of Life Savers gone soft in the wrapper, and the wet rag. It rode against her hip. She did not notice it. She had stopped noticing it in 1961.</p>
<p>Dr. Taub had the Sudbury Weekly folded open to page three. The picture on page three was Nick, head down over the Les Paul copy in the music-room shot they had run with the WBCN story — the same shot that had been on the newsbrief last week when the PTA vote went statewide for eleven seconds. Grace's speech at the library had made page four. Dr. Taub had not mentioned it.</p>
<p>He was seventy now. The suit jacket hung over the back of the booth. Under it he wore the same kind of cardigan he had worn when she was twelve and his wife had been Ruth Lindstrom, in the L-S class of '57 two years behind her, and the two of them had sat at the same lunch table in a cafeteria that did not exist anymore. He did not remember that. He had never needed to.</p>
<p>The man across the booth from him was another doctor — younger, maybe fifty, from the Emerson group — asking about the paper in his hand. Grace was at booth eleven with the orange-collared pot, topping the cup of an old man in a Wayland Country Club windbreaker who did not look up.</p>
<p>"Came in on the train from Boston in '56," Dr. Taub was saying, over her shoulder, not to her. "People were still talking then. Some of these families — you'd be surprised. That's all I'll say."</p>
<p>She did not turn her head. She poured the regular. She said thank you to the Country Club man, who had not said anything to thank her for, and walked back to the counter, past the ice cream case and the chalkboard and the hot-water jar that rang once again as the scoop went in. On the shelf under the register, next to the box of straws, the frosted glass jar of Maxwell House instant sat where it had sat every day she had worked here — squat, shouldered, a little amber through the frost — and for half a breath she was eight years old in a doorway on the northwest side of town watching a man close a lid on a sideboard jar and look up and smile before he remembered he was supposed to be working.</p>
<p>She took the decaf to booth twelve.</p>
<p>"Grace," Dr. Taub said. He did not look up from the paper. "I've been reading about this Battle."</p>
<p>"Yes, Dr. Taub."</p>
<p>"I worry about these boys. Yours. The Marsh boy." He touched the photograph with his index finger, the way he touched a chart. "The competition sets them up. Expectations. Talent alone — you know — it takes training. Connections. Most of these kids are going to be disappointed."</p>
<p>There was a small brown stain on the lapel of his cardigan he had not noticed. Coffee, or something coffee-adjacent, from a morning older than today. She noticed it.</p>
<p>"Disappointed," she said.</p>
<p>"I delivered that boy, Grace." He said it the way a man lays down a trump. "I delivered Nick in August of sixty-one at Emerson. I'm not against him. I'm *for* realism."</p>
<p>The pot in her left hand was half full. She did not set it down. She did not slam it. She held it.</p>
<p>"My son has been playing guitar since he was eight."</p>
<p>She said it the way she would have said, *the oven's on.* The late-morning sun on the parking lot caught the chrome handle of the station wagon — a flash she swallowed around — and she did not let her voice shake.</p>
<p>"He practices every day in the garage his father built. He's won every talent show he's entered since he was nine. WBCN called my house. Not his. Mine. And you're drinking decaf in my section at eleven in the morning and telling me he's going to be disappointed."</p>
<p>Dr. Taub opened his mouth.</p>
<p>"Enjoy your coffee, Dr. Taub."</p>
<p>She moved to booth seven. Jim Dandy kid. "You need anything, honey?" He shook his head. She moved to the cops. Refilled them both. Regular. Orange collar. She did not look back at booth twelve. She did not need to. The whole dining room had gone a shade quieter, the way a pond goes quiet when something has fallen in it.</p>
<p>Tom Andrews was forty-eight and had been the Friendly's manager on Route 20 for eight of those years. His office was a cinder-block closet behind the walk-in, with a gray metal desk, a swivel chair with duct tape on the arm, a bank calendar from Middlesex Savings still turned to March. He had grown up on Horse Pond Road. He had poured concrete on the same crew as Dan Johnson for two summers in the early sixties, and in the summer of '66 he and Dan and three other guys had spent a Saturday on the Friendly's back roof with buckets of hot tar for cash-in-the-envelope off the then-manager, the same roof over his head right now. He had been at the funeral.</p>
<p>"Sit down, Grace."</p>
<p>"I'll stand, Tom."</p>
<p>"Sit down."</p>
<p>She sat.</p>
<p>He did not look at her while he said the sentences his job required him to say. Professionalism. Customer relations. The uniform meaning something. The word *uniform* was doing work in his mouth that the word was not built for. He finished. He set both hands flat on the desk.</p>
<p>"Fifteen more cents an hour."</p>
<p>"Thanks, Tom."</p>
<p>"Get back to work, Grace."</p>
<p>"Yes, sir."</p>
<p>She stood. She untied and retied the apron. Her name tag had come loose on the left pin; she straightened it. GRACE, in the raised plastic letters, and under it MANAGER, and the small gold foil star the company had sent in February. She went back out to the floor.</p>
<p>He came in at four-ten, through the glass door with the Friendly's-cone sticker on it, and he stood by the register with his L-S jacket on and his hands in his pockets and his weight on his right leg. Dan had stood like that. Waiting for Grace to finish a shift, Dan had always tilted the weight off the bad knee, and Nick had never once in his seventeen years been told to copy the stance, and there it was. Grace saw it from booth four, where she was wiping syrup off a formica edge, and went still for a half-second and then kept wiping.</p>
<p>He crossed to her.</p>
<p>"Mom."</p>
<p>"It's fine, Nick."</p>
<p>"I heard."</p>
<p>"You deserve a chance."</p>
<p>"You didn't have to do that."</p>
<p>"Yes, I did."</p>
<p>She folded the rag. She did not look at him right away. When she did, his face had the Friendly's sign in the window of it — the cursive neon red reflected on the lens of his reading glasses, which were her reading glasses, which he had grabbed off the counter when he came in, and he did not know he was wearing them. She almost said so. She didn't.</p>
<p>"Wait on the bench by the door," she said. "Two minutes."</p>
<p>He waited on the bench by the door. She took the apron off in the back. She hung it on the hook that had her name taped above it in Tom's block print. She clocked out. She walked past the ice cream case with its blue light and the mint chocolate chip and the Jim Dandy sign and out through the glass door, and Nick stood up off the bench and walked out beside her, and above them the Friendly's sign in its ice-cream-cone cursive threw red over the hood of the station wagon and red over the shoulders of her polyester and red, a softer red, over the side of Nick's face.</p>
<p>They did not speak in the parking lot. He opened the driver's side for her the way Dan used to. She got in. He walked around to the passenger side. For a moment, before he opened his door, he stood in the lot with his hand on the roof of the wagon and looked back at the diner where his mother had just, without once saying the word, defended him in front of the doctor who had delivered him and the town that had watched her serve coffee for twenty-three years.</p>
<p>Then he got in, and she started the engine, and they went home up Route 20 with the red sign fading in the rearview and the spring light going long over the Cavicchio fields.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 24: "LIGHT MY FIRE"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Tuesday afternoon, May 1979. Six days before the Battle.</p>
<p>Bob Wentworth had the jazz band in a horseshoe — seventeen kids on folding chairs in the cinder-block room off the east wing, ballast buzz in the fluorescents, dust motes turning in the late light that came slantwise through the one high window. The posters on the wall were a small argument he had been having with himself since 1962. Ellington at the Blue Note. Miles in shadow, the Harmon mute at his lips. Bernstein with his baton down.</p>
<p>The Yamaha upright stood against the long wall, lid open, middle C pitted where ten years of middle-school hands had found it first. Nick's Marshall practice amp sat on a chair by the piano, not plugged in yet, the cord coiled on top with the precision of a boy who owned one cord. Music stands at seventeen different heights. A broken maraca on the shelf above the tuba case, the handle snapped off at the bead, which Wentworth kept for reasons he could not articulate. The room smelled of old valve oil and floor wax.</p>
<p>He had the photograph in his drawer. He had been thinking about it all period.</p>
<p>"Put the scales away," he said. He set a stapled two-page chart on each of their music stands. "Brubeck. 'Take Five.' Nick, Jackie — just you two. The rest of you listen."</p>
<p>Seventeen heads came up.</p>
<p>Nick was by the amp, not looking at the piano. Jackie was already on the bench, not looking at the amp. They had not spoken, Wentworth knew, since October. Two Aprils of careful air between them in a building with two hundred and forty feet of corridor.</p>
<p>"Mr. Wentworth," Nick said. He had not picked up the guitar. "I don't think —"</p>
<p>"Five-four," Wentworth said. "One, two, three, four, five. The off-balance is the point. You lean into it. Just see."</p>
<p>Jackie put his hands on the keys. Nick, because there was nowhere else for his hands to go, plugged in.</p>
<p>The left hand came first. D, E-flat, F — ostinato, walking — the Brubeck bass figure the way the record had it, Jackie's fingers finding the shape clean on the first pass because classical training had given him the 5/4 the way it had given him everything, for free. The ostinato settled into the room like a heartbeat that had decided to come in a pattern nobody's body expected. One, two, three, four, five. One, two, three, four, five.</p>
<p>Nick listened for four bars. He found the crack between the four and the five and he put a bent blue note in it. Low on the neck. Not loud. The string was slightly flat and he let it be flat. Another bar of listening, and then he threaded the second phrase, longer, letting the time signature lean him sideways and catching himself on a chord — not a Brubeck chord, an Aerosmith chord — and it sat down in Jackie's ostinato like it had been waiting there.</p>
<p>Jackie's right hand came in above it. Not flourish — restraint. A single high note held, then a run, then held again. He was leaving Nick room.</p>
<p>Nick stepped into the room Jackie left him.</p>
<p>For thirty seconds it was a working thing. The off-balance found its center the way a bicycle finds its center, not by stopping the wobble but by leaning into it. In the horseshoe one kid — Brian Castellano, second chair trumpet — stopped chewing his gum and his jaw hung half-open and he did not remember to close it. Near the back, Patty Lally had been humming something under her breath the whole period — a Fleetwood Mac melody she didn't know she was humming — and the hum went now, quiet, underneath the 5/4 like a third voice the room had not been asked to provide. She stopped.</p>
<p>Wentworth did not move. He was not sure he was breathing. The ballast buzz was the only thing in the building that did not know what was happening in the room.</p>
<p>The door whooshed on its hydraulic hinge.</p>
<p>Don Gould came in the way Don Gould came in — Northeastern coffee mug in the left hand, jeans, sleeves rolled to the elbow, wool tie loose at the collar, the AP American History grin he wore on days he thought he was going to teach somebody something. He stopped in the doorway long enough to register the music, which was still happening, the ostinato walking under him, and then he smiled wider.</p>
<p>Patty's hum stopped.</p>
<p>"You know what this is?" Gould said, loud, over the music that was still going. "This is the Lincoln-Douglas debates, right here."</p>
<p>Nick's hand came off the strings.</p>
<p>Jackie's ostinato carried one more bar on its own, alone, the D-E-flat-F figure with nothing on top of it, and then Jackie's left hand stopped too, the last note hanging and dying into the ballast buzz.</p>
<p>Nick set his pick down on the amp. He set it down carefully, because if he had not set it down carefully he would have thrown it.</p>
<p>"We were playing music," he said.</p>
<p>Gould was still smiling. "Right, but — the tension, Nick, the tension between —"</p>
<p>"Between you and us." Nick stood. The strap caught on the music stand; the stand went over; the Brubeck chart went under a chair. He didn't pick it up. "You want a lesson? My dad said *these boys* on a bridge in October. Your side of town. My side of town. You just did the same thing with a metaphor."</p>
<p>Gould's smile did a thing. It did not go away. It got confused at the edges.</p>
<p>Nick was unplugging the cord now. His hands knew the cord. His hands did not need his face to do it. His jaw was tight enough that the inside of his cheek had cut against a molar again — the small bright copper of his own blood, the same taste as the dirt bikes on the plank bridge.</p>
<p>"Nick." Jackie's voice was small. He had not stood up. His hands had come off the keyboard and were flat on his thighs.</p>
<p>"What."</p>
<p>"Don't."</p>
<p>"Don't what."</p>
<p>Jackie's hands came off his thighs and went back to the keys and did not press anything. The fluorescent buzzed. One of the tubes up near the door was going; it ticked twice, a small electric tick, and then buzzed again.</p>
<p>"I was trying to avoid this." Jackie's voice had gone quieter, not louder. The precision was starting to crack at the edges the way it did when he was tired. "I thought maybe we could get through one week without —"</p>
<p>"Without you stepping aside." Nick coiled the cord. "Yeah. I got it."</p>
<p>"Slumming." Jackie said the word like it was a small heavy object somebody had handed him. He did not look up.</p>
<p>He stood. The piano bench scraped on the tile — oak on waxed linoleum, loud because the room had gone so quiet. He shut the fallboard. He did not slam it. He set it down the way his mother set a china cup down.</p>
<p>"See you Friday," Jackie said.</p>
<p>That was all. He walked out. The door whooshed on its hinge and closed on the soft seal.</p>
<p>Nick was still holding the coiled cord.</p>
<p>Wentworth got them out of the room. He did not remember, afterward, what he said to do it — some sentence with the word *dismissed* in it, probably — but the seventeen chairs emptied, folders under arms, and the last kid out was Patty Lally, who paused at the door with her canvas bag on her shoulder and looked back once at the piano bench and then was gone.</p>
<p>The door closed on its seal.</p>
<p>The ballast buzz was still the only sound.</p>
<p>Wentworth walked past the Yamaha, past Nick's amp with its coiled cord, past the Brubeck chart under the chair, and went around behind the piano to the stand where the trumpet had been since September, in its black case, and had not been out of its case in fourteen months. He opened the case. The felt lining had gone bald at the bell end. The valve oil bottle on the inside of the lid was three-quarters down, where he had left it.</p>
<p>He took the horn out. He set the mouthpiece in. The mouthpiece was cold. He wet his lips. He raised the horn. The ring on the bell said *Bach Stradivarius 37* in the same small script it had said it in at Jordan Hall in March of 1958.</p>
<p>He blew one note.</p>
<p>It was not a good note. It was an F, and the F sagged under his breath because the embouchure had gone and the lip had gone and the air had gone, and the note came out of the bell and went nowhere. Nobody heard it. The room ate it.</p>
<p>He set the horn back in the case. He closed the case.</p>
<p>He sat down on the piano bench Jackie had left.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 25: "NIGHT MOVES"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Tuesday night, the fifteenth of May, 1979. The night before the Battle.</p>
<p>The garage bay door stood up to the chest-high stop, lifted halfway for the air — sixty-five degrees if it was a degree, the kind of spring night that felt warm only because April had set the bar so low. Crickets had started in the grass by the oil stain. Behind the Cavicchio fence a spring peeper was going in the wet low place, one note at a time, not fast. A Chevy with a bad belt whined down Dutton Road and faded toward the Horse Pond light.</p>
<p>Inside, the air was forty watts worse than outside. One bare bulb on a length of exposed Romex swung a finger's width when a kid walked under it. The Marshall practice amp on the milk crate was warm enough to put a palm on. The guitar case was open on the concrete, cables heaped in it like rope in a dinghy.</p>
<p>Nick stood in the middle of it with the twenty-five-dollar acoustic he'd walked out of a pawn shop in Framingham the summer he was fourteen — Dan still around for one more year, the price Dan had paid because he'd said *don't tell your mother* and then told her at dinner that night himself. The guitar had a crack in the upper bout Nick had packed with wood glue the summer he was fifteen. His fingertips knew every raised grain on the neck.</p>
<p>Stephen Murphy had his Univox Hi-Flyer plugged into a little Kustom 2x12 borrowed off his cousin in Hudson. Univox was the cheap copy of the Mosrite the real players in Boston clubs used, and Stephen had saved for it out of a paper route and three-fifty an hour at the Sunoco on 126. It was the color of a Ford brake light. He'd tuned it four times already.</p>
<p>David Cassel sat on a crate behind a Ludwig four-piece his uncle had handed down, one cracked crash riveted back together with a brass washer his father had drilled out on the basement bench. Mark Pianka had a Rhodes on loan from Mr. Wentworth — a Stage Piano out of the music-room closet that buzzed on the B below middle C if you leaned on it. Mark had figured out not to lean on it, mostly.</p>
<p>"One more time," Nick said.</p>
<p>"We've done it four times," David said.</p>
<p>"One more."</p>
