<p>They ran "Dream On." Stephen's bass locked in under Mark's keyboard figure. David's snare hit was quieter tonight than it would be tomorrow — the neighbors closer at a milk crate's remove than they'd be at the back of an auditorium. Nick sang.</p>
<p>When they finished, Stephen laid the Univox flat on his thigh.</p>
<p>"Nick."</p>
<p>"Yeah."</p>
<p>"Whatever happens tomorrow." Stephen looked at the pegboard, not at Nick. "We're with you. Not because of your mom at Friendly's. You're the reason any of us started playing."</p>
<p>Mark was wiping Rhodes-sweat off the F key. "Freshman year you were the only one who didn't laugh when I came in with my grandmother's Magnus chord organ."</p>
<p>David twirled a stick between his fingers and watched it. "Three lunches. The music room. You taught me the grip."</p>
<p>"So tomorrow," Stephen said, "we play. Screw the rest."</p>
<p>Nick nodded once.</p>
<p>"One more," he said, when he could. "Then go."</p>
<p>They ran it one more time. Then the Kustom cut and the Rhodes cut, and David laid the sticks crossed on the snare head the way a carpenter lays a level down, and they were packing up.</p>
<p>At the curb Stephen's father's '74 Nova sat idling with the headlights aimed at the garage. Mark's brother was five minutes out. David lived a quarter mile down Dutton and was walking. The three of them went out under the half-raised bay door with their cases and their cords, and at the end of the driveway Stephen said *Nick,* once, and Nick lifted two fingers off the neck of the acoustic, and Stephen got in the Nova.</p>
<p>The Nova's radio had something slow on it — piano up front, a voice that wasn't Tyler — and Stephen's father rolled the window up without changing the station and pulled away down Dutton and the headlights took the last of themselves with them.</p>
<p>The crickets came back up.</p>
<p>Grace stood on the back step in her Friendly's uniform with her hand on the storm-door handle and did not open it.</p>
<p>The laundry line ran from the porch post to the maple in the side yard and the line was empty — she had brought the sheets in at six the way she had brought them in every Thursday for twenty-three years. Across Dutton Road on the Connolly porch the orange cat sat on the railing with its tail hanging over the mailbox side. Up by the mill the Chevy with the bad belt passed again, coming back. The belt did the same thing.</p>
<p>From the open garage she could hear him finishing out. The kids were gone. It was just the one guitar now.</p>
<p>She stood with her hand on the handle until the chrome warmed. Then she took her hand off and went in.</p>
<p>The storm door shut behind her on its own hinge.</p>
<p>In the garage Nick stood alone in the forty-watt yellow.</p>
<p>His left hand hurt. The pads of his fingers had gone past sting to something that was not quite numb. The high E bit him a little every time he landed on it, and he landed on it a lot, because "Dream On" started there and the bite got worse as the night went. His grip on the neck tightened and he made himself open the hand twice and close it.</p>
<p>He bent and picked up a pick off the concrete — Fender medium, the logo worn white down one side where his thumb had been. He turned it over. The other side still had the tortoise pattern readable. He set it on the Marshall next to the one already there and stood up.</p>
<p>The toolbox sat where it had sat for eighteen months, under the 1975 April calendar, red paint chipped at the corners, latch shut. On the Maxwell House lid beside it, the 9/16ths socket. Nick glanced at the latch once. Still shut.</p>
<p>He sat on the milk crate. He put the acoustic across his lap. He worked his left hand open and shut twice — *shake it out, Nicky, don't fight it* — and laid his fingers on the neck and played the piano intro to "Dream On" on six strings of gut and wire.</p>
<p>On the record there was a piano on the intro — Tyler himself, one finger at a time, the way Nick had read it in *Circus* two summers ago on the floor of the garage. Nick played the piano part on the twenty-five-dollar acoustic because there was no piano in the garage and because it was the only way he could play it alone. His right thumb picked the melody low on the neck. His left hand fretted slow. When he came to the bend at the end of the first phrase his fingertip bit and he breathed in through his nose and did not stop.</p>
<p>Outside, the crickets had gone quiet.</p>
<p>He was in the third phrase before he noticed. The peeper had stopped too. The Chevy with the belt was gone up the road. From Route 20, faint through the pines, somebody's AM found the last bars of a slow song and lost it again between the trees.</p>
<p>He played the passage through, alone, the acoustic bright and thin against the pegboard and the bare joists and the concrete, the way Dan had bent over him one Sunday in the summer he was fourteen and guided his hand on the neck until the fingering came right. When he stopped the crickets started back, first one, then two, then the whole yard, and the peeper came in behind the Cavicchio fence on its slow cadence, and the bare sixty-watt bulb on the Romex swung the width of a finger because a moth had hit it.</p>
<p>Nick set the guitar down in the case on the tangle of cables. He closed the lid. He did not latch it. He reached up and pulled the bay door down to the concrete with both hands, slow, the way Dan had taught him, easing the springs the last six inches so the counterweight did not slam.</p>
<p>On his way to the side door he did not look at the toolbox.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 26: "FATHER AND SON"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Tuesday evening, the fifteenth of May, 1979. The same night Nick was running "Dream On" one more time on Dutton Road.</p>
<p>Jackie Marsh was alone in the basement on Maple Street when the Crown Jewels came down the side stairs — running the intro of "Bohemian Rhapsody" in B-flat because his left hand had asked for it the second he sat down.</p>
<p>The room had been finished the summer he turned fourteen. Foam panels on the walls, the two-inch studio kind — white once, now going yellow at the glued edges the way a paperback goes yellow on a south-facing shelf. A Shure SM58 stood on a boom stand at the center of the floor, black grille, the XLR running in a clean loop to the four-track against the wall — a Tascam, four little red LED indicators glowing dim as pilot lights in a kitchen at night. Against the long wall, the upright. A 1920s Baldwin, its walnut case gone plum-dark at the corners, its ivory top keys chipped in two places from a thumbnail that had been Eileen's mother's in 1931 in a parlor on Beacon Hill. The Steinway lived upstairs in the good light. The Baldwin lived down here with the amps. That had been decided early.</p>
<p>Jackie played the opening six bars and let them stop themselves. His right thumb rested on the D. The ballast in the overhead fluorescent made its small even buzz and the foam drank it.</p>
<p>He had heard about Grace Johnson's speech at Friendly's. Everyone in Sudbury had heard about it. The knowledge sat in his chest the way a swallowed pill sits before it starts to go down.</p>
<p>They came in at six-thirty the way they always came in, Tuesday-Thursday-Saturday.</p>
<p>Ace Callahan first, because Ace always came first — the Premier kit was already set up down here, red-sparkle shells, the kick drum head with the black CROWN JEWELS decal he'd rubbed on with a pencil and a sheet of Letraset. He sat and ran a double-stroke roll on the snare without a word and the foam ate most of it. Max Wesslen came in behind him with the Fender Precision — black body, rosewood neck, the bass his father had bought him at E. U. Wurlitzer in the Back Bay for his sixteenth birthday — and plugged into the Ampeg cabinet and played four notes of the Queen bassline to warm the hand.</p>
<p>Elizabeth came last, because she lived upstairs and there was nothing to carry but the case. Thirteen years old. Violin under her arm in its student Gewa case. She sat on the arm of the chair Dr. Marsh sat in and opened the case on her lap and took out the chamois and wiped the rosin dust off the belly of the violin in small slow circles. On the left side of her neck, above the collar of her cardigan, the skin had gone pink where the chinrest lived. She was not aware of it, which was the point. She had cleared the bar so young that nobody had thought to put a bar there.</p>
<p>"From the top," Jackie said.</p>
<p>They ran the Chopin étude first — Opus 10, No. 3, the one called "Tristesse," the one anyone's mother recognized — arranged for four pieces, Jackie's arrangement, the melody in the violin, the harmony voicings on the Baldwin, Max walking the root under Ace's brushes. Jackie had written out every chart in his own hand, the ink neat as a ledger. It came out of the four of them the way a good clock comes out of its case. Nothing missing.</p>
<p>Then "Somebody to Love" in A-flat major — the Mercury gospel piled up in stacked thirds, Elizabeth doubling the top line with the bow, Max and Ace holding the pocket. Then "Bohemian Rhapsody," the whole six-minute architecture, B-flat opening, the operatic middle stacked, the hard-rock vamp, the coda. The harmonies locked. Every chord where it went. The kind of rehearsal a band videotapes and sends to a label.</p>
<p>Dr. Marsh came down during the last verse.</p>
<p>He came down with the leather notebook under his left arm, the one he used for patients and lectures and his children. He did not sit right away. He walked across the carpet behind Jackie, behind Ace's kit, and stood at the Baldwin for a breath — and as the last chord rang Jackie, whose eyes were on the keyboard, did not see his father's right hand come out and settle, and did not see the thumb touch middle C. Not press. Touch. Half a beat. The key did not sound. The thumb came off. His father's hand closed, went to the spine of the notebook, steadied it. Then Dr. Marsh crossed to the chair by the wall and sat, and opened the notebook on his knee.</p>
<p>Jackie played the last resolving chord and lifted his hands.</p>
<p>"The Chopin was excellent," his father said. He was smiling the small careful smile. "Elizabeth, your intonation in the second phrase was exactly right."</p>
<p>Elizabeth said thank you and put the chamois back in its corner of the case.</p>
<p>Dr. Marsh stood. He walked to the Baldwin and rested his hand on Jackie's shoulder, the weight of it careful as a stethoscope.</p>
<p>"The Queen, Jackson. When you play it like that — controlled. Precise. Every note exactly where it belongs — " He paused. He was choosing his word. He found it. "You remind me of Glenn Gould. That kind of technical mastery. Mercury is a talent, but he is undisciplined. You've taken his chaos and made it civilized."</p>
<p>He squeezed the shoulder once. The notebook went back under his arm.</p>
<p>"Keep this up," he said, "and tomorrow will take care of itself."</p>
<p>The side stairs took him back up. The door at the top closed on its soft seal.</p>
<p>Jackie's hands stayed on the keys another beat. Then they came off.</p>
<p>Ace packed first, because Ace always packed first, sticks into the stick bag, cymbals into the gig bag with a folded bath towel between them. Max coiled his cord over-under and slid the Precision into its case. Goodbyes at the side door. Feet going up the driveway in sneakers and a Chuck Taylor, a car door, a second car door, an engine, another engine. Quiet.</p>
<p>Elizabeth was still on the chair arm.</p>
<p>"You okay."</p>
<p>"Fine."</p>
<p>"Jack."</p>
<p>He looked at her. She had the chamois folded into its small square now and was closing the case in the way she closed books — not snapping the latch, pressing it shut.</p>
<p>"You know he stopped asking me to practice last year," she said. "I'd already done the job. He lets me alone." She looked at the Baldwin, not at him. "But you keep trying to make him hear something. And he doesn't hear music, Jack. He counts notes."</p>
<p>Jackie did not answer.</p>
<p>"Go to bed," she said. "Tomorrow's long."</p>
<p>She took the violin upstairs. The door at the top closed again.</p>
<p>He sat on the bench another minute with the fluorescent buzzing and the four little red Tascam LEDs glowing at the wall and the foam doing its patient work on every sound.</p>
<p>Then he played "Bohemian Rhapsody" for himself.</p>
<p>Not the arrangement. The record. He started at the operatic middle section where the voices stack, and he sang the *Galileo, Galileo, Galileo figaro* in his head because you could not sing it in a Marsh basement, and his left hand made the big jumping accompaniment that wasn't in any of the charts, and his right hand did the Mercury flourishes the charts had taken out. The ballast buzzed. The foam drank. The Tascam's LEDs held their small steady red.</p>
<p>Then the song ended.</p>
<p>He gently lowered the piano lid. The soft wooden click went across the foam and was absorbed before it finished. He killed the basement lights at the switch by the stairs.</p>
<p>He went up.</p>
<p>He passed the study door, open a hand's width — his father at the desk inside, textbooks fanned out in the green pool of the banker's lamp, a pencil in his hand, checking one book against another for tomorrow's lecture. He passed the living room. His mother in the wing chair under the floor lamp, a novel on her lap, her eyes on the page and not on the page. He passed Elizabeth's door, closed, the thin line of her bedside light under it.</p>
<p>His own door closed behind him with the quiet click of a door hung right.</p>
<p>On the desk, the Juilliard envelope lay where it had lain for three weeks, cream laid paper, the letter knife slit along the top. Next to it, face down on the stack of old Curtis programs where he had put it in March, the small silver frame held the photograph he could not look at tonight.</p>
<p>He did not turn the photograph over.</p>
<p>He did not open the envelope again.</p>
<p>He took off his shoes, set them at the foot of the bed, and got under the covers in his clothes.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 27: "TURN THE PAGE"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Three hours into the broadcast. A little under an hour left.</p>
<p>The Heaven Hill paper cup has gone soft at the rim. I refill it with coffee — cold from the pot in the break room, the way it always is after midnight. My hands on the console are steady. Guilt doesn't shake you forever. It just sits down beside you and waits for the cue.</p>
<p>The ON AIR sign holds its red. Behind the glass, the kid finally put the phone down. Quit scrolling about an hour ago. He's watching me the way you watch an old drunk at a bar who's about to say something true.</p>
<p>The Neve 8068 under my fingers was old when I first sat in this chair in 1970. Faders worn smooth at the tips from a thousand hands. Coffee ring from '86 still there, exactly where I left it. Studio B still smells like the forty years of cigarette smoke nobody bothered to shampoo out of the walls, even after the state banned it in 2003. Some of that smoke is mine. Pall Malls, '73 to '76. I quit the year I quit smoking. One vice at a time.</p>
<p>On the wall above the console, the old NAB cart rack — steel frame, thirty slots — is empty. Just the dust shadows where the brown-and-cream carts used to sit. Little gray rectangles. Ghosts of rotation. In '75 I could put my hand on any one of those carts in the dark and know the song by the weight of the tape. Now it's a laptop and a playlist somebody in Texas printed out. The kid runs the laptop. The kid does what the laptop tells him. Nobody in this building has their hand on a song anymore.</p>
<p>The Pink Floyd LP is still cued on the turntable, needle up. Waiting. To my right, the demo cassette: *Demo — Nick/Jackie — un-Authorized — May 1979*. I picked it up twenty minutes ago and I haven't put it down.</p>
<p>You heard January. Kowalski's charts. Article 7, Section 3 on the third page of a packet nobody reads until they need an exit. I told you where the tie language came from. I didn't tell you what I did with it.</p>
<p>The Battle of the Bands was my idea.</p>
<p>Not Tom Andrews's. Tom ran it. Tom executed. The seed was mine — pitched with Tom in the winter of '78, when the station was looking for something to do in the spring that wasn't another remote from a dealership parking lot. Regional high school contest. WBCN sponsorship. A recording session for the winners. Find the next kid who could make you feel something before the labels did. That was the whole point. Not a referendum on Sudbury. Not two names I didn't have yet. Discovery.</p>
<p>Sudbury turned out to be a town with a deep bench — more young musicians per square mile than any map would predict. A freshman band went on first and cleared out before the room knew what it was watching. Other kids from that hallway would make careers the room didn't stay for. Nick and Jackie were the ones it couldn't stop watching — and they were the band that never was.</p>
<p>I judged it as WBCN. I still thought I was the woman from the Brickyard who put conviction on the air because it deserved to be heard.</p>
<p>I'm going to tell you what I did in the judges' room before you hear the bands play tomorrow on this broadcast. Facts only. You render the verdict.</p>
<p>May sixteenth, 1979. Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School auditorium. Four hundred and forty-three seats. I sat on the panel between two people whose names I have let myself forget, clipboard on my knees, pencil in my hand.</p>
<p>The rubric on that clipboard was mine. Technical execution. Arrangement. Stage presence. Originality. Little boxes. Little numbers. Originality and emotional connection weighted double — the language Tom and I wrote so discovery could win on paper. Also weighted so neither side of Hop Brook was going to win clean on arithmetic alone. I knew that going in. I wrote it that way.</p>
<p>Article 7, Section 3 was on page three. *In the event of a tie in the final round, tied contestants shall share the studio recording session equally.* Boring line. Sponsor-friendly. I'd added it at my kitchen table in January, before I knew Nick Johnson's name, before I'd met Jackie Marsh in a garage on Dutton Road in March. I wasn't aiming it at two boys. I was aiming it at a room that might need an exit.</p>
<p>When the scores landed close — when the room was already humming with sides — I did not recuse myself. I did not throw out the clause. I did not pretend I didn't see it.</p>
<p>I wanted them both in Studio C. I wanted the photograph. I wanted the story. I used the machinery.</p>
<p>Hop Brook divided those kids. The cafeteria divided them. Dan Johnson's crane cable in November of '77 divided them. Dr. Marsh's kitchen table divided them. I didn't create any of that.</p>
<p>But I took a slow private death and I put a ticket price on it. The Battle was supposed to find talent. The room turned it into a war anyway. For content. For sponsors. For a photograph in the Globe.</p>
<p>I didn't kill their friendship. But I gave class warfare a microphone.</p>
<p>Eleven fifty-one. Nine minutes.</p>
<p>Freddie Mercury's voice comes through the monitors from somewhere deeper than analog. He's been gone eighteen years. Thirty years of medicine too late.</p>
<p>I stayed at WBCN through 1980. Left eventually — the industry moves you or breaks you. I carried a cassette I never played for anyone.</p>
<p>The station brought me back tonight because nobody else wanted it. I drew short straw. Walked in at nine o'clock to play eulogy for my own life. On paper I'm the guest of honor. In the actual room I'm a mirror.</p>
<p>Four decades of rock radio. The brochure says *fifty-six* because somebody in corporate couldn't count and nobody stopped them. Monday morning, 98.5 starts playing contemporary Christian out of this same transmitter. Jesus on the frequency we used to give Patti Smith. I don't know if that's irony or just America.</p>
<p>The demo cassette is warm in my palm. *un-Authorized.* Still unplayed on air. I haven't put it down since the ON AIR light came on.</p>
<p>The Pink Floyd LP waits. *Shine On You Crazy Diamond.* The memo says that's how WBCN goes out. Graceful. Elegiac. Ending by committee.</p>
<p>Nick closed his garage bay door last night on Dutton Road. Jackie went to bed in his clothes on Maple Street. Grace stood on a back step with her hand on a storm door she didn't open. The town is asleep. The auditorium is dark. The rules are already on the copier at Lincoln-Sudbury, third page down.</p>
<p>Tomorrow the lights come up.</p>
<p>The engineer behind the glass isn't blinking. I set the coffee cup down next to the demo tape. I lean into the mic.</p>
<p>Turn the page with me.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 28: "RHIANNON"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Monday, May 12, 1979. Four days to the Battle.</p>
<p>The rooster didn't crow at the Lally place. The pigs handled that — two hundred and sixty Yorkshires grunting their way to consciousness at five-fifteen every morning, a sound like the earth clearing its throat.</p>
<p>Patty Lally pulled on her boots at the back door and stepped into air thick with hog waste and wet hay. The southeast corner of Sudbury didn't wake up pretty. It woke up honest.</p>
<p>Five-gallon buckets. Grain mixed with kitchen scraps. Eight pens while her father checked water lines and her mother tallied receipts at the kitchen table with the radio low — country, turned down to company, not conversation. The pigs pressed against the gates when they saw her coming. Not affection. Hunger wearing a familiar face. Patty didn't mind. There was something clean about a relationship that never pretended to be more than it was.</p>
<p>By seven-forty she'd showered twice, braided her hair still damp, and caught the bus at the corner of Morse and Landham. Soap could mask pig but not erase it. She'd learned that in seventh grade when Cheryl Mancini wrinkled her nose in homeroom and said, loud enough for everyone, "Something smells like bacon."</p>
<p>The class laughed.</p>
<p>Patty looked at Cheryl until the laughter died. "Bacon pays our mortgage."</p>
<p>Nobody brought it up again.</p>
<p>Lincoln-Sudbury in May smelled like chalk dust and floor wax and the low-grade panic of seniors counting down to graduation. Patty chained her blue Schwinn to the rack by the gym door and walked the east hallway toward first period, past lockers tagged with band names and AEROSMITH vs QUEEN graffiti the janitor scrubbed off every Friday and that reappeared every Monday, the argument as permanent as the cinder blocks underneath.</p>
<p>On the corkboard outside Mr. Wentworth's music room, the goldenrod flyer was curling at the corners:</p>
<p>BATTLE OF THE BANDS MAY 16, 1979 LINCOLN-SUDBURY REGIONAL HIGH SCHOOL GRAND PRIZE: $500 — PROFESSIONAL RECORDING SESSION SPONSORED BY WBCN — THE ROCK OF BOSTON</p>
<p>Seven names on the sign-up sheet. One line left.</p>
<p>Patty read the date twice. Not the money — five hundred dollars wouldn't change what she already knew about her music. She read the empty line and the war the whole school had been choosing sides for since March.</p>
<p>The cafeteria split like a cell dividing.</p>
<p>Nick Johnson's table near the windows — Rollers, shop kids, trays cleared fast, jaws working. Jackie Marsh's table by the doors — Rampers, college talk over sandwiches wrapped in wax paper, every gesture measured before it left his mouth. Between them, forty feet of linoleum nobody crossed.</p>
<p>Patty sat with Kristy Wesslen and Robin Bosshard near the milk coolers — neutral ground, claimed by nobody. From there she could see both boys without turning her head.</p>
<p>The strange thing — the thing only Patty seemed to notice — was the way they never looked at each other. Not avoiding. Avoiding would require acknowledgment. This was worse. They'd erased each other from the room. Nick's eyes swept the cafeteria and skipped Jackie's table like a scratch on a record, the needle jumping over the part it couldn't play. Jackie's gaze moved through the space where Nick sat as if that corner held nothing but empty chairs.</p>
<p>Patty bit her apple and watched.</p>
<p>And for one breath the cafeteria thinned — pine resin, lake water turning copper at the edge of dark, three voices finding the same held note on a dock she'd left four summers ago. Nick's root. Jackie's fifth above. Her E on top, clean and unshowy, the way a bell finds the pitch it's been waiting for. The meadow had sent the sound down to the water and the water had sent it back, and for five days nothing in the world had mattered except what happened when they stopped trying to make it.</p>
<p>Then Donnie said something that made Kathy laugh on the platform, and the image broke, and the linoleum was linoleum again.</p>
<p>Memorial Congregational Church at Sudbury Center smelled like old hymnals and lemon oil and whatever they put in the wood polish that made the pews glow amber under the overhead lights — white clapboard and a modest steeple you could see from Route 20 when the leaves were down, the kind of New England church that had been holding the town's ordinary Sundays since before anybody's grandmother was born.</p>
<p>Tuesday evening. Five o'clock. Reverend Goff left the key under the mat because he trusted musicians the way he trusted anyone who brought their own instrument to a house of God. By five-fifteen they'd moved hymnals off the front pews, run an extension cord from the sacristy, and turned the chancel rail into a stage line.</p>
<p>Steve Bankuti set up on the chancel rug — snare, hi-hat, kick, a ride cymbal he'd bought at a yard sale in Maynard for three dollars. Robin Bosshard plugged his bass into a small Fender amp wedged between the pulpit and the lectern. Kristy Wesslen perched on the communion rail with her acoustic, tuning by ear.</p>
<p>Patty stood where the minister stood on Sundays. Guitar strap over her shoulder. Steel strings, calluses that had nothing to do with feed buckets and everything to do with the reclaim corner where her father had taught her that quit-on things still had worth.</p>
<p>"Battle of the Bands," she said. "WBCN. May sixteenth. One slot left."</p>
<p>Robin looked up from his bass. "That's the Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh show."</p>
<p>"I know."</p>
<p>Steve's sticks stopped on the pew back — a paradiddle cut in half by silence. "Whole school's chosen sides."</p>
<p>"I'm not choosing sides." Patty looked at the stained glass above the chancel — Sudbury names in gold leaf, the town's war dead and its ordinary dead honored in red and blue light that fell across the floor like bruises on the church's skin. "Somebody has to play music that isn't a weapon."</p>
<p>Kristy exchanged a look with Steve. "You want to walk into their war?"</p>
<p>"I want to play our songs. Not louder. Not angrier. Not more perfect than them. Honest."</p>
<p>Robin set the bass down, then picked it back up — his thinking move. "So we're entering a war to play peace songs?"</p>
<p>Patty almost smiled. "Something like that."</p>
<p>Nobody said yes. Nobody had to.</p>
<p>"Rhiannon," she said. "From the top. Count it in, Steve."</p>
<p>Steve touched the hi-hat with his stick — one, two, three — and Robin found the pulse underneath, a walking line that didn't hurry, hooves on wet ground, the song's restlessness before the voice arrives. Kristy came in on the second pass, picking the figure that circled the root without crowding it, the way ivy circled a trellis without choking it.</p>
<p>Patty let two bars go by with the guitar silent. Just the room breathing — stone floor, empty pews, dust in the stained-glass light — and then she opened her mouth.</p>
<p>Not loud. Not yet. The melody came up through her chest the way the brook came up through the conservation land in March: everything to say, nowhere to hide.</p>
<p>She didn't sing the words all the way through — she never did in rehearsal, not until the song had a body — but the vowels were there when they needed to be, the rise on *night*, the turn where the line refused to resolve, where Stevie Nicks had built a song about a woman who wouldn't stay where you put her. Patty understood that song. She had always understood it.</p>
<p>By the second chorus Robin had locked in. His bass hummed through the floor — you felt it in your feet, the building's bones carrying what the air carried. Kristy's harmony wound around the lead without copying it, different temperature, same weather. Steve moved from brushes to sticks on the snare rim, a hiss that said *listen* before it said *move*.</p>
<p>They ran it again.</p>
<p>The third pass was the one. The church stopped being a church and became a resonant box — vaulted ceiling, old beams, brass plaques on the wall and the long honor roll of Sudbury names hearing a prayer they'd never been offered before. Patty's voice climbed and didn't show off the climb. It just went where the song asked. When she hit the last held note she let it die in the rafters instead of forcing it, and the silence after was full, the way the meadow had been full when the mosquitoes finally drove them inside and the lake still held the echo.</p>
<p>Steve set his sticks down. "That's the one."</p>
<p>Robin nodded. "That's the one."</p>
<p>Kristy was crying a little, which she would deny if you asked. "We're going to get swallowed."</p>
<p>"Maybe," Patty said. "I don't care about winning."</p>
<p>"Then what's the point?"</p>
<p>She thought about the dock. The triad. The half-second the lake gave back.</p>
<p>"The point is showing up," she said. "The point is playing music that remembers what music is for."</p>
<p>She walked home after rehearsal because the bus didn't run past seven and her mother was buried in feed invoices. Two miles through Southeast Sudbury in the May dark, past the Cavicchio security light throwing long shadows across empty pens, past mini-bike ruts in the conservation trailheads like old wounds.</p>
<p>The smell found her at the property line. Thick, animal, alive. Home.</p>
<p>The house sat dark except for the kitchen light — vinyl siding that needed paint, barn leaning slightly east, chickens having beaten the grass into mud. Her mother sat at the table, calculator and ledger open, reading glasses pushed up on her forehead.</p>
<p>"Rehearsal okay?"</p>
<p>"Yeah. We're entering the Battle of the Bands."</p>
<p>Her mother looked up. "At the high school. The one with the radio station."</p>
<p>"WBCN. Yeah."</p>
<p>Calculator set down. Glasses removed. The look a mother gives when she knows her daughter is about to walk into something complicated and has decided to let her do it anyway.</p>
<p>"That's the Johnson boy and the Marsh boy's thing. The whole town's been talking about it."</p>
<p>"I know."</p>
<p>"You sure you want to get in the middle of that?"</p>
<p>Patty opened the refrigerator, found last night's meatloaf, ate it cold over the sink. The meat was dry. She didn't care.</p>
<p>"I'm not getting in the middle of anything. I'm just playing music."</p>
<p>Her mother watched her eat. The expression on her face said she'd raised a daughter who made her own decisions and wasn't about to start second-guessing now — but she reserved the right to worry.</p>
<p>"Okay," she said. And went back to the ledger.</p>
<p>Mortgage due the twentieth. Feed bill the twenty-second. The numbers didn't care about battles.</p>
<p>Thursday. Last period. 2:45 PM.</p>
<p>The hallway outside Wentworth's room had cleared — buses idling, parking lots emptying, the building settling into the hollow quiet of a Thursday afternoon.</p>
<p>Patty walked into the music room alone. Fluorescents humming their one flat note. Wentworth sat behind his desk grading papers with the patience of a man who'd traded symphony halls for steady paychecks twenty years ago and had mostly stopped regretting it.</p>
<p>The sign-up sheet sat at the corner of his desk. Seven names filled the seven lines above the last blank space.</p>
<p>"Mr. Wentworth."</p>
<p>He looked up. Pen still in hand.</p>
<p>"I'd like to register a band."</p>
<p>Something shifted in his face. Not surprise. Recognition. He'd heard Patty sing in music class — had stopped mid-sentence once, the way a counselor at camp had stopped on a dock four years ago, because the voice coming out of this tall girl from the southeast corner wasn't the voice of a girl filling out worksheets about time signatures.</p>
<p>"Band name?"</p>
<p>"Rumours."</p>
<p>He wrote it in careful block letters.</p>
<p>"Members?"</p>
<p>"Robin Bosshard — bass and guitar. Kristy Wesslen, harmonies and acoustic. Steve Bankuti, drums. Me — lead vocal and guitar."</p>
<p>"Genre?"</p>
<p>"Fleetwood Mac."</p>
<p>Wentworth set his pen down. Leaned back. Studied her — damp braid, steel-string calluses, the faintest trace of something soap couldn't conquer.</p>
<p>"You know what you're walking into," he said.</p>
<p>It wasn't a question.</p>
<p>"I know what I'm walking into."</p>
<p>He held her gaze a moment longer, then slid the registration form across the desk. "Eight bands. You're the last slot."</p>
<p>Patty signed her name. The pen scratched against the paper, loud in the empty room.</p>
<p>She took the carbon copy, folded it into her back pocket, and walked out into the corridor. A locker slammed somewhere down the hall — last student, last echo. Four days.</p>
<p>She hummed "Rhiannon" all the way to the bus stop. Not as a statement. Just something the song did when you let it live in your mouth — restless, certain, moving through the May air like it knew where it was going even when the town didn't.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 29: "ROCK AND ROLL ALL NITE"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Thursday, May 15, 1979. One day to the Battle.</p>
<p>Lincoln-Sudbury had a fever.</p>
<p>Not the kind the nurse measured with a thermometer under the tongue — the kind that lived in the hallways, in the way kids walked faster between bells, in the volume of nothing important turned up to maximum. Posters for May sixteenth had multiplied overnight. One on the music-room corkboard. One above the milk coolers. One in the glass corridor where the Rats smoked between classes and pretended not to read anything official. The Xerox toner still smelled faintly of ozone, as if the paper itself were warm.</p>
<p>Mr. Wentworth's door stayed open. Inside, his clipboard had become a battle map — eight band names, eight time slots, pencil marks crossed out and rewritten until the graphite shone. He sat at his desk with the metronome that didn't work and the Jordan Hall program in the drawer and the expression of a man who had traded symphony halls for this and was not sure which had been the sacrifice.</p>
<p>Patty Lally came through the east hallway with her biology textbook and her canvas bag and the melody that lived in her throat whether she wanted it or not. She had not slept much. Rumours had run the set at Memorial Congregational until Reverend Goff's nine o'clock curfew — Rhiannon twice, then Dreams, then a stumble through Silver Springs that Robin made right on the fourth pass. Her fingers still felt the steel strings in her pockets when she wasn't playing.</p>
<p>The cafeteria at lunch was geography turned vertical.</p>
<p>Nick Johnson's table near the windows — Stephen Murphy with his chocolate milk, David Cassel drumming the formica, Mark Pianka arguing about a Rhodes buzz on the B below middle C. Nick sat with his tray untouched and his jaw set, guitar case propped against the radiator like a rifle he didn't plan to put down. He had lost weight. The boy from Elbanobscot was still in there somewhere, cheekbones and all, buried under two years and a garage that smelled like Dan.</p>
<p>Jackie Marsh's table on the platform — Crown Jewels in blazer and jeans, Elizabeth Chen running a passage on her violin without the bow touching the strings, just fingerings in the air. Jackie sat with the posture of a conductor waiting for a downbeat that wouldn't come until tomorrow night. His eyes did not cross the forty feet of linoleum. Neither did Nick's.</p>
<p>Patty sat with Kristy and Robin by the milk coolers and ate her apple and watched the room divide itself along lines that had nothing to do with Hop Brook and everything to do with a poster Maxanne Sartori had pitched in the winter of '78.</p>
<p>"Whole school's gone stupid," Steve Bankuti said, sliding onto the bench with a bag of chips and a grin that didn't reach his eyes. "My sister asked me who I was voting for. Like it's an election."</p>
<p>"It's not an election," Kristy said.</p>
<p>"Tell them that."</p>
<p>The between-class crush at eleven-fifteen moved like cattle through a chute — four hundred kids in six minutes, shoulder to shoulder, sneakers squeaking, the air thick with body spray and bubble gum and voices saying nothing important at maximum volume.</p>
<p>Patty pressed against the lockers to let a knot of Rampers pass. Her combination stuck since February; she had the textbook in her left arm and the canvas bag on her right hip.</p>
<p>Nick Johnson came from the other direction.</p>
<p>Walking fast. Guitar case slung over his shoulder like a rifleman carrying his weapon, head down, the set of his jaw saying *don't talk to me* and the tension in his shoulders saying *I dare you to try*. He passed within arm's reach.</p>
<p>Patty hummed.</p>
<p>Not deliberately — the way other people cracked knuckles. Five notes from the opening of "Go Your Own Way," a reflex, not a message. The melody left her throat before she could swallow it.</p>
<p>Nick stopped.</p>
<p>One step past her. Frozen. His shoulders tightened, loosened, tightened again — micro-movements of a body fighting a memory it didn't want to acknowledge. He turned his head, not all the way, just enough to show his profile against the fluorescent light. Something in the phrase had snagged him — a lake, pine trees, a girl's voice finding the space between his and Jackie's, a week when music hadn't been a war.</p>
<p>He looked at Patty. She looked back.</p>
<p>For one second the hallway noise fell away and they stood in the gap between past and present — two people who knew what the other sounded like at thirteen, what a voice did when it wasn't trying to prove anything. Recognition faint as a shape through clouded glass.</p>
<p>Then Nick blinked. Shook his head, half a motion, like shaking off a chill. He walked on. Guitar case bouncing against his hip. Gone around the corner before Patty could exhale.</p>
<p>She watched him go. Lockers slammed. Somebody laughed too loud about something that wasn't funny.</p>
<p>Patty leaned against the cool metal and let the melody die. Five notes. Unplanned. But they'd landed — somewhere behind Nick's eyes, in the part of his brain that remembered what his body had decided to forget.</p>
<p>Whether it mattered was another question.</p>
<p>She hefted the biology book and walked to class.</p>
<p>Third period, the parking lot.</p>
<p>Somebody's Trans Am had the windows down and the radio up — KISS, "Rock and Roll All Nite," the chorus bleeding through the open windows of the auto-shop bay where Mr. Pacenka pretended not to hear it. Two juniors by the vending machines mouthed the words without getting the irony right. Patty walked past on her way to the side door and heard the hook one more time before the car pulled out toward Route 117 — *party every day* — and the sound thinned to tire hiss on asphalt.</p>
<p>The town was tuning itself. Some kids for volume. Some for precision. Rumours for something else entirely.</p>
<p>Last period. 2:45 PM. Wentworth's music room.</p>
<p>Patty had come back for the guitar picks she'd left in the instrument cupboard. The hallway was empty — after-school clubs migrating toward the gym, where custodians were already stacking chairs in the auditorium and testing the microphone with a tap that echoed through the whole east wing.</p>
<p>She opened the door and heard the piano.</p>
<p>Jackie Marsh sat at the upright in the corner — not the Steinway in the band room, the scarred practice piano Wentworth kept for students who needed to work through something the Steinway was too pristine to hold. Jackie's blazer was draped over the bench beside him. His sleeves were rolled to his elbows. His hands moved with the precision ten thousand dollars in lessons bought — but something else was happening in the music. Something the lessons hadn't taught.</p>
<p>"Somebody to Love."</p>
<p>Not performing it. Not rehearsing it for tomorrow. Playing it the way a person writes in a journal — private, unpolished, honest. The runs weren't clean. He let notes ring too long or cut them too short, feeling his way through instead of executing. His left hand found the bass line by instinct, not chart, and the chord voicings were his own — darker than the original, stacked with dissonance that resolved into something aching and true.</p>
<p>He sang it quiet. Barely above the piano's volume. Not Freddie Mercury's operatic howl — Jackie's voice, stripped of the performance polish he wore like armor, raw and reaching.</p>
<p>Patty stood in the doorway. She didn't move. Didn't breathe loud enough to break the room's agreement with what Jackie was doing — the old walls and the fluorescents and the instrument lockers all holding still, offering the boy what the rest of his life refused: a space where he didn't have to be perfect.</p>
<p>The song ended. The last chord hung three seconds, then two, then one, then gone.</p>
<p>"That was real," Patty said.</p>
<p>Jackie spun on the bench. His hands came off the keys like they'd been burned. Eyes wide — not fear, exactly, but the wild exposure of someone caught without their costume.</p>
<p>"How long have you been standing there?"</p>
<p>"Long enough."</p>
<p>He stared at her. Mouth opened, closed, opened again. She watched the armor rebuild in real time — shoulders squaring, chin lifting, the careful construction of a boy taught that vulnerability was weakness and musicianship was measured in precision, not feeling.</p>
<p>Underneath it, in the second before the walls went up, she saw something she recognized. Elbanobscot. The music cabin. Queen when he thought nobody was listening. Shoulders dropping three inches. The same breath.</p>
<p>Freedom.</p>
<p>Patty turned to leave.</p>
<p>"Patty."</p>
<p>She stopped. Looked back over her shoulder.</p>
<p>Jackie sat on the piano bench with his hands in his lap. The armor was up, but it had a crack in it — a thin line from his throat to his chest where the song still lived.</p>
<p>"What are you doing here?"</p>
<p>"Getting my picks." She crossed to the cupboard, found the tortoiseshell Fenders worn smooth at the tips, put them in her jacket pocket.</p>
<p>At the door she stopped again. Not for drama. Because something in her wouldn't let her leave without saying it.</p>
<p>"You don't play like that when anyone's watching."</p>
<p>Jackie said nothing.</p>
<p>"You should."</p>
<p>The door closed behind her with a soft click. Her boots on the linoleum sounded like a slow heartbeat in the empty hall.</p>
<p>Five o'clock. Memorial Congregational Church, Sudbury Center.</p>
<p>The parking lot held three cars and a Chevy van with a rumble strip on the rear bumper. Steve was already inside, setting up on the chancel rug. Robin coiled cables from the last run. Kristy tuned by ear on the communion rail.</p>
<p>Patty shouldered her Martin through the side door — the D-28 her father had bought used in Framingham when she was fourteen, spruce top darkened with age, neck worn smooth where her thumb rode the back during chord changes.</p>
<p>"One more," she said. "Full run. Like tomorrow."</p>
<p>Steve counted in on the hi-hat. They ran Rhiannon into Dreams into the bridge of Silver Springs they kept botching and then, because Steve was seventeen and nervous and couldn't help himself, the opening riff of "Rock and Roll All Nite" — four bars on the snare rim, a joke, a pressure valve.</p>
<p>Kristy laughed into her hand. Robin shook his head but his foot kept time.</p>
<p>"Not on the setlist," Patty said.</p>
<p>"Thank God," Robin said.</p>
<p>They packed up at seven. Reverend Goff looked in once, nodded, said *I'll lock up after you*, and disappeared toward the parish office. Steve loaded his kick pedal into the Chevette with the hinge that whined past halfway. Kristy hugged Patty a little too long at the door — the way you hug somebody the night before something you can't take back.</p>
<p>"See you at the school," Kristy said.</p>
<p>"See you at the school."</p>
<p>Patty stood in the parking lot after the taillights went. The steeple caught the last sun. Sudbury Center was quiet — white clapboard, honor-roll glass, the town's ordinary Sunday face turned toward tomorrow's war.</p>
<p>She did not hum on the ride home. She listened to the wind off the Cavicchio fields instead, and to the engine of her mother's Plymouth, and to the silence that wasn't empty — the silence of a girl who had put her fingers on the fretboard and knew what she was for.</p>
<p>Tonight the town still held its breath.</p>
<p>Tomorrow the fever would break into noise.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 30: "SILVER SPRINGS"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Thursday night, May 15, 1979. A little after nine.</p>
<p>The last car door in the Memorial Congregational parking lot had been Steve Bankuti's Chevette — the driver-side hinge whining past halfway — and Patty had counted it the way she counted beats, and then she'd been alone.</p>
<p>Rumours had packed up an hour ago. Kristy's amp. Robin's bass in its gig bag. Steve's kick pedal and snare in their padded cases. They'd hugged her at the side door the way you hug somebody the night before surgery — a little too long and too careful — and gone home to their mothers and their own nerves.</p>
<p>The sanctuary was what it always was on a Thursday night when the town wasn't looking: empty.</p>
<p>Patty had moved the guitar in on purpose. The Martin D-28 her father had bought used in Framingham when she was fourteen — spruce top darkened with age, neck worn smooth where her thumb rode the back during chord changes. She took it out and sat in a front pew with the case open beside her and tuned by ear.</p>
<p>E. Flat. A hair on the tuning key. The string came up under her thumbnail with the small sweet creak of bronze against bone nut. A. Fine. D. Fine. G — sharp; she gave it back. B. B was always the one. She tuned it twice. High E. Fine.</p>
<p>Jeans with barn mud dried on the left knee. Flannel over her Rumours T-shirt. Hair braided over the right shoulder. The back of her left hand had a pink line from barbed wire last Tuesday. On her right forearm, a hoof step going yellow at the edges. She smelled faintly of pig. She knew it. She was not going to apologize for it to a room full of Sudbury names in gold leaf.</p>
<p>She walked to the votive rack by the side wall — red glass jars on a black iron frame, most unlit on a weekday. She struck a long match. The head caught with that small chemical pop. The flame found a wick in the second row. The wax made a tiny suck of air taking the fire, and the red glass around it went alive.</p>
<p>She did not say who it was for. She never said.</p>
<p>Above the chancel, the honor-roll window held the town's dead in lead seams — ordinary dead and war dead together, red and blue light gone dark without the sun behind it. Sunday's incense still hung in the wood. Melted wax. Worn oak. The brass plaques on the wall caught nothing but the votive's flicker.</p>
<p>The hymnal was red. She rolled it back and forth along her thigh, pebbled leatherette on denim, a soft dry sound.</p>
<p>She opened the camp notebook.</p>
<p>The letters were inside the back cover. Seven of them, folded small, paper gone soft at the creases. Four from Nick. Three from Jackie.</p>
<p>She took Nick's top one and opened it across her knee.</p>
<p>The handwriting was cramped and sideways-slanted, like he was running out of room even though the page was half empty. In the third line he had crossed out *my dad* and written *my father* above it and then crossed that out too and put *him*. Three tries on two letters. The pen had pressed so hard on the last one that the paper had dimpled through to the back.</p>
<p>She folded it. She slid it back.</p>
<p>Jackie's top letter was thinner paper, the kind with faint blue lines. The handwriting went straight across the page like a prayer. Every *t* crossed. Every *i* dotted. At the bottom, where he had written *I am learning the C-minor prelude and I can almost make it breathe*, he had underlined *almost* once, careful, with a ruler.</p>
<p>Her thumb rode the crease. She put it back.</p>
<p>Nick's cramped urgent fingers. Jackie's ruler.</p>
<p>She thought about Nick in the hallway today — five notes, a head turn, a shake, gone. She thought about Jackie at the practice piano with his sleeves rolled up, singing *Somebody to Love* to an empty room that wasn't empty.</p>
<p>She let the letters sit. She did not want to sort them anymore. She was not going to fix two boys by reading hard enough.</p>
<p>She pulled the guitar back onto her thigh and played a D. Then a G. She slid to a C; her ring finger missed the third fret and buzzed against the second, and she laughed — out loud, in the empty sanctuary, one small startled bark. The honor-roll window did not move. She played the C again and this time it rang.</p>
<p>Then, without meaning to, she was humming.</p>
<p>It came up out of her the way weather came up out of a field — the shape of *Silver Springs*, not the words, the step-down at the end of the line, her right hand finding a quiet bass note underneath because the song wanted it and her hand knew.</p>
<p>Camp Elbanobscot. Four summers ago. Lake going copper at the end of the day. Nick's guitar out of tune and neither of them caring. Jackie finding a suspended chord nobody had asked for, a fourth hanging in the air, and her voice coming in over the top without thinking — lake water on the dock pilings, sap-hot pine, three voices and two guitars and nobody keeping count. They had not been trying. That was why it worked. They had been breathing, and the breathing had happened to rhyme.</p>
<p>She hummed another bar. She was on the turn the song wouldn't give her cleanly — the place between *you* and *isn't* — when a car door went in the parking lot. Short. Metal. Far side.</p>
<p>Her thumb went still on the G string. The car started. The car pulled out. It wasn't for her.</p>
<p>Her hand went into her canvas bag and found the little white Panasonic transistor her brother had given her two Christmases ago, antenna bent from too many glove compartments. She almost turned it on. WBCN would be where she'd left it; it was always where she'd left it. She took her hand out of the bag. Not tonight.</p>
<p>She tuned the B string one more time because the B string was always the one.</p>
<p>She played the opening of the song — the first four bars, for herself — and let it die against the stone. The note went up into the rafters and came back thinner, the church holding it the way the meadow had held the triad, the way the lake had held the dock.</p>
<p>She put the guitar in the case and latched it.</p>
<p>The votive was still going. The red glass was warm when she passed a hand near it, not touching. At the door she dipped two fingers in the font and crossed herself the way her mother had taught her — quick, four points, sternum and shoulders — without closing her eyes.</p>
<p>She pushed the door open with her hip, guitar case in her right hand, notebook in her left. The night air smelled of cut grass and diesel off Route 20 and, faintly from the southeast, home.</p>
<p>Behind her the little orange flower kept burning inside its red glass. The hymnal sat on the pew with the print of her thumb on the cover.</p>
<p>She let the door close on its soft seal.</p>
<p>She walked to her mother's Plymouth at the curb.</p>
<p>Tomorrow she would stand on a stage with eight bands and a town that had turned music into sides. Nick with grief and duct tape. Jackie with precision and a blazer. She could not save them.</p>
<p>She could play *Silver Springs* and mean it.</p>
<p>The Plymouth's engine turned over on the third try. She drove home past the Cavicchio fields with the notebook on the seat beside her and the guitar in back and did not turn the radio on.</p>
<p>The stars were out. The sow pens were quiet.</p>
<p>She wasn't nervous. She wasn't hopeful.</p>
<p>She was ready.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 31: "THUNDER ROAD"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>May 16, 1979. Battle day.</p>
<p>The heat had come in overnight — eighty-nine by noon if the bank sign on Route 20 could be trusted, the air thick enough to chew. By seven in the morning the sky over Sudbury was the color of a bruise healing wrong: yellow at the horizon, iron-gray above the pines, and somewhere west of Worcester the first distant roll of thunder that wasn't thunder yet, just pressure moving through the atmosphere the way a chord moves through a room before anybody plays it.</p>
<p>Nick Johnson woke to the house ticking.</p>
<p>Refrigerator. Pipe in the wall. Grace already up — coffee pot burbling in the kitchen, the smell of Maxwell House through the closed bedroom door. He lay on his back with the sheet at his waist and listened to the thunder again, farther off, a low note under the morning.</p>
<p>He got up.</p>
<p>Grace had left a plate on the counter — two eggs scrambled dry the way he liked them, toast cut diagonal, glass of milk. She was in the good navy dress already, the one with the buttons, winter coat folded over the back of the chair though it was May. Friendly's had given her the night off. Tom Andrews had pinned a WBCN button crooked over her purse clasp without being asked.</p>
<p>"You eat," she said.</p>
<p>He ate standing up.</p>
<p>The Marshall sat by the side door in its case. The pawn-shop guitar Dan had bought him — crack in the upper bout packed with wood glue, strap dark where his thumb rode — leaned against the pegboard with the picks in his pocket. Stephen's Nova would be at the curb at eight. David was walking from the quarter mile down. Mark had the Rhodes in his brother's van.</p>
<p>Grace watched him check the latch on the guitar case twice.</p>
<p>"Don't forget to breathe," she said.</p>
<p>"I know."</p>
<p>"Nick."</p>
<p>"I know, Mom."</p>
<p>She put her hand on his shoulder once, the way she put her hand on things she couldn't fix — flat, brief, warm — and took it away before it could become a speech.</p>
<p>Two miles north on Maple Street, the Marsh house faced clipped hedges and a sky that looked the same from every window.</p>
<p>Jackie had been awake since five.</p>
<p>He sat at the kitchen table in jeans and a white shirt with the top button open, coffee he didn't drink cooling in front of him. Eileen moved through the room in her morning way — pearls, orange juice poured into a glass with the pulp screened out, toast triangles on a plate nobody had asked for. Dr. Richard Marsh read the *Globe* business section without turning the page.</p>
<p>"The forecast calls for storms after six," Eileen said.</p>
<p>"Mm," Richard said.</p>
<p>On the sideboard, the Juilliard envelope sat unopened where it had sat for three weeks. In Jackie's room upstairs, face down in a drugstore frame, the Polaroid from Camp Elbanobscot — three kids on a dock — had not been turned over since March.</p>
<p>Stephen Marsh would drive him at eight-fifteen. Ace and Max and Elizabeth would meet at the loading dock behind the auditorium with the Baldwin parts and the Ampeg and the violin case that cost more than Nick's entire garage.</p>
<p>Jackie finished the coffee because finishing was what you did. He went upstairs to tie his shoes.</p>
<p>Patty Lally fed the sows at five-fifteen the way she always fed them. Thunder rolled closer while she hosed the near pen — not close, not yet, just the sky clearing its throat.</p>
<p>By seven-thirty she was in the Plymouth with her Martin in back and Steve's textless plan already made: meet at the school at four, sound check at five, Rumours seventh on the card if Chuck Morrison read the lineup the same way Wentworth had posted it. She had the set in her head. Rhiannon. Dreams. Silver Springs. Go Your Own Way. Not a weapon. A reminder.</p>
<p>She drove past the Cavicchio fields with the windows down. The smell came in and she did not apologize for it.</p>
<p>Route 20 was already wrong by eight o'clock.</p>
<p>Cars lined the shoulder near the Lincoln-Sudbury front walk — station wagons from the northwest waxed and quiet, pickups from the northeast with rust at the wheel wells, a Volvo with a college sticker and a Nova with a dent in the rear quarter panel and Steve Bankuti's Chevette with the hinge that whined. Kids in denim and kids in blazers crossed the parking lot carrying cases the way soldiers carry rifles — not looking where they were going, looking at who was looking.</p>
<p>The booster club had set up a table by the lobby doors. Popcorn machine not running yet. Programs stacked in a cardboard box. WBCN banner tacked to the brick with duct tape.</p>
<p>Nick came up from the shop-corridor side with Stephen and David and Mark behind him, Rhodes on a dolly rattling over the expansion joints. The Marshall case banged Nick's knee through the leather. Stephen said something about the set list order and Nick didn't answer because his throat had gone dry the way it went dry before every room that mattered.</p>
<p>Jackie came from the east entrance with his father walking ten feet behind — not escorting, accompanying, the way Richard Marsh accompanied everything — and Ace carrying the kick drum and Elizabeth with her violin case and Max silent with the Precision on his back.</p>
<p>They crossed the same asphalt without crossing the same path.</p>
<p>Nick's eyes swept the lot and skipped Jackie's blazer the way a needle skips a scratch. Jackie's gaze moved through the space where Nick stood as if that patch of pavement held nothing but empty oil stains.</p>
<p>Forty feet. Linoleum's outdoor cousin.</p>
<p>Patty watched from the loading ramp with her case on her hip and Robin tuning bass behind her and did not wave at either of them.</p>
<p>The auditorium doors were propped open with a wedge of two-by-four.</p>
<p>Inside, the air smelled of floor wax and cast iron and four hundred and forty-three folding seats waiting to groan. The painted maple backdrop from every school play since 1960 hung behind an empty stage — red gone brown at the edges. Fluorescent cove lights threw the room into grocery-store color. Somewhere backstage a custodian tested a microphone — tap, tap, feedback squeal, apology.</p>
<p>Nick stood at the stage-right gap in the velvet curtain where the hem didn't reach the floor and looked at the house through the slit. Third row left was where Grace would sit. He made himself look away.</p>
<p>Jackie stood stage left at the Yamaha they'd rolled out for the night — not the Steinway, the school grand, scarred but in tune — and ran a scale without sound, fingers on the keys, breath held.</p>
<p>Patty stood in the wings with her guitar strap over her shoulder and felt the thunder move closer — a real roll this time, east to west, the building's old bones carrying it through the concrete rake.</p>
<p>Not hope. Pressure.</p>
<p>The sky outside the lobby doors had gone green at the edges.</p>
<p>Chuck Morrison's voice would be hot on the mic by seven. Maxanne Sartori was already somewhere in the building with a clipboard and a pencil and a styrofoam cup of WBCN coffee. Eight bands. Five hundred dollars. A town that had turned music into sides.</p>
<p>Nick adjusted the strap. Jackie rolled his sleeves. Patty tuned the B string once because the B string was always the one.</p>
<p>The doors kept opening. The heat kept rising. The thunder kept not breaking — held, loaded, waiting for the downbeat nobody in Sudbury had agreed to share.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 32: "BARRACUDA"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Seven o'clock. May 16, 1979.</p>
<p>The auditorium had filled while the sky outside went green at the edges — four hundred and twenty-seven bodies in four hundred and forty-three seats, the empty chairs in the back like teeth missing from a grin. Popcorn butter and Aqua Velva and girls' perfume layered over floor wax and adolescent heat. The balcony groaned when the seniors stood to wave at other seniors. Somewhere in the lobby the booster club's cash box clicked.</p>
<p>Chuck Morrison tapped the microphone once. The tap came back through the house PA like a door closing.</p>
<p>"Good evening, Lincoln-Sudbury." A good-natured roar. "First ever WBCN Battle of the Bands, brought to you by the Rock of Boston and by Strawberries Records and Tapes, where for seven ninety-nine you can take home an album that will change your life or your sister's." He let the laugh build and cut it off. "Eight bands. Five hundred dollars. Recording time on Boylston Street. Let's go."</p>
<p>The judges' table sat stage-left, draped in black cloth, four folding chairs behind it.</p>
<p>Maxanne Sartori had been in the building since noon — leather jacket over a plain shirt, jeans, boots, styrofoam cup of WBCN coffee with the red logo still on it. Clipboard on her knees. Pencil sharpened to a point that would snap before the night was over. On page three of the packet, Article 7, Section 3: *In the event of a tie in the final round, tied contestants shall share the studio recording session equally.* Sponsor boilerplate. Boring line. She had added it at her kitchen table in January before she knew any names from Sudbury.</p>
<p>Beside her Carter Alan took notes like a man filing taxes. Bob Walker from Strawberries had forearms laid across a second clipboard as if selecting the draft. Neither of them had read page three. Maxanne had. That was the difference between guilt and paperwork.</p>
<p>Chuck introduced her and the room gave the warmest hand of the night — half of Sudbury had heard her on 104.1 before bed. Carter got applause. Bob Walker got the loudest — studio time, album contract, the kind of future a kid could carry out of a gymnasium in a cardboard box.</p>
<p>Grace Johnson sat third row left in the navy dress with the buttons, winter coat folded over one arm, programs folded once in her lap, purse on top, hands on top of the purse. Friendly's had let her off at four. Tom Andrews's WBCN button caught the cove light when she shifted.</p>
<p>Three rows back on the aisle, Dr. Richard Marsh sat gray suit, pressed shirt, no tie loosened, palms flat on his knees, thumbs in line with the seam of his trousers. The way he sat at department meetings.</p>
<p>Along the rake the waiting arranged itself without a map. Leather and denim colonized the back rows, boots on armrests. Grease under the fingernails leaned against the side wall near the shop-corridor door because folding seats put the ache in the wrong part of the back. Study-hall kids held the first two rows in neat sections, pens out as if there would be a quiz. Nobody had planned it. Bodies just went where they recognized themselves.</p>
<p>Backstage, Nick Johnson stood at the stage-right gap in the velvet curtain where the hem didn't reach the floor.</p>
<p>Through the slit he watched Chuck hold up the lineup card and read the first slot — no band name, just *Lincoln-Sudbury freshmen, take it away,* the way you throw chum to see what rises.</p>
<p>Four kids walked on with borrowed gear and faces too young to be embarrassed yet. The bass player — tall, wire-rim glasses, Route 217 haircut — missed the turnaround twice on the opening number and kept going anyway, sweet and wrong, the way you keep going when the room hasn't decided if you're food yet. Nick had seen him once at a register on Route 217 buying strings he couldn't afford. *You're in the Battle, right?* The kid had nodded like a war was a weather report.</p>
<p>The room clapped because it was a start.</p>
<p>Nick did not look at Jackie at the far wing. Jackie did not look at Nick. Patty Lally stood behind the Steinway case with her Martin on the strap and counted the claps the way you count seconds between lightning and thunder.</p>
<p>Detroit Rock City came from Acton-Boxborough in greasepaint and platform boots, a blood packet gone off early in the first verse, a fire-breather reduced to steam by the custodian's nervous glance at the sprinkler heads. The first five rows loved them for trying. The balcony loved them for the mess. Chuck flicked the house lights twice when the smoke got thick and they landed it into applause that was kind and a little exhausted.</p>
<p>The Dead Heads — Wayland, patchouli, Birkenstocks — opened warm and rolled toward a long jam the back half of the balcony lost around minute seven. Kids drifted to the lobby for popcorn, to the smoking corner outside the fire doors, to the bathroom in groups of three because nobody went alone. The house mood wasn't electric yet. It was **waiting** — circling, patient, the way something with teeth holds still in clear water before it moves.</p>
<p>Talking Heads Talking from Boston Latin wore matching thrift-store suits and played "Psycho Killer" with clinical tightness. A whole row of older brothers in the back threw their hands up, delighted — Rats in training, recognizing precision when it wasn't theirs. The southwest section murmured assent. The northeast section answered with volume, not applause — a rumble in the chest, boots on floor, the first real split in the room's single body.</p>
<p>Maxanne wrote a number in a little box. Originality weighted double. She wrote another number. Stage presence. The pencil moved faster than she wanted it to.</p>
<p>By the end of the fourth band Chuck Morrison had sweat on his upper lip and the WBCN banner behind him had started to peel at one corner where the duct tape gave up.</p>
<p>He read the next name from the card and grinned at the room.</p>
<p>"When we come back — Sudbury. The part you've been waiting for."</p>
<p>House lights to half. The balcony stood first. Popcorn line in the lobby. Outside the fire doors, cigarettes flared in the green light before the storm.</p>
<p>Backstage the locals became three separate weather systems.</p>
<p>Nick Johnson stood at the stage-right gap with Stephen Murphy's hand on his shoulder — not comfort, anchor. Mark checked the Rhodes mute. David tightened the high hat for the fourth time since seven o'clock.</p>
<p>"Crown Jewels first," Stephen said. "We're after them."</p>
<p>Nick nodded. He did not look across the stage to where Jackie Marsh had his palm on the Yamaha fallboard without making a sound. Jackie did not look at Nick. Forty feet of wing and velvet and gaffer's tape between them — linoleum's indoor cousin.</p>
<p>Patty Lally tuned the B string once because the B string was always the one. Robin leaned against the amp case. Steve Bankuti counted change in his pocket like a nervous metronome. Rumours was two slots out. That was far enough to breathe and close enough to choke on.</p>
<p>Out in the house Grace Johnson's hand stayed closed on the folded programs. Richard Marsh did not move. The thunder that had been holding all day finally broke somewhere west of Worcester — not here, not yet, just a long roll through the auditorium's concrete rake, pendant lights swaying a millimeter, a metronome nobody had asked for.</p>
<p>Maxanne Sartori flipped to page three of the packet. Article 7, Section 3. Boring line. She closed the clipboard.</p>
<p>The opening acts had been the small fish — freshmen, greasepaint, patchouli, thrift-store suits — and the room had clapped the way you clap a warm-up band, polite, already leaning toward what came next. Strawberries logo on the judges' table. WBCN red on the banner. Five hundred dollars on Chuck's tongue. The water had gone from clear to stocked. The teeth were waiting in the wings.</p>
<p>Barracuda don't stay for the opening act.</p>
<p>When the house lights came back up, Chuck Morrison would call a name from Sudbury. The painted maple backdrop would still hang behind the empty mic stands — red gone brown at the edges, October forever — and four hundred and twenty-seven people would decide which side of Hop Brook they were on before a single local note got played.</p>
<p>Nick breathed in. Jackie breathed out. Patty's pick rested on the string without moving.</p>
<p>The room leaned forward.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 33: "SOMEBODY TO LOVE"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>House lights up. Ten minutes.</p>
<p>Jackie Marsh sat on a metal folding chair backstage with the Martin D-28 across his thighs and tuned the low E by ear.</p>
<p>The guitar was there because it kept his hands busy. He had decided a week ago he would not carry it out. The D-28 would stay on its stand. He would walk on and go to the school's Yamaha, and the Yamaha would be the only instrument four hundred and twenty-seven people saw him touch. His father had said nothing about it either way, which had been its own kind of pressure.</p>
<p>The A off the D. The E off the A. One string at a time, patient, the spring in the tuning peg resisting just so before it gave.</p>
<p>Elizabeth stood beside a road case playing G-major scales into her chest, bow moving, eyes on nothing, a pink crease at the left side of her neck where the violin sat. Max Wesslen plucked the open A on the Precision and moved a peg a sixteenth of a turn. Ace Callahan ran one stick around the rim of a practice pad — tick tick tick tick, the dry small clock of a drummer's nerves.</p>
<p>Footsteps on the wing stairs. A measured tread.</p>
<p>"Jackie."</p>
<p>Dr. Richard Marsh stood in the curtain of the stage-left wing. He did not come all the way in. He stopped at the Steinway case, and his right hand — without Jackie seeing — came down and rested for half a second on the polished lid, wide and long-fingered and steady, the hand that had not played since before Jackie was born. He took it off again before he spoke.</p>
<p>"Fine?"</p>
<p>"Fine," Jackie said. "Just nervous."</p>
<p>"Good." Dr. Marsh let the silence do the next thing. Then, very quiet: "Play it the way you hear it." He turned and went back down the stairs to the house.</p>
<p>Jackie stood where he was after the footsteps were gone. His father had never said that sentence before. His father did not know he had said it now.</p>
<p>He laid his own hand flat on the lid where the hand had been. Warm. He took it away.</p>
<p>Someone called three minutes. Jackie set the Martin on its stand. Cold air in through the nose, warm out through the mouth — his father's trick for the pre-concert body. Eleven years of it.</p>
<p>"Hey," he said.</p>
<p>Ace, Max, Elizabeth looked up.</p>
<p>"We're playing Queen because we love Queen. That's it."</p>
<p>"What if we don't win," Ace said.</p>
<p>"Then we don't win."</p>
<p>"A-flat on the second," Max said.</p>
<p>"A-flat on the second."</p>
<p>Elizabeth tightened the horsehair a quarter turn and did not say anything.</p>
<p>One minute. Ace went through the blacks toward his kit. Max slung the Precision. Elizabeth followed with the violin at her side. Jackie walked past the Martin without looking at it.</p>
<p>Chuck Morrison's voice rolled down from the PA.</p>
<p>"Ladies and gentlemen. From Sudbury, Massachusetts — Crown Jewels."</p>
<p>The cove lights brightened. Applause — uneven, already sorted, southwest loud, northeast answering with volume instead of hands.</p>
<p>The house was bigger from the stage than it had looked from the floor. The lights cleaned depth and left a hot white wall — first three rows as faces, maybe four, past that charcoal haze. Jackie walked to the Yamaha. Half-open on the short stick. He had played this piano for the spring recital in March and the district assembly in April. His father had helped the school buy it in 1976. He had never thought of it as his before tonight.</p>
<p>He sat. Middle C without sound. The key cool. Key-dip two millimeters deeper than the Steinway upstairs. Hammer action leaning a hair to the treble — felted badly, maintenance filing once a year with sandpaper whose grit nobody asked about. A fact. You sit on a bad floor anyway.</p>
<p>Third row aisle: Dr. Richard Marsh a shape in a good suit, dark on dark. Hands on his knees or in his lap — impossible to tell from the bench. Jackie would not know what his father's face did tonight. That would have to be all right.</p>
<p>Elizabeth raised the violin. Ace clicked four on the sticks.</p>
<p>"Killer Queen" came out clean.</p>
<p>Sixteenths in the right hand, music-box clockwork rehearsed twenty times in the Marsh basement. He let the Yamaha's treble lean work for him, figures bright and close to the fallboard where the hammers spoke easier. Max walked the roots under Ace's brushes. Elizabeth threaded countermelody an octave above where the horn would have lived on the record.</p>
<p>The southwest section made the sound a crowd makes when expectation gets delivered — front-of-chest murmur, assent. Jackie did not look up. Last chord, pedal hung a breath longer than needed, the hall keeping the note and then not. Applause arrived as the note died.</p>
<p>He did not stand.</p>
<p>Nod to Elizabeth. A-flat.</p>
<p>Max found the root on the E string fifth fret, held under his palm. Ace to brushes. Jackie put his left hand on the A-flat second inversion, intro chord, first line into the cardioid at the piano shell. The floor monitor tilted too far back — he leaned to catch his own return. Fine.</p>
<p>"Killer Queen" was jewelry. This was a church.</p>
<p>Left hand into the big octave roll under the verse. Right hand answering up the stacked thirds. Ace's brushes to sticks at the pre-chorus. Max walking under on the off-beats. Elizabeth did not double the melody — he had said no in rehearsal. On the second chorus she played a long bowed descant in fifth position, eight bars, narrowing the field to a spotlight and widening it when she cut out. The southwest section went quiet through it. Jackie did not understand why until he was most of the way through the bridge and realized they were **listening**.</p>
<p>The climb into the last chorus is the climb into the last chorus. The voice has to go up. It has to break a little or it is a lie.</p>
<p>He hit the note on *find me somebody to love* and his voice cracked on the second syllable.</p>
<p>He heard it crack.</p>
<p>His left hand did not adjust. The note held anyway.</p>
<p>Down off the line into the vamp. Max up a fifth. Ace on the ride. Elizabeth's bow line back from under. Last twelve bars the way they had ridden them in Memorial Congregational on Tuesday — except the pews were four hundred folding seats and the lemon-oil smell was floor wax. Jackie lifted the sustain pedal on the downbeat and killed the Yamaha clean. Ace caught the crash. Elizabeth pulled the bow off. Cymbal decay alone for four seconds before the room knew it could clap.</p>
<p>Third row aisle: Dr. Richard Marsh's left hand opened once in his lap and closed again. Nobody beside him saw it. Grace Johnson, three rows forward, had both hands on her purse and did not let go.</p>
<p>Maxanne Sartori wrote a number in the originality box and underlined it twice.</p>
<p>The hall did not come up all at once. Back first. Then front. Then middle. Not thunder — four hundred separate decisions. Jackie sat at the bench with his hands shaking on his thighs. Sweat at his collar. The A-flat key under his left thumb still warm.</p>
<p>He stood. Elizabeth was already off stage-right. Ace carried the snare. Max slung the bass. Jackie followed through the wing curtain — cooler air, fluorescent backstage, the Martin D-28 on its stand where he had left it. He walked past without looking. It had done its job: the thing he did not pick up.</p>
<p>Stephen Murphy's voice, stage-right, not loud: "Walking the Line. On deck."</p>
<p>Nick Johnson somewhere behind the velvet with a guitar on the strap and a throat gone dry did not answer.</p>
<p>Jackie kept walking.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 34: "TRAIN KEPT A-ROLLIN'"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>He was in the wings when Jackie's voice cracked.</p>
<p>Nick had been watching the gap between the curtain and the proscenium wall — a slice of stage, the back of Jackie's head at the Yamaha — running the set in his head the way a guy runs a road before he drives it. Then Jackie hit the note in *Somebody to Love* and the note did not come out the way eleven years of lessons had built it to come out. It cracked. The auditorium did the thing four hundred and twenty-seven people do when they are holding one breath together — a half-second where nobody coughed.</p>
<p>Nick felt it in the knuckle of his left hand.</p>
<p>He had come up the side stairs planning to drop a hammer — louder, faster, dirtier — and what had just happened out there was not something a hammer could answer. The set was the set. Four songs.</p>
<p>Crown Jewels came off the way bands come off a stage they had just done the thing on.</p>
<p>Jackie passed him six feet out, did not look at him. Max Wesslen touched Nick's shoulder with two fingers going by — not comfort, acknowledgment, the bass player's whole vocabulary. Nick did not try to say what that meant.</p>
<p>"Walking the Line," the stage manager said, not loud. "On deck."</p>
<p>The Tele was Mr. Wentworth's. Late-sixties butterscotch blonde, clearcoat rubbed off where the teacher's forearm had lain for twenty years. Wentworth had met him at the music-room door at seven and handed over the gig bag without taking it out. *Back Friday.* Two steps away, over his shoulder: *The neck'll pull sharp under the lights — keep your ear on the B.* Nick had not trusted his voice to say thanks.</p>
<p>Least-torn jeans. Black t-shirt with nothing on it. Dan's work boots, a size too big, two pairs of socks, the left heel lifting a quarter-inch when he walked.</p>
<p>Nick rolled his neck once. Closed his left hand, opened it. The pads on his fingertips met the strings and stopped. Still his hand.</p>
<p>Stephen Murphy at his shoulder. David behind the Ludwig with the cracked crash riveted by a brass washer. Mark at the Rhodes that buzzed on the B below middle C, thumb already off that key.</p>
<p>They walked through the curtain-break.</p>
<p>Heat first.</p>
<p>Four par-cans twelve feet up, putting light down the way a kitchen window in August puts the sun down. Scalp sweating before the monitor. Somebody else's hairspray and gaffer's tape and Lysol on the microphone grille. First three rows pale ovals; past that, dark.</p>
<p>Grace Johnson third row left. Purse on her lap. Both hands on the clasp.</p>
<p>Stephen's Univox hummed, cord curled at his ankle. Nick plugged the Tele into the Fender Twin on loan from the band room. Low E with the side of his thumb. The amp said yes. Volume to seven. Tone to eight. He stepped to the mic. Did not lean in.</p>
<p>"Thanks."</p>
<p>One word. He stepped back. Did not look at the wings where Jackie had gone.</p>
