<p>David counted under his breath — *one-two-three-four* — and Nick dropped the opening riff of *Walk This Way* on the downbeat.</p>
<p>Joe Perry's thing — three fingers, pick on the downstroke, muted chug between accents, accent on the two-and. You did not ease into *Walk This Way*. It came in at your waist and lifted. Stephen's bass underneath on the second bar. David's kick tucked in. Mark laid a Rhodes chord on top like a sheet going over a bed.</p>
<p>Nick did not sing the verses. Tyler's syllables lived in a mouth nobody on this stage had. He carried the vocal line on the top two strings, octaved, syllable-tight; the chorus he hit with open A and let the band find him. The northeast section came up on the third bar. A kid in front yelled something that was not a word.</p>
<p>Tag. David hit the crash. The brass washer rattled against the bronze. The cymbal sounded like a trash-can lid. The house was loud.</p>
<p>Nick waited for it to come down. Did not speak. Turned the volume knob a half-notch. Looked at Stephen.</p>
<p>Stephen started *Sweet Emotion*.</p>
<p>The descending figure Tom Hamilton built the song on — low G, F-sharp, E, D. Stephen had practiced it in North Sudbury for a month and played it now like he had owned it his whole life. Nick let him walk alone four bars. Mark laid a Rhodes pad under, barely there. The house got quiet because the song wanted quiet.</p>
<p>Then the talk-box line without a talk-box. No Heil on Dutton Road. Tone knob halfway off, borrowed wah under his boot at sweep-middle. Fingered the line on D and G, rocked the toe a quarter-inch — not Perry's gear, a nasal rub, a close-mouthed vowel. It worked because it had to work.</p>
<p>Third row: Grace let go of the purse clasp. Hands flat in her lap.</p>
<p>Nick did not look again. The song demanded the fretboard.</p>
<p>Break and out the far end. Final crash. Feedback off the Tele caught the Twin and squealed high and clean. Nick's palm down on all six strings at the bridge — squeal died mid-note. Palm stung.</p>
<p>He nodded at Mark. Mark laid the one-bar figure for the next one on the Rhodes.</p>
<p>Nick hit the riff.</p>
<p>*Train Kept A-Rollin'.*</p>
<p>Not *keep*. **Kept.** Dan had the vinyl label right. Stephen had said *keep* in rehearsal twice and Nick hadn't corrected him because rehearsal wasn't the point.</p>
<p>The Aerosmith version — heavier, meaner, stutter on the downbeat Perry built off Jeff Beck and made his own. Stutter in the pick hand. Heel of the right palm muted, riff out of the Twin like sheet metal hit with a tire iron in rhythm. Stephen locked on. David's kick doubled the stutter. Mark stayed off the B.</p>
<p>Halfway through the second verse the tremor started in his left pinkie — the shake from Sunday rehearsals he hadn't told anybody about. He fingered it out by tightening his grip a half-pound. The train kept rolling. Copper at the back of his throat.</p>
<p>Double-time out. David's crash. The cracked cymbal rattled again. Nick stood with the Tele against his hip, chest heaving.</p>
<p>Three down. One left.</p>
<p>Mark played the piano intro of *Dream On* on the Rhodes that buzzed on the B below middle C.</p>
<p>Not a piano — a bell in the top, a roll an upright didn't roll. Mark's version on borrowed gear with a buzzing B, the thing Nick had been hearing since the summer he was fourteen in the garage on a nicked-up first album.</p>
<p>Nick came in on the open A.</p>
<p>No singing. Vocal line on the Tele, single-note, clean, no wah — Wentworth's butterscotch handling the melody the way Grace handled a plate she didn't want to break. Stephen to whole notes. David to the ride. Mark off the B.</p>
<p>His eye traveled when he didn't want it to. Third row left. Navy dress. Purse in her lap. Hands flat on the fabric the way they had been at the kitchen table the morning after Beechwood, waiting for the next thing.</p>
<p>She was looking at him. Not looking away.</p>
<p>Four hundred people and he held her there for the length of the solo's pickup bar. Any longer and he would not finish. He looked back down at the fretboard.</p>
<p>The solo. The Dan solo.</p>
<p>Dan in the garage one Sunday when Nick was fourteen, putting fingering into his left hand with a hand that had hefted ninety-pound bags of Quikrete and turned a wrench on Fords since 1952. *Thumb anchored, Nicky. Bend on the second. Breathe on the pause.*</p>
<p>Slow. Dan's way. The bend on the second fret bit the fingertip — Tele strings lighter than the acoustic's, cut different. He let the bite happen.</p>
<p>Out of the solo. Band rode the chord. Mark on the Rhodes. Stephen long. David wash on the crash.</p>
<p>Nick did not sing *Dream on, dream on*. No Steven Tyler on the Lincoln-Sudbury stage. He played the last chorus melody on the top string, clean, through the par-can haze, down off the high note to the A. Mark closed on the Rhodes chord. David let the ride die on its own.</p>
<p>Half a second the house was quiet the way Jackie had made it quiet.</p>
<p>Then it came.</p>
<p>Not the *Walk This Way* applause — something else. Wide, uneven, northeast loud first, then spreading. Nick stood with the Tele against his hip. Did not lift his arm. Nodded once, not at anybody in particular.</p>
<p>He unplugged. Laid the Tele flat on the Twin, strings up, the way Wentworth had shown him. Stephen said something with *Nicky* in it and clapped his shoulder.</p>
<p>Nick walked off stage left.</p>
<p>The stage manager had a clipboard and a headset on one ear. Did not look up.</p>
<p>"Ready Rumours," he said into the headset.</p>
<p>Nick walked past. Left hand shaking a small shake. Both hands in his jeans pockets.</p>
<p>Stage-right wing: Jackie standing the way Jackie stood when he was trying not to be seen, black piano jacket still buttoned, hands at his sides. Forty feet of velvet and gaffer tape between them.</p>
<p>Nick did not look twice.</p>
<p>Somewhere outside the lobby doors thunder finally broke over Sudbury — not applause, not a train, just weather moving in after a day that had been holding its breath since seven o'clock.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 35: "GO YOUR OWN WAY"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Chuck Morrison read the card.</p>
<p>"Slot seven. From the southeast corner of Sudbury — where the bacon pays the mortgage — Rumours."</p>
<p>Applause polite at first. Then louder when Patty walked on barefoot, boots in her left hand, Martin case in her right, flannel over the Rumours T-shirt, pig on her clothes whether the room knew it or not.</p>
<p>She kicked the boots under the riser edge and stepped up. Stage wood warm where the lights had been cooking it two hours. Sweat and amp dust and burnt-resistor tang from Nick's set still hanging in the air. She set the Martin on the strap — spruce top darkened with age, scratch along the lower bout catching the gel light the blue of a bruise. Soft case open behind her amp, shoelace through the broken pull tab trailing over the riser.</p>
<p>Robin Bosshard came up left with the bass. Kristy Wesslen took the second mic and the acoustic. Steve Bankuti was already behind the kit — snare stand turned a quarter-inch, sticks crossed on his knee, kick pedal with the hinge that squeaked on beat two like his father's truck door.</p>
<p>Patty leaned into the mic. Did not say anything. Tuned the B string. B was always the one. Nodded at Robin.</p>
<p>Stage-left wing: Nick leaning on a flight case, towel around his neck, hands in his pockets. Stage-right: Jackie standing when he was trying not to be seen, black piano jacket buttoned, hands at his sides.</p>
<p>Patty saw both of them the way you see a thing you are not going to look at twice. She looked back at the neck of the guitar.</p>
<p>*Now here you go again, you say you want your freedom.*</p>
<p>She held the word *go* — not show-off long, long enough. C chord clean under it, ring finger on the third fret where it had buzzed Monday in Memorial Congregational and she had laughed out loud at herself. The auditorium came down off the Aerosmith ride. A booster-club mother halfway down the aisle with a tray of Cokes stopped mid-stride and did not put the tray down. Row H: a man put his arm around his wife's shoulder and left it there.</p>
<p>Patty let the held note fall into the next line.</p>
<p>No talk between songs. B string again. Robin on a tuning peg. Kristy sipped water. Steve wiped palms on his jeans. The room waited the way a room waits when it has agreed, without anyone saying so, that the person on stage gets to choose the pace.</p>
<p>She'd cut *Rhiannon* at breakfast. Her mother had set down the coffee pot and Patty had said *not tonight* out loud to the kitchen and her mother had nodded without asking why. Three songs. She'd told the band in the dressing room ten minutes ago. Nobody argued.</p>
<p>Count-in for *Silver Springs.*</p>
<p>Learned last week in the basement of Memorial Congregational — Kristy's dubbed cassette, four parish women who came Thursdays to arrange altar flowers standing in the doorway with hands in cardigan pockets, listening. Grandmother in a Sudbury Southeast kitchen in 1954, radio on the Frigidaire. Mother in the same kitchen in 1966 with a baby on her hip.</p>
<p>Long held second chorus — *you'll never get away from the sound of the woman that loves you* — Patty was not thinking about Nick. Not thinking about Jackie. Thinking about the four women in the church basement and the two women in her kitchen. Private inheritance in her hands going out through the mic. The note bloomed. Kristy came in a third below on the second *loves you*, voices braided. Steve's ride shimmered under like water over flat stones.</p>
<p>Row H: the man's face in his hand. House lights half up so the judges could take notes — Patty could see the first fifteen rows as clearly as her own fingers. Chorus fell. She stepped back off the mic. B string one more time.</p>
<p>Robin looked at her. Patty nodded.</p>
<p>Steve's count-in — four clean clicks of maple on maple, in her head since November.</p>
<p>Bass on the Lindsey Buckingham pulse. Kristy's rhythm guitar on the one. Patty opened her mouth.</p>
<p>*Loving you / isn't the right thing to do.*</p>
<p>The hum had lived under her breath seven months — in Wentworth's room when Nick's amp was plugged in and Jackie's hands were on the keys, in the barn when the sows sighed, folded with seven letters in the camp notebook. Now it came all the way out. Not a fragment. The whole song, full voice, bare feet on warm wood, Martin D-28 on a hand-me-down strap.</p>
<p>First chorus: the room found her. Second chorus: Kristy's harmony over her like a second sky, Robin walking the Buckingham line, Steve's snare on two and four like a carpenter driving a nail — one hit, clean, no flourish.</p>
<p>One beat: Nick at the back of stage-left. One beat: Jackie at the back of stage-right. Not looking at each other. Both looking at her. She did not hold either look. Back to her fingers.</p>
<p>*You can go your own way. Go your own way.*</p>
<p>Bridge. Last chorus. Last chord. Flat of her right palm on the strings.</p>
<p>One beat of silence.</p>
<p>Then it came.</p>
<p>Not polite. Rows standing in a wave from the middle outward. Four hundred throats, twice that many hands. Something shouted near the back — her name or just a sound. The booster mother clapping with the Coke tray balanced on her forearm.</p>
<p>Patty lifted the Martin off her shoulder. Knelt on the riser. Laid it in the case, zipper as far as it would go, shoelace through the pull tab — the knot she used on the barn gate. Boots by the laces in her left hand. Case in her right.</p>
<p>Off stage-left past the monitor, past the flight case Nick had moved, into the dim wing.</p>
<p>Kristy put a hand on her shoulder. Two seconds. Took it off.</p>
<p>Steve did not know what to do with his face. "Hi."</p>
<p>"Hi," Patty said.</p>
<p>She stood with her boots in her hand and listened to the applause do what applause did when it was not going to stop on its own — go on until somebody on the crew brought the house lights up the rest of the way.</p>
<p>Somewhere down the hall the judges' room door was still closed.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 36: "STAIRWAY TO HEAVEN"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>The Regulators had cleared the stage forty minutes ago — Framingham kids, loud, forgotten by the time Rumours walked on barefoot.</p>
<p>Now the guidance-office conference room off the main hall smelled like burned coffee and indecision. Four metal folding chairs around a formica table. Fluorescent tube on uneven ballast, low hum under the voices like a note somebody had left on. Coffee urn brewed at five, color of wet tar. Sloppy joe smell when the door cracked. Nobody had cracked it in twenty minutes.</p>
<p>Maxanne Sartori sat with her back to the door, fingers on a styrofoam cup she hadn't drunk from. Carter Alan leaned on his elbows, jaw working. Bob Walker from Strawberries stood at the window watching the parking lot fill with headlights.</p>
<p>"We've been at this forty minutes," Carter said.</p>
<p>"I'm aware." Maxanne set the cup down. A ring bloomed on the table.</p>
<p>"Walking the Line," Chuck Morrison's voice wasn't in the room — he was stacking chairs in the auditorium — but his scores were. Carter tapped the legal pad. "Johnson's *Dream On* — that wasn't a cover. That was a conversation."</p>
<p>"Crown Jewels," Bob said from the window, not turning. "Marsh on *Somebody to Love*. Session control. The sister on violin — architecture, not rock."</p>
<p>"Rumours," Carter said. "The Lally girl. You felt that room."</p>
<p>Bob nodded once. "Felt it. Not a tie between three bands. Between two sides of town."</p>
<p>Maxanne had been quiet through most of it. Clipboard on her knees. Technical execution. Arrangement. Stage presence. Originality weighted double — language she and Tom had written in January so discovery could win on paper. Also weighted so neither side of Hop Brook would win clean on arithmetic alone. She had known that going in.</p>
<p>The numbers had landed close. The room outside was already humming with sides.</p>
<p>"Can you honestly pick one?" she said.</p>
<p>Carter looked at her. Bob turned from the window. The fluorescent buzz filled the space where an answer should have gone.</p>
<p>Carter shook his head.</p>
<p>Bob looked at his hands. "No."</p>
<p>Maxanne pulled the packet from her bag — photocopied on the Lincoln-Sudbury office machine, toner fading at the margins. Page three. Article 7, Section 3.</p>
<p>*In the event of a tie in the final round, tied contestants shall share the studio recording session equally, collaborating on material of their choosing.*</p>
<p>Boring line. Sponsor-friendly. She had added it at her kitchen table in January, before she knew Nick Johnson's name, before she met Jackie Marsh in a garage on Dutton Road in March. An exit for a room that might need one. Not a trap aimed at two boys who didn't exist yet for her.</p>
<p>She saw it now anyway.</p>
<p>"Shared studio time," she said. "Split prize. Per the packet — collaborative use or forfeit."</p>
<p>"That's not judging," Carter said. "That's paperwork."</p>
<p>"Yes," Maxanne said. "It is."</p>
<p>Silence filled the room the way water fills a glass — completely, with nowhere for air to go.</p>
<p>"Then it's a tie," Bob said.</p>
<p>Maxanne did not recuse herself. She did not throw out the clause. She did not pretend she didn't see it.</p>
<p>She wanted them both in Studio C. She wanted the photograph. She wanted the story. She used the machinery.</p>
<p>The auditorium held four hundred and twenty-seven people and sounded like twice that.</p>
<p>Nick Johnson stood stage left with Stephen Murphy, David Cassel, and Mark Pianka behind him. Wentworth's butterscotch Tele on the strap. Jackie Marsh stood stage right — Elizabeth with her violin case at her thigh, Ace and Max flanking him.</p>
<p>Between the bands: twelve feet of stage boards taped in black gaffer, and a single Shure SM58 on its chrome stand, cable coiled at the base.</p>
<p>Maxanne walked to the microphone. House noise dropped to murmur, then breathing.</p>
<p>"This has been one of the finest competitions I've witnessed." The FM warmth that had introduced Aerosmith to Boston in '73 came out like she'd never left the booth. "Both bands performed at a level that would be remarkable for professionals. For high school musicians, it borders on the extraordinary."</p>
<p>Pause. Dead air the way a drummer uses space between beats — the long climb before the verse you're not sure you've earned.</p>
<p>"The judges' decision is unanimous. The 1979 WBCN Battle of the Bands ends in a tie."</p>
<p>The auditorium inhaled.</p>
<p>Not cheering. Not booing. The confused electric sound of four hundred people processing something that wasn't in the script. Murmur, scattered applause, murmur again. A whistle from the back, high and piercing.</p>
<p>"Prize money of five hundred dollars split equally. Two hundred and fifty to Walking the Line. Two hundred and fifty to Crown Jewels."</p>
<p>She held up the contract. Paper caught the par-can light.</p>
<p>"Recording time at WBCN Studio C on Boylston Street — shared. Per the contract, used collaboratively by both bands — or forfeited entirely."</p>
<p>Now the murmuring stopped. Now the room understood.</p>
<p>Nick's head turned toward Jackie. Jackie's head turned toward Nick. Twelve feet of boards. Microphone at knee height. Light through the margins of the paper Maxanne held.</p>
<p>"Gentlemen. Come forward and sign the award acknowledgment."</p>
<p>They walked from opposite directions and met at the mic like two tides that didn't want to touch. Maxanne held the contract between them. Nick took the left edge. Jackie took the right. Shoulder to shoulder, same piece of paper, neither looking at the other.</p>
<p>Two hundred and fifty dollars. Grace could use it. The studio came with a chain: shared, collaborative, with Jackie Marsh, who had not spoken to him since the funeral.</p>
<p>Jackie stared at the words. His father would tell him to forfeit. Walk away clean. Take the money and the moral victory and pack for Juilliard.</p>
<p>They signed. Separate pens. Same line.</p>
<p>Third row left: Grace Johnson stood.</p>
<p>She didn't applaud. She didn't shout. Straight-backed, dry-eyed — the iron that got her through double shifts and mortgage payments and PTA votes. Navy dress with the buttons. Winter coat folded over the seat behind her. She would not wear this dress again after tonight.</p>
<p>Her son on stage holding a contract with the Marsh boy. Dan in her head, clear as one seat to the right: *Take it. We need it.*</p>
<p>Grace sat. Hands folded in her lap. Waited.</p>
<p>Three rows back on the aisle: Dr. Richard Marsh unclenched his hands.</p>
<p>Red grooves across his palms where the armrests had been. He looked up at Jackie in the stage lights with someone else's contract in his hand — one flashing second of himself at twenty-two, fallboard lowering, sheet music into a box sealed before sundown. The rational choice.</p>
<p>The pinky of his left hand uncurled once, an octave-reach his thumb had not been asked to make since he was twenty-two. He closed his hands again. Gently. The marks would fade.</p>
<p>Back row: Patty Lally sat alone.</p>
<p>She hadn't won. Her name wasn't on the contract. Wouldn't be in the Sudbury Town Crier tomorrow. Wouldn't be mentioned at Friendly's when the town replayed the Battle.</p>
<p>She had played *Go Your Own Way* barefoot with her eyes closed and her voice wide open, and for four minutes the auditorium had belonged to her — a girl from the pig farms who sang Fleetwood Mac because Stevie Nicks knew what it meant to love something already leaving.</p>
<p>She hummed it now. Quiet. *Go your own way.*</p>
<p>People filed out. Suburbans and pickups and Volvos in the lot. Families on the sidewalk, animated — everyone had an opinion, no one sure what had happened.</p>
<p>Patty didn't move. She'd wait until the auditorium emptied. Then walk.</p>
<p>Outside, rain finally broke over Sudbury — not the held thunder from seven o'clock, real rain on new maple leaves, sodium lamps throwing shadow on wet asphalt. Peepers from the dump half a mile west, thin silver on the low air.</p>
<p>Nick and Jackie came out separate doors, contract folded between them like a custody agreement for something that hadn't been born yet. Kitchen windows lit down the hill. Route 20 hiss east toward Wayland.</p>
<p>Two hundred and fifty dollars would pay an insurance bill somewhere. Two hundred and fifty would sit in a savings account thick with Juilliard money, barely noticed.</p>
<p>A hundred hours of studio time hung in the air between them, unclaimed.</p>
<p>Grace came out the lobby with her coat over her arm, walked to the wood-paneled wagon in the second row. Dome light on, off. Engine caught on the second turn. She sat with hands on the wheel before she put it in gear.</p>
<p>On Route 20 a quarter mile east, Patty was already walking — boots in her left hand, guitar case in her right, hum in her throat.</p>
<p>From a car pulling out of the lot, two bars of Led Zeppelin bled through an open window before the door shut and the rain swallowed it. *Stairway to Heaven.* The intro climbing. Then gone.</p>
<p>Nick stood under the shop-corridor overhang with the contract in his jacket pocket. Jackie stood under the east entrance awning with his in his. Forty feet of wet pavement between them.</p>
<p>Neither crossed it.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 37: "THE BOYS ARE BACK IN TOWN"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Stephen Murphy's truck smelled like wet retriever and gear oil. Nick sat in the passenger seat with the window cracked two inches. May air, damp after the rain — fog on the inside of the glass. Sodium lights made the wet Lincoln-Sudbury lot look like a record under a needle.</p>
<p>Grace had taken the wagon home twenty minutes ago. Navy dress, Friendly's double tomorrow, exhausted. *Come home when you come home.* Touch on his elbow. Gone.</p>
<p>Stephen was inside helping David load the Ludwig. Nick had said he'd wait. He did not want the auditorium handshakes — the *great job* from people who meant it and the *great job* from people who didn't.</p>
<p>A tie. He rolled the word against his teeth. Two hundred and fifty dollars. The wagon's transmission slipping in second since March. The money would delay it, not fix it.</p>
<p>Knuckles on the passenger window.</p>
<p>Nick's hand went to the lock. Jackie Marsh stood outside in his father's peacoat, collar up, breath ghosting. The coat Dr. Marsh had worn on Saturday mornings when they were twelve and the world was simple.</p>
<p>Nick rolled the window down three inches.</p>
<p>Jackie held up the contract, paper softening at the folds. "We have to decide about the studio time."</p>
<p>"You take your half."</p>
<p>"Doesn't work that way." He turned the contract toward the dash light. "*Shared.* Together or forfeit."</p>
<p>"Then we forfeit."</p>
<p>"A hundred hours. Professional studio. Engineer."</p>
<p>"I know what it is."</p>
<p>"Do you know what it's worth?"</p>
<p>Jackie's face half-lit, half-shadow, split down the middle.</p>
<p>"What do you want me to say?"</p>
<p>"I want you to get out of the truck."</p>
<p>Stephen came out carrying the kick drum by its lug. Looked at Jackie. Looked at Nick. Big kid who didn't ask questions you didn't invite.</p>
<p>"Stevie. Run us to my house?"</p>
<p>"Yeah. Lemme get the floor tom."</p>
<p>Stephen drove them up Route 20 past Store 24 on Route 217, Ludwig rattling under a moving blanket. Nobody spoke. Dash light made Jackie's cheekbone the yellow of old varnish. At Horse Pond Road the Chevy with the bad belt was out again; Stephen lifted two fingers off the wheel.</p>
<p>Thin Lizzy came on the radio — *The boys are back in town* — and Stephen reached for the knob without looking. The song cut to static. He found WBCN mid-sentence and left it there.</p>
<p>Dutton Road. The Johnson house white paint going gray on the north side, bay window dark, kitchen light on. Stephen put it in park.</p>
<p>"Call me tomorrow."</p>
<p>"Yeah."</p>
<p>Jackie got out first. Nick followed with the pawn-shop guitar case — Dan's money, crack in the upper bout packed with wood glue, handle re-riveted the summer Nick was fifteen. Tail lights took Stephen down Dutton toward his own kitchen and his own night.</p>
<p>Nick put his shoulder to the bay door.</p>
<p>The springs groaned at quarter past eleven the way they had for twenty years — two-tone complaint that belonged to the house. The door rose. The smell hit them both.</p>
<p>Sawdust and motor oil. WD-40 and cold concrete. Dan Johnson's workshop, preserved past the point where anyone had decided to preserve it. Tools on pegboard in Grace's Sharpie outlines — ghost of the tool each outline held. Bare sixty-watt bulb on exposed Romex. April 1975 Aerosmith calendar above the workbench, Joe Perry in a white scarf. On the Maxwell House lid, Dan's 9/16ths socket, set there the week after the funeral and not moved since.</p>
<p>Red toolbox under the calendar. Latch shut. Paint chipped at the corners. Nick did not look at it.</p>
<p>Jackie stopped in the doorway.</p>
<p>He hadn't been here since the summer before eighth grade. The Brubeck summer. Three weeks on *Take Five* because Jackie said five-four was impossible and Nick said impossible was just a word for things that hadn't been done yet. Dan had brought lemonade in Mason jars with too much sugar and sat on the workbench with his boots swinging, listening like a man watching a magic trick he couldn't explain.</p>
<p>"It's the same," Jackie said.</p>
<p>"Yeah." Nick plugged the Marshall on the milk crate. Tubes warmed to a hum under the bulb's own hum a whole tone lower. "Sit down or go home."</p>
<p>Jackie sat. Pulled a folding chair to the middle of the floor. Eyes went — once — to the red toolbox, then away.</p>
<p>"Behind the salt," Nick said.</p>
<p>Leaning against the workbench behind a bag of Morton's road salt Dan never put out: the Wurlitzer 200A Jackie had left spring of '76. Power cord coiled on top. Three keys stuck. Middle C cracked — timbre bent toward a small broken bell. Jackie pressed it twice, listening.</p>
<p>Nick strapped on the pawn-shop guitar. Hit an E. The walls shivered.</p>
<p>"What do you want to play?"</p>
<p>"*Walk This Way.*"</p>
<p>"Fine."</p>
<p>Nick counted off — one, two, three, four — too fast. Anger tempo. Right hand attacked the riff, all downstrokes, Joe Perry's groove replaced by fury. The garage filled with sound. Notes piled like cars in a chain collision.</p>
<p>Jackie came in on the Wurlitzer too controlled — melody flattened clean and mathematical, every note accountable, no room for accident or breath.</p>
<p>They crashed at the chorus. Nick pushed faster. Jackie pulled tighter.</p>
<p>Nick stopped. Last chord rang out and died against the concrete.</p>
<p>"That was garbage."</p>
<p>"Your tempo —"</p>
<p>"My tempo was fine. You played it like sheet music."</p>
<p>"You played it like a bar fight."</p>
<p>Six feet of oil-stained concrete between them. The Marshall hummed.</p>
<p>"*Bohemian Rhapsody,*" Jackie said.</p>
<p>"No."</p>
<p>"You picked first."</p>
<p>Nick exhaled through his teeth. "Fine."</p>
<p>Jackie counted it proper. Opening chords, hands moving without instruction. Too respectfully — afraid of breaking something.</p>
<p>Nick came in on guitar for the operatic section and overplayed. Notes strained and sharp.</p>
<p>Fell apart at the hard-rock section. Nick heavy. Jackie theatrical. Two different bands in one room.</p>
<p>Silence.</p>
<p>"We can't do this," Jackie said.</p>
<p>"No."</p>
<p>"We sound like strangers."</p>
<p>"We *are* strangers."</p>
<p>The Marshall hummed. Headlights swept the small window above the workbench and vanished. Somewhere in the house a faucet ran and stopped.</p>
<p>The side door opened.</p>
<p>Grace stood in the frame, backlit by the kitchen. Friendly's sweatshirt. White Bradlees sneakers with the new insoles. Hair down. Tired past sleep into something structural.</p>
<p>She looked at Nick. At Jackie. At the Wurlitzer plugged in on the folding chair.</p>
<p>"Your father would've liked that," she said. "Both of you winning."</p>
<p>She went back inside. Door clicked shut.</p>
<p>The silence after was different. The silences before had been walls. This one was a window.</p>
<p>Nick sat on the workbench edge, guitar in his lap, heel of his hand muting the strings. Jackie stared at the closed door.</p>
<p>"Don't," Nick said.</p>
<p>"He used to bring us lemonade."</p>
<p>"I said —"</p>
<p>"Mason jars. Too much sugar. Sat right where you're sitting."</p>
<p>Nick's throat closed. The bench was solid — Dan had built it from reclaimed oak, sanded smooth enough to run a palm across. Grain through his jeans.</p>
<p>"Remember the Brubeck?" Jackie said.</p>
<p>Nick's hands loosened. A harmonic escaped — thin, bright, gone.</p>
<p>"Yeah."</p>
<p>"*Take Five.* You said five-four was impossible."</p>
<p>"You said that."</p>
<p>"Did I?"</p>
<p>"You sat right there and said *nobody plays in five-four*, and I said *Brubeck does*, and you said *Brubeck is an alien.*"</p>
<p>Something moved in Jackie's face. Not a smile — too much scar tissue — but the muscles around his eyes loosened, and for a second he was the kid with lemonade who believed music was the only honest language.</p>
<p>"Want to try?" Jackie said.</p>
<p>"You know what."</p>
<p>Nick counted off slower. Not angry. Not controlled. Honest.</p>
<p>Guitar found the *Sweet Emotion* bass line clean — no distortion, notes breathing the way Dan used to say you let wood breathe before you sealed it. Once. Twice. Finding the groove.</p>
<p>Jackie played one chord. D minor. Let it hang. Played it again, added a ninth — door opening into a room locked two years.</p>
<p>Nick's hands responded before his brain could object. Aerosmith groove inside Queen harmony. Two songs breathing the same air.</p>
<p>Jackie added melody — tentative. *Is this the real life* over the *Sweet Emotion* groove. Nick looked once. Jackie's eyes closed, right foot tapping quarter notes on the concrete, loafer heel catching grit. Looked back at the fretboard.</p>
<p>The guitar started to sing the way it only sang when Nick forgot to be angry — hands talking to the instrument instead of fighting it. Fragments of *Walk This Way* crept in as texture, thread in the weave, not a separate song.</p>
<p>Tools vibrated on the pegboard. Screwdriver walked to the edge of its hook. Phillips-head chattered against wood. The 9/16ths socket rolled a quarter-inch on the Maxwell House lid and stopped. Concrete hummed under their feet. Joists overhead picking up a sympathetic drone not in either instrument but somehow in both. The red toolbox did not move. Jackie's eyes went to it once — latch shut, paint chipped — then back to the broken middle C.</p>
<p>Six minutes. No structure. No arrangement. Two musicians remembering what their hands had always known and their lives had made them forget.</p>
<p>When they stopped, the silence rang.</p>
<p>Nick's fingers trembled on the fretboard. Adrenaline. Or something older.</p>
<p>"That's what we could have been," he said.</p>
<p>Jackie looked at him. Two years of silence. Two years of choosing sides and crossing streets. "We could still do it."</p>
<p>The words sat in the garage like a chord nobody resolved.</p>
<p>Nick stared at the pegboard. Jackie stared at the Wurlitzer. Neither one said yes. Neither one said no.</p>
<p>"We forfeit if we don't use it," Jackie said finally. "Both of us. That's the deal."</p>
<p>"Yeah."</p>
<p>Nick went through the side door into the kitchen. Grace was at the table with a glass of water and her checkbook open to a page she wasn't writing on — the way she looked when she was waiting for a sound she couldn't name.</p>
<p>The WBCN card was still in his wallet from March. 104.1 FM. Edges soft where his thumb had worn the cardboard. He unfolded the contract from the Battle and found the station number on the back in Tom Andrews's block print.</p>
<p>He dialed. Jackie stood in the doorway behind him, arms crossed, not coming all the way in.</p>
<p>Three rings.</p>
<p>"WBCN."</p>
<p>"It's Nick Johnson. Jackie Marsh is here." He looked at Jackie. Jackie didn't look away. "We want to use the studio time. The shared hours."</p>
<p>A pause — not long, the pause of a woman who already knew what the contract said.</p>
<p>"Both of you?"</p>
<p>"Both of us."</p>
<p>"Saturday. Four o'clock. Studio C, Boylston Street. You drive yourselves. You walk in together. You forfeit if one of you doesn't show." Another beat. "Don't be late."</p>
<p>She hung up.</p>
<p>Nick set the receiver in the cradle. The kitchen clock said eleven-seventeen.</p>
<p>"Saturday," he said.</p>
<p>Jackie nodded once. "Four o'clock."</p>
<p>Grace closed the checkbook. She didn't ask where Boylston was. She didn't offer to drive. She looked at both of them the way she had looked at the Wurlitzer through the side door — tired, careful, not filling the room with hope it couldn't afford.</p>
<p>"Keys are on the hook," she said to Nick. That was all.</p>
<p>Jackie called home from the wall phone by the refrigerator. Nick heard one side of it — *yes*, *Saturday*, *I'll find a ride* — and then Jackie hung up and stood with his hand still on the receiver like he didn't trust his legs.</p>
<p>"Stephen can drop me Saturday," he said. "I'll meet you there."</p>
<p>"I know where it is," Nick said.</p>
<p>They looked at each other for a second that didn't become anything. Then Jackie went out through the side door into the damp May dark. Footsteps on the drive. A car door — not Grace's wagon, Stephen's truck starting up the road.</p>
<p>Nick pulled the bay door down slow — both hands, easing the springs the last six inches so the counterweight didn't slam, the way Dan had taught him. He did not look at the toolbox on the way out.</p>
<p>Thursday and Friday would be school. Hallways. Forty feet. The question mark still hanging.</p>
<p>Saturday was three days away.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 38: "SWEET EMOTION"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Thursday they passed in the east hallway without speaking. Friday Jackie sat in Dr. Holloway's studio with the Juilliard envelope unopened on the bench and did not play the Battle set. Nick fixed a screen door on Dutton Road for Mrs. Connolly and sanded until his hands went numb.</p>
<p>Saturday was three days after the tie. May nineteenth. Four o'clock.</p>
<p>Nick drove the wagon himself.</p>
<p>Route 20 east through Weston, then the Jamaicaway, then Boylston into the city — forty minutes with the timing off in third and the radio scanning static because WBCN was local and he was almost there. He parked in the lot behind the station with twenty minutes to spare and sat with both hands on the wheel and the pawn-shop guitar in its case on the passenger seat.</p>
<p>Jackie was already on the sidewalk when he climbed out.</p>
<p>Peacoat collar up. Hands in pockets. Breath visible in the May air that wasn't cold but felt cold anyway. Stephen's truck was pulling away from the curb — two fingers off the wheel, gone.</p>
<p>They didn't say hello. They walked in together because the contract said together.</p>
<p>The hallway smelled like tape oxide and old cigarettes. Maxanne stood under the WBCN sign with her arms crossed and her leather jacket zipped to her throat — less radio legend than a woman who had bet something she couldn't afford to lose.</p>
<p>"You showed," she said. "That's the hard part."</p>
<p>She led them down the hall without looking back. Boot heels on linoleum. Nick carried the guitar case. Jackie carried nothing — the piano was already in the room.</p>
<p>Studio C was the size of Grace's living room.</p>
<p>Nick had imagined palatial — walls of equipment, a board the size of a car. Instead: acoustic panels like quilted armor. Two boom mics four feet apart. A window into the control room where a kid in a Red Sox cap sat behind a board that was, in fact, the size of a small car.</p>
<p>"Mike," Maxanne said, knocking the glass. "They're here."</p>
<p>Wire-rims. Enthusiasm like heat off an engine. Thumbs-up. Switches — red, green, amber — the room humming awake.</p>
<p>"Where's the amp?" Nick said.</p>
<p>"DI box." Maxanne pointed at a small metal rectangle by the right mic, cable to the wall. "Plug in. Mike takes the tone off the board."</p>
<p>"Never played through a DI."</p>
<p>"Today you have."</p>
<p>Against the far wall: a Yamaha U3 upright — black lacquer, overheads reflected in long bright streaks. Not the Wurlitzer with its cracked middle C. Jackie lifted the fallboard. Weighted keys. He pressed middle C and the note **bloomed** — clarity that made the garage version sound like it had been played through a blanket.</p>
<p>"Sit." Maxanne pointed Jackie to the bench, Nick to a stool by the DI. "Tune. Mike needs five minutes."</p>
<p>She stepped into the control room. Through the glass Nick watched her lean to Mike's ear. Mike nodded. Hands on the board.</p>
<p>Nick plugged in. Without an amp the guitar sounded thin in the monitors — string without chest — until Mike shaped it, added room, gave it body.</p>
<p>Jackie ran scales. Long strokes. Technique so internalized it had gone invisible. The piano responded like it had been waiting.</p>
<p>Maxanne's voice through the overheads, studio-clear: "One take. No overdubs. No punch-ins. Play what you unlocked in that garage. Mike rolls until you're done or I stop you."</p>
<p>Nick looked at Jackie across four feet of treated air. "Ready?"</p>
<p>Jackie didn't smile. "No."</p>
<p>"Good."</p>
<p>The first thirty seconds were wrong.</p>
<p>Nick came in too fast — Battle anger without the Battle. Jackie answered with sheet music hands. The monitors threw it back naked. Nick stopped.</p>
<p>"Again," he said.</p>
<p>Jackie flexed his fingers. "Again."</p>
<p>Second start: honest tempo. D minor — the chord from the garage, but on the Yamaha it didn't open a door. It knocked down a wall. Overtones spiraled and hung.</p>
<p>Nick's hands found the *Sweet Emotion* bass line — low G, F-sharp, E, D — clean, no distortion, the bones without the muscle. The title groove. Heartbeat under everything.</p>
<p>Jackie's left hand doubled the line an octave up. Four bars in unison — guitar and piano, same phrase, same breath. Then Jackie's right hand drifted upward and the Queen melody entered, not sung, **played** — piano carrying the vocal line the way a river carries a leaf.</p>
<p>The songs merged the way tributaries merge — each losing its name, not its current. Aerosmith groove. Queen architecture. Fragments of *Walk This Way* crept in as texture, Tyler's strut on six strings, answering, trading, the conversation they'd started at thirteen when they didn't know enough to be afraid.</p>
<p>Nick's eyes closed. He didn't need the fretboard. The operatic section arrived — Yamaha bass to treble, thunder to glass — and Nick's guitar sang a counter-melody on one line where Brian May had stacked three.</p>
<p>Once they stumbled — Nick's fingers slipped at the twelfth fret, caught, turned the slip into a bend. Jackie matched with a crushed note on the piano. The crack let the light in. Proof this was live and would never happen exactly this way again.</p>
<p>The hard-rock section hit. Nick dug in. Jackie hammered the keys. Thirty seconds loud enough to rattle the panels, monitors feeding back, building until it crested and broke —</p>
<p>And they didn't stop.</p>
<p>They kept going because Mike's light was still red and neither of them wanted the red to go out. The groove widened. The D minor came back. *Is this the real life* over the descending bass figure. A thread of *Dream On* on the high strings — not the whole song, just the ache in the melody Dan had put into Nick's left hand one Sunday when the garage smelled like Quikrete and hope.</p>
<p>Eleven minutes before Nick's right hand finally lifted and the last harmonic rang out and died against the panels.</p>
<p>Silence.</p>
<p>Two beats.</p>
<p>Jackie played the final chord — D minor, sustain pedal down, note fading like the last light of a day neither of them wanted to end.</p>
<p>Nick's hands shook on the strings.</p>
<p>Through the glass Mike sat with his mouth open. Maxanne's palm flat on the glass. The reel still turning.</p>
<p>Mike's voice on the talkback, young and steady: "Eleven minutes. Still rolling."</p>
<p>Maxanne came in. She didn't rewind. She looked at both of them the way a woman looks who has been in rooms where music happened for thirty years and knows what it costs.</p>
<p>Jackie pressed his fingers to his eyes. When he dropped his hands, his face was wet.</p>
<p>"This is what we could have been," he said.</p>
<p>Nick looked at him. Studio light on the wetness.</p>
<p>"We could still be this," Nick said.</p>
<p>The monitors hummed. A question nobody in the room could answer out loud.</p>
<p>Nick thought about Grace — home on Dutton Road right now, probably in the Friendly's uniform for the Saturday dinner shift, probably not sitting down. The wagon transmission. The mortgage. The Army recruiter's card in his dresser drawer from April, the path that meant a paycheck and a ticket out that wasn't a dream and wasn't a betrayal if you didn't call it one.</p>
<p>He couldn't walk back into that kitchen and tell her he was throwing it over for eleven minutes on a reel in a room that smelled like tape oxide. Couldn't take more than she'd already given.</p>
<p>"I can't," Nick said.</p>
<p>Jackie sat on the bench, hands on his thighs, away from the keys.</p>
<p>His father. Fourteen years of Saturday drives on Route 9. Bach on the tape deck. Juilliard application — three drafts, his father's careful handwriting in the margins. The bankbook in the desk drawer — deposits since Jackie was five, twelve years of numbers in a column that only went one direction.</p>
<p>Dr. Marsh had closed a practice-room lid in 1960 and never opened it for himself. He had poured everything he sacrificed into his son — not the same dream, a translation of it, the safe version of the dangerous thing.</p>
<p>Jackie knew his father was wrong about which version of him was real. He also knew the bridge was real.</p>
<p>He closed his eyes. "Me either."</p>
<p>The words fell like stones into water.</p>
<p>"It's not giving up," Nick said, voice rough. "It's choosing the people who already chose us."</p>
<p>Maxanne turned to the window. Mike watched through the glass with headphones around his neck, the face of a man who knew he was witnessing something he would spend years failing to describe.</p>
<p>Jackie stood. Looked at Nick. Nick looked back.</p>
<p>"If things were different," Jackie said.</p>
<p>"Yeah," Nick said.</p>
<p>Jackie extended his hand. Nick took it — one firm grip, two seconds, released. Not a hug. A hug would have been too much.</p>
<p>Nick went out through the studio door to the parking lot and the wagon with the timing off in third. Jackie went out through the control room and down the hall the other way, toward the street where Stephen had said he'd circle back.</p>
<p>Separate exits. Separate lives waiting the way debts wait — patient, certain, compounding.</p>
<p>Maxanne stood alone in Studio C. Empty chairs. Boom mics angled toward nothing.</p>
<p>Mike pressed the talkback. "What do we do with this?"</p>
<p>He held up the reel — seven inches of magnetic oxide, **eleven minutes**, everything.</p>
<p>Maxanne took it in both hands. Light. Lighter than it should have been for what it carried.</p>
<p>"We preserve it," she said. "Somebody needs to remember this was possible."</p>
<p>She slid the reel into a cardboard sleeve. Wrote on the label in block letters she would still recognize in 2009. Turned off the studio lights.</p>
<p>The Boylston lot at five-thirty held two empty spaces where two boys had parked. Afternoon sun on the brick. WBCN sign buzzing overhead.</p>
<p>She tucked the reel under her arm and walked inside to file what the station would pretend never happened.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 39: "FREE BIRD"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>The town decided the tie meant nobody won.</p>
<p>Friendly's replayed the Battle in fragments — *Did you hear the Marsh kid?* / *The Johnson boy was loud* / *That girl from the farms* — and the register kid on Route 217 rang up coffee while two men argued about whether a tie was a cop-out or the only honest ending. The Sudbury Town Crier ran a photo of the contract signing: two boys on stage, two pens, faces turned slightly away from each other. The caption said *Shared Victory* in a font that made sharing sound like a refund.</p>
<p>Nick and Jackie did not explain Studio C.</p>
<p>They did not explain the garage. They did not explain why they passed in the east hallway with their eyes on the lockers and their shoulders angled toward opposite walls. Three weeks left of senior year felt like three weeks of held breath — teachers handing out review packets, yearbook signatures in the commons, the class of '79 counting down to something nobody could name without lying.</p>
<p>Patty signed yearbooks with *Stay wild* and meant it. She saw Nick once at the bike rack and Jackie once at the west doors and did not try to stand between them. She had sung *Go Your Own Way* on a stage that belonged to her for four minutes. She knew what leaving sounded like before the leaving happened.</p>
<p>Graduation was the second Friday in June.</p>
<p>The field behind Lincoln-Sudbury smelled like cut grass and sunscreen and the faint diesel from the buses idling on Route 117. Folding chairs in rows on the turf. Families in the bleachers with cameras and folding fans. The sky was the color of a clean channel — no static, no promise.</p>
<p>Grace Johnson sat mid-bleachers in a dress she'd bought at Filene's Basement in April — not the navy Friendly's dress from the Battle, never that dress again. She held a tissue she didn't use. When Nick's name was called she stood before the rest of the row did, one sharp motion, and sat back down when he crossed the stage.</p>
<p>Dr. Richard Marsh sat three sections over with Eileen and Elizabeth. His suit was gray. His hands were still. When Jackie took the diploma he did not stand — a restraint that cost him something visible only in the set of his jaw.</p>
<p>Nick and Jackie crossed the same stage twelve names apart.</p>
<p>Nick in a borrowed sport coat from Stephen's father, tie loose, mortarboard straight. Jackie in what his mother called appropriate — blazer, crease, the uniform of a life already packed in boxes marked JUILLIARD.</p>
<p>They did not shake hands in the line. They did not look at each other on the grass after. Forty feet of parents and balloons and other people's joy between them — the same forty feet they'd been carrying since November, since the funeral, since before the funeral if you counted honestly.</p>
<p>Patty stood at the edge of the parking lot with her Martin in its case and her boots unlaced. She watched Nick load his gown into the wagon's back seat. She watched Jackie's mother steer him toward the Volvo. She hummed nothing. The pig-farm quarter air moved through the pines toward the dump and carried the smell of turned earth and something sweeter underneath — cut grass, exhaust, the end of something.</p>
<p>From a Trans Am pulling out of the lot, two bars of Lynyrd Skynyrd climbed through an open window before the driver rolled it up against the wind.</p>
<p>*If I leave here tomorrow…*</p>
<p>Then the parking lot swallowed it.</p>
<p>Nick cashed the two hundred and fifty dollars the Monday after graduation.</p>
<p>Grace protested at the kitchen table — Maxwell House between them, the check flat on the Formica. *That's your money. Your prize.*</p>
<p>He left it beside the salt shaker. "Transmission."</p>
<p>She cashed it without ceremony. The mechanic on Route 20 replaced the belt and told her the timing would need work by fall. She wrote the estimate on the back of a deposit slip and stuck it to the refrigerator with a magnet shaped like a lobster.</p>
<p>Nick mowed three lawns on Dutton Road. Fixed Mrs. Connolly's screen door without being asked. Slept with the pawn-shop guitar on the floor beside his bed — crack in the upper bout, handle re-riveted, Dan's purchase still holding.</p>
<p>In July he signed the papers the recruiter had left in an envelope thick enough to mean business. Three years. Education benefit. Fort Benning in September. Grace read the signature line twice and poured more coffee. She did not say *don't*. She did not say *your father*. She said, "Eat something before you go," which was the same sentence she'd used the morning of the Battle and the morning of Studio C and every morning that mattered without announcing that it mattered.</p>
<p>The last night in Sudbury he opened the garage bay door and played unplugged with the door up and the stars over the conservation pines. No Marshall. No audience. The notes barely left the strings — structure only, the way blueprints hold a house before the walls go up. He played until his fingertips hurt and the hurt felt like proof and then he closed the door and latched it and did not look at the red toolbox under the calendar.</p>
<p>Jackie left for New York the third week of August.</p>
<p>Two suitcases. A garment bag for concert black. His mother cried at the door with her hand over her mouth — quiet, disciplined grief. Dr. Marsh drove him to Logan with the radio off and both hands at ten and two.</p>
<p>The Juilliard dorm room was nine feet by eleven. Jackie measured it with his shoes heel to toe because measuring was what you did when the world gave you a box and asked you to become yourself inside it. Twin bed. Desk. Dresser. Window on West 66th where taxis ran yellow all night and the sound never stopped.</p>
<p>His bedroom in Sudbury had been fourteen by sixteen with an oak tree outside the window his mother had planted the year he was born.</p>
<p>The practice-room Steinway on the fourth floor was better. Seven-foot Model B. The keys responded to intention the way a blade responds to pressure — immediately, without forgiveness.</p>
<p>He practiced six hours a day. Chopin etudes. Bach inventions. Debussy preludes. His left hand was called exemplary. His sight-reading was called exceptional.</p>
<p>Paul Nakamura from Portland shared the suite — cello case by the door, turntable in the corner, Monk and Miles in rotation.</p>
<p>"What kind of music do you listen to?" Paul asked their second night.</p>
<p>"Classical," Jackie said.</p>
<p>He did not mention Queen. He did not mention the Crown Jewels or the tie or the Wurlitzer with the cracked middle C or the garage on Dutton Road where lemonade had come in Mason jars. He said *classical* the way you close a door — firmly, with your back against it.</p>
<p>September — Fort Benning, Georgia.</p>
<p>The barracks smelled like floor wax and sweat and Pine-Sol, and underneath all of it, red clay. Georgia clay got into boot seams and nostrils and the spaces between teeth. By the second week Nick had stopped tasting it. By the fourth week he'd stopped noticing most things.</p>
<p>The body adapted. Run. March. Crawl. Repeat. His legs learned distances his brain refused to calculate. His hands learned the M16 the way they'd learned the pawn-shop guitar — repetition so thorough it became reflex.</p>
<p>The guitar lived in his footlocker. Bedsheet wrapped around the body because the latches were loose and the lock was broken and the instrument was the one thing he owned that could not be replaced at a surplus store. Combination set to his mother's birthday. Thirty-eight men in the barracks. He trusted none of them with this.</p>
<p>After lights out, when the breathing settled into the rhythm of collective sleep, Nick opened the footlocker. He didn't plug in. No amp. No room for an amp. He played so quiet the notes existed only between his fingers and the wire — audible to himself and maybe to Specialist Haywood two bunks down, who listened a month before he spoke.</p>
<p>"You any good?" Haywood whispered one night.</p>
<p>"I was."</p>
<p>"What happened?"</p>
<p>Nick fretted a D minor chord soft as paper tearing. "Joined the Army."</p>
<p>Grace wrote every week. Envelopes from Woolworth's — white, standard, handwriting tight as inventory forms. She wrote about Friendly's. The new assistant manager and the register. The wagon holding. Leaves turning on Dutton Road. Mrs. Abernathy's dog and the Hendersons' tomatoes.</p>
<p>She never asked about the guitar. She never asked if he was happy. She asked if he was eating and if he'd gotten the oatmeal cookies and if he needed socks.</p>
<p>Nick wrote back on government lined paper. Obstacle course. Rifle range. Ten-mile march. Blister the size of a quarter. Haywood from Detroit who sang Motown in the shower with a voice that belonged somewhere else.</p>
<p>He did not mention the studio. He did not mention Jackie Marsh. He did not mention the nights he lay awake after playing and heard eleven minutes of music in a room the size of his mother's living room — music that worked, music that wasn't enough, music that lived somewhere now without his name on the door.</p>
<p>The bargain was the bargain. Three years for tuition. Room. Board. A shot at becoming something Dan had never gotten the shot to become. Dan had traded his body for a mortgage and a garage full of tools and a red box he never opened again.</p>
<p>The bargain killed him.</p>
<p>Nick closed the footlocker. Set the combination. Stared at the ceiling until staring became sleep.</p>
<p>November. Jackie took the train to Boston.</p>
<p>Thanksgiving break. WBCN's hallway looked smaller in daylight — scuffed linoleum, coffee stains the color of old pennies. Maxanne was in Studio B setting up for the evening show, leather jacket over the back of her chair, cassette on the console with the leader pulled out a quarter inch.</p>
<p>"I wanted to say goodbye," Jackie said.</p>
<p>"You came from New York to say goodbye?"</p>
<p>"I was in town."</p>
<p>"How's Juilliard?"</p>
<p>"Fine. Different." He paused. "We don't talk."</p>
<p>Maxanne's eyes narrowed. "We?"</p>
<p>"Nick and me. Different worlds."</p>
<p>Silence that tasted like tape oxide.</p>
<p>"You heard from him?" she asked.</p>
<p>"No."</p>
<p>She looked at him a long moment. Looked at the cassette. Looked back.</p>
<p>"The music you made together—"</p>
<p>"Was a long time ago," Jackie said.</p>
<p>Eight months since the tie. He said it the way people say *a long time ago* when they mean *a distance I've put between myself and something I can't afford to look at directly*.</p>
<p>His eyes moved to the metal shelving behind the console — seven-inch reels spine-out, dates in black marker. He looked a second longer than a glance. Looked away.</p>
<p>"I've got a train to catch," he said.</p>
<p>He rode back to New York. Practiced Debussy. Did not play anything in D minor. Slept in nine by eleven and dreamed in dimensions he couldn't measure.</p>
<p>Sudbury kept going.</p>
<p>The Battle poster came down in Wentworth's music room and was replaced by the fall concert schedule. Hop Brook ran clear through October. Store 24 on Route 217 sold cigarettes and bait and did not stock a single record with both Aerosmith and Queen on the label.</p>
<p>Grace worked doubles. Dr. Marsh drove to Boston for department meetings. Patty rode the blue Schwinn on the conservation trails and sang to the pines when nobody was listening.</p>
<p>Nick and Jackie did not share a stage again.</p>
<p>They did not share a garage. They did not share a studio. They shared a town until one of them left and then they shared a silence that covered everything — the tie, the contract, the eleven minutes Maxanne labeled in block letters and filed where boys who chose their people would never think to look.</p>
<p>If you leave here tomorrow, the song had asked from a Trans Am in a graduation parking lot.</p>
<p>They left.</p>
<p>Not together. Not on the same road. Not with the thing they'd built still burning in the same room.</p>
<p>The town called it a tie and moved on. The boys called it choosing and did not call it anything at all where anyone could hear.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 40: "COMFORTABLY NUMB"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Fifty-one minutes.</p>
<p>Kevin runs the block — a Cars deep cut, low in the mix, something safe for the laptop to chew while I leave the chair. The ON AIR sign stays red. I'm still live; the mic is hot in the room even when I'm not leaning on it. My voice has been going four hours. It doesn't feel tired. It feels **used**, the way a good pair of headphones feels used — leather cracked where your head lives, foam gone soft, still the only thing that fits.</p>
<p>"I need five," I tell him through the glass.</p>
<p>He nods without looking up. Twenty-five. Bruins hoodie. Phone face-down for once. Progress.</p>
<p>I take my purse and I walk.</p>
<p>The hallway outside Studio B is stacked with cardboard — institutional death in tidy stacks. STUDIO A — EQUIPMENT. ARCHIVES 1971-1980. PROMO — UNSORTED. AWARDS / PLAQUES. The Gold Records are gone. They left clean rectangles on the wall where forty years of nicotine couldn't reach, ghosts the exact shape of things that used to matter.</p>
<p>My heels click. I haven't worn heels in the booth since 1996. Tonight I wore them on purpose. You don't go barefoot when you're walking through your own wake.</p>
<p>The building hum is **wrong**.</p>
<p>For forty-one years WBCN had a heartbeat you could lean your palm on — electricity through the board, signal up the tower, the floor vibrating like a bass note you felt in your teeth. I pressed my hand to this plaster in '76 during a Springsteen broadcast and the wall **moved**. I felt it in '73 when Steven Tyler heard his own voice on the radio for the first time — the look on his face in the hallway afterward, like a man who'd just learned he was real.</p>
<p>Tonight the hum is thin. Mechanical. Bleeding through the Promotions door down the hall — Christian contemporary test tones, a synthesized choir finding pitch in an empty room, the new tenant rehearsing salvation five hours early. K-LOVE moving in before the body is cold. The frequency learning a language it was never built to speak.</p>
<p>I stop. Listen. My skin prickles — not grief yet, **revulsion**. Alive revulsion. The animal part of you that knows when the air in a room has turned.</p>
<p>I keep walking.</p>
<p>Reception is stripped to laminate and dust.</p>
<p>The desk where some kid buzzed visitors for decades — promo records, chewed pen on a string, calls from artists who thought they were both — is a shell. Computer gone. Phone gone. A box labeled RECEPTION — MISC holds a tangle of cords and a chipped mug: WBCN — THE ROCK OF BOSTON.</p>
<p>I pick it up. Ceramic cold against my thumb. I put it back. The cold stays on my skin.</p>
<p>The station manager's office door is open. Lights on. Nobody home.</p>
<p>I shouldn't go in. Thirty years of not doing what I should have done makes small crimes feel like balance.</p>
<p>The desk holds three items. Telephone. Lamp. Document in clear plastic.</p>
<p>ASSET PURCHASE AGREEMENT</p>
<p>Between: WBCN Radio, Inc. (Seller)</p>
<p>And: Educational Media Foundation (Buyer)</p>
<p>The Globe said it. The Herald said it. The trades covered it like a terminal prognosis. Effective August twelfth, 2009, 12:01 AM, 104.1 becomes automated praise music out of North Carolina.</p>
<p>Below the header, blue ballpoint — somebody's human moment:</p>
<p>*41 years. Thank you.*</p>
<p>Four words. I stand there until my calves ache. The phone rings — once, twice, five times — and nobody answers because nobody works here anymore except ghosts and a kid in a hoodie who thinks rock history started with Nirvana.</p>
<p>I leave the lamp on. I leave the document where it is.</p>
<p>ARCHIVES 1971-1980.</p>
<p>I cut the box open with my keys. Tape smell hits first — oxide and cardboard and the particular dust of magnetic memory. Promo singles. Cart labels. A stack of seven-inch reels spine-out, dates in black marker running down the edges.</p>
<p>My handwriting.</p>
<p>May 1979.</p>
<p>I pull the sleeve. Cardboard soft at the corners from thirty years of hands that weren't mine checking inventory, moving boxes, not knowing what they held. The label in block letters I wrote in Studio C at five-thirty on a Saturday afternoon while two boys drove home on separate roads:</p>
<p>*Nick/Jackie — un-Authorized — May 1979*</p>
<p>Eleven minutes. Mike kept rolling. D minor and *Sweet Emotion* and a stumble turned gold.</p>
<p>The reel weighs almost nothing. Lighter than the mug. Lighter than the document. Heavier than every Gold Record they took off the walls.</p>
<p>I tuck it under my arm.</p>
<p>Back in Studio B the Cars track is ending. Kevin fades it. Through the glass he watches me return — reel in one hand, purse in the other — the way you watch someone carry something that might be explosive.</p>
<p>I sit. The broadcast chair creaks the same way it creaked in 1974. I put the reel on the console to my left.</p>
<p>From my purse I take the cassette.</p>
<p>I've carried it Brookline to New York to LA to Nashville and back. Warm from body heat. Label gone the color of weak tea — *Demo — Nick/Jackie — un-Authorized — May 1979* — my block letters blurred but legible. Hairline crack along one edge I Scotch-taped in '93 and check every few months the way you check a pulse that hasn't changed.</p>
<p>It weighs like bird bones.</p>
<p>I set it on the console to the right of the Wheatstone, four inches from the cart machine. Not a plan. A arrangement. Two objects on a mantel — the way Grace set Dan's watch beside his wedding ring the first night, the way you lay out proof before you decide what you're willing to lose.</p>
<p>The cart machine holds one cartridge. Masking tape. Sharpie in somebody else's tidy hand:</p>
<p>SHINE ON YOU CRAZY DIAMOND — PINK FLOYD — FINAL TRACK — DO NOT REMOVE</p>
<p>Corporate's picked ending. Twenty-six minutes of elegy committee. Gilmour reaching for a man who wasn't coming back. The right song for a rock station dying — accidentally right, the way a stopped clock is right twice a day.</p>
<p>I pick up the cart. Warm from standby heat. I smell the plastic and for one second I'm twenty-nine in September 1975, shrink wrap on a new LP, needle down, six phone calls during the fade and one of them a man crying who couldn't say why.</p>
<p>I set the cart down. Gentle. Respect isn't numbness. Respect is knowing what you're holding.</p>
<p>"You want coffee?" Kevin asks on the talkback.</p>
<p>"I want bourbon."</p>
<p>Pause. "I've got Red Bull."</p>
<p>"That'll have to do."</p>
<p>He retreats. Through the glass his phone stays face-down. Born in '84. Never felt a city lean in when a needle dropped on something nobody had heard yet. Never felt the booth when the board was **alive**.</p>
<p>I pour Heaven Hill into a paper cup from the second drawer — two fingers, thumb measure. One pour. The budget's almost spent.</p>
<p>I lift the cup toward the cart. Toward the reel. Toward the cassette. Not a toast speech. A breath.</p>
<p>I drink.</p>
<p>You've heard the judges' room. You heard Studio C — Saturday, May nineteenth, four in the afternoon, Yamaha middle C blooming, eleven minutes Mike didn't stop. You heard the garage and the tie and the forty feet of wet pavement. I won't gloss it again. Guilt doesn't need another coat of paint.</p>
<p>Nick is in Sudbury. Alive. Last I heard — construction, the body trade his father made, the bargain that killed his father and saved his mother. Jackie is in New York. Alive. Juilliard whole-looking, which is its own kind of gone. I won't pretend otherwise tonight.</p>
<p>The Pink Floyd cart waits. The corporate ending. Comfortable. Numb. The easy exit.</p>
<p>The reel waits. The cassette waits. Eleven minutes of unauthorized proof that two kids could still merge when the world had finished sorting them.</p>
<p>Forty-six minutes until I have to choose which truth goes out on a frequency that's already half a ghost.</p>
<p>The ON AIR sign holds red.</p>
<p>Outside the booth windows, Boylston shimmers — August heat, glass towers where dive bars used to breed bands, luxury condos where The Rat used to make your grandmother blush. The antenna on the roof points at something nobody wants to look at.</p>
<p>I put the MDR-7506s around my neck — electrical tape on the headband, three black rings where it cracked in '98, the only equipment in this room older than my regrets.</p>
<p>My knuckles crack. One hand. The other.</p>
<p>Down the hall the synthesized choir swells — hopeful, ascending, praise without sweat. The old hum is gone. My pulse isn't.</p>
<p>I lean toward the pop filter.</p>
<p>The board glows amber. Little lights doing their small electrical business. Waiting for a hand that still knows what music is for.</p>
<p>I'm not numb. I'm **electric**.</p>
<p>Final hour. Let's go.</p>
</div>
</div>
<div class="chapter-break">
<p class="chapter-heading">Chapter 41: "WALK THIS WAY"</p>
<div class="prose">
<p>Final hour.</p>
<p>The Pink Floyd cart sits in the machine like a corporate tombstone — twenty-six minutes of accidental elegy somebody in North Carolina picked from a brochure. I leave it where it is. Shine On You Crazy Diamond can guard a suite that never airs.</p>
<p>On the console, the cassette.</p>
<p>*Demo — Nick/Jackie — un-Authorized — May 1979.*</p>
<p>The monitor beside the request line blinks amber. Voicemail — one message flagged PRIORITY from a listener in Somerville, logged at ten-forty-two. Kevin played it for me before the Cars block. I don't play it again. I don't need to.</p>
<p>A man's voice, older, cigarette-rough: *The Neighborhoods opened for the Cars at the Rat in seventy-nine. These kids never even made the card. If you're going out tonight, play something that matters.*</p>
<p>I almost laughed when I heard it. Not because it was wrong. Because it was the whole town in one sentence.</p>
<p>I lean into the pop filter.</p>
<p>"Corporate left me a cart. I'm not going to play it."</p>
<p>Through the glass Kevin's eyebrows go up. He doesn't stop me.</p>
<p>"I'm going to play a dub I shouldn't have. May nineteenth, nineteen seventy-nine. Studio C. Two boys from Sudbury who tied a Battle of the Bands and chose their people and recorded eleven minutes anyway. Unauthorized. Never sold. Never released. The best band this town ever had — and they were never a band. Not once. That's the story you've been listening to for four hours. This is the only proof."</p>
<p>I pick up the cassette. The plastic shell clicks into the deck — old habit, muscle memory from the Brickyard days when conviction went on a Maxell and into the world before anyone asked.</p>
<p>"I've carried this thirty years. I'm playing it now. Full volume. The engineers used to hate me for this. The listeners never did."</p>
<p>I punch the fader up.</p>
<p>The VU meters slam red.</p>
<p>D minor — Jackie's Yamaha, middle C blooming through Studio C walls, through the Wheatstone, through fifty thousand watts for the last time tonight. The chord hangs in the booth like a held breath the building has been waiting for since nineteen sixty-eight.</p>
<p>Then Nick's guitar — *Sweet Emotion* stripped to its bones, clean, no amp hiding anything. Low G, F-sharp, E, D. Heartbeat under everything.</p>
<p>I don't narrate. I listen.</p>
<p>Jackie's left hand doubles the line an octave up. Four bars in unison — guitar and piano, same phrase, same breath. Queen melody rising out of the piano like a leaf on a river Nick's strut answers on six strings. Fragments of *Walk This Way* creep in as texture — not the single, not the record, the **conversation** they started at thirteen when they didn't know enough to be afraid.</p>
<p>The operatic section — Yamaha bass to treble, thunder to glass. Nick's counter-melody where Brian May stacked three. A stumble at the twelfth fret turned into a bend. Jackie's crushed note matching. The crack that lets the light in.</p>
<p>Hard rock — thirty seconds of controlled fury, tenderness with nowhere else to go. They don't stop. Mike's red light still burning in my memory. The groove widens. D minor again. *Is this the real life* over the descending bass. A thread of *Dream On* on the high strings — not the whole song, just the ache Dan put in Nick's left hand one Sunday when the garage smelled like Quikrete and hope.</p>
<p>Eleven minutes.</p>
<p>Nick's right hand lifts. A last harmonic rings and dies.</p>
<p>Two beats.</p>
<p>Jackie's final chord — D minor, sustain down, note fading like the last light of a day neither of them wanted to end.</p>
<p>The cassette spins into leader. The meters fall.</p>
<p>The booth is quiet in a way no booth in this building has ever been quiet.</p>
<p>I lean toward the mic.</p>
<p>"This is Maxanne Sartori." A beat. "WBCN. The Rock of Boston."</p>
<p>Another beat.</p>
<p>"Goodnight."</p>
<p>The clock reads eleven fifty-nine.</p>
<p>The ON AIR sign dies at midnight. Not a second off. Forty-one years of red light — and then no red light. The booth drops to emergency fluorescent, the color of old newsprint, and the absence of the red is louder than anything I ever played through this microphone.</p>
<p>Transmitter silence. Not quiet. **Empty.** The space where something used to be.</p>
<p>Twelve-oh-one.</p>
<p>Static. Then smooth.</p>
<p>A man's voice mixed too hot, processed through reverb, the audio equivalent of a smile with nothing behind it.</p>
<p>*You're listening to JOY FM, bringing you the good news in music and ministry.*</p>
<p>The synthesized choir from down the hall is no longer rehearsal — it's **on the dial**. Praise without sweat. The frequency learning a language it was never built to speak.</p>
<p>I eject the cassette. The shell is warm from the deck. I slide it into my purse where it has lived through Brookline and New York and LA and every year I told myself not tonight.</p>
<p>Kevin opens the booth door. He stands in it. He does not speak. I understand we are not going to speak.</p>
<p>The hallway's moving boxes have not moved. The lobby is darker than when I came in. The chipped WBCN mug still sits in RECEPTION — MISC, cold ceramic, nobody coming back for it.</p>
<p>Outside, August in Boston. Asphalt and harbor heat. The antenna tower dark against the sky for the first time in forty-one years.</p>
<p>My Volvo is the only car in the lot. Twelve years old. I get in and close the door.</p>
<p>I leave the radio off.</p>
<p>The engine catches on the second try. I pull out onto the Pike heading west — toward Cambridge, toward home — and the silence wraps around me the way Hop Brook wraps around a stone.</p>
<p>I could put Nick somewhere too — a footlocker, a sergeant who looked away, strings that barely leave the wire. Ch 39 already did that work. The reader has his body in a barracks. I don't need to invent him again.</p>
<p>The piano is the part I can't stop hearing.</p>
<p>Somewhere in a practice room — Mass Ave, or West Sixty-Sixth, or a room I have never stood in — a man in his late forties sits at a Steinway with the lid open and his hands resting on his thighs. Not playing. Has not played seriously for himself in years. Plays for students now — voice leading, a chord to make a point, lid down at the hour. Back straight. Feet flat under the pedals. The shape his body settled into one November afternoon on Boylston Street when he stood in a doorway and looked at a wall of reels and said he had a train to catch.</p>
<p>He did not hear what I played tonight. Nobody told him. The broadcast is already gone — sold to a choir in North Carolina. He is alone with a piano that does not forgive hesitation.</p>
<p>His right hand lifts from his thigh. It does not lift because he decides to lift it. It lifts the way a hand lifts from a table when someone calls you from the next room and you are already rising before you know why. It moves over the keys and comes down on the D above middle C — finger on the ivory, key not moving, string not sounding. A beat. Two beats. His left hand comes up. The weight comes down.</p>
<p>D minor. Once. Just the chord.</p>
<p>He nods — very slightly — the way people nod when they finally hear somebody say out loud the thing they had decided they were alone with.</p>
<p>I don't know if this is Jackie Marsh. I don't know where he is tonight. I'm making him up in a Volvo on the Mass Pike at one-twenty in the morning because I need to. He might be out there. He might not. The imagining is what I have.</p>
<p>I drive west. The radio stays off.</p>
<p>— END —</p>
</div>
</div>
